# Research log — Dashboard Design

*One record of the journey: what we set out to find, what we found, what we learned, what we left out and why, the threads we pulled, the questions we left open, and how each chapter looks back at the ones before it. Appended, never overwritten. Started 5 October 2026; version 1's research (August) is summarised in the first entry so the record is whole.*

**How to read an entry.** Each chapter sprint gets one entry with the same sections: *Framed* (written before research began) · *Threads pulled* (planned and unplanned) · *Sources read* (how read, what it establishes, strength, used or not and why) · *Learned* · *Left out, and why* · *Open questions* · *Looking back* (per earlier chapter: strengthens / weakens / counterbalances / opens a thread) · *Flags* · *Rich references for Carly*. Flags use the vocabulary in `AGENTS.md`.

---

## Entry 0 · Version 1, 16–17 August 2026 — the record of what was done before this log existed

**Framed.** Version 1 asked fourteen design decisions in sequence, from the default view to breaking rules well, under a brief that said "research the methodology for building the Vibe Map as an interactive dashboard; never research nightlife".

**What was done.** Chapters 1–8 were researched live with sources verified at URL on 16 August and revised on Carly's comments. Chapters 9–14 were written in one overnight run under a no-new-research rule, from material already gathered. A sources ledger (`SOURCES.md`) and per-chapter working notes (`02-research-notes/`) were kept. A display audit of ten live instruments and an evidence sweep on first-screen research are the two largest primary pieces.

**Learned, and carried forward.** The evidence that holds: defaults are read as advice (Jachimowicz et al., meta-analysis); the first view anchors exploration (Cho et al.); words carry the message (Borkin et al.); appeal is judged in 50 ms on simplicity and prototypicality (Lindgaard; Tuch); "overview first" has never been validated (Craft & Cairns); stories do not beat exploration (Boy, Détienne & Fekete); quantile dotplots for uncertainty; rankings are reactive (Espeland & Sauder); recommendation explanations raise trust (Herlocker); platform death (Every Noise at Once). The house findings: the 78%/32% aggregation problem; identity never ranked, conditions gated; the crop test; co-production of vibe; the heartbeat.

**Left out or found wanting, and why it matters now.** The book reported rather than taught; it used insider vocabulary; it did not say what kind of claim each claim was; Chapters 9–14 were never verified; the gallery's "nobody has built this" claim outran its evidence; Chapter 13 made creative decisions that are Carly's; the analytical and statistical foundations — how value is found and measured in data, how other sectors build and read evidence — were barely touched. Carly's reading on 5 October set version 2's brief.

**Flags carried into version 2.** `[Further research]` the defaults meta-analysis has never been run for dashboards · `[Prototype flag]` control-usage analytics on the Vibe Map itself · `[Validate with users]` the five-second test with the three user groups · `[Revisit at end]` the suppression threshold as a collection-design problem (GAP-13) · `[Revisit at end]` hostile contributors and gaming · `[Rich reference]` Craft & Cairns 2005 on the mantra; Jachimowicz et al. 2019 on defaults.

## Entry 1 · Chapter 1 — What a dashboard is for

*Sprint opened 5 October 2026, evening.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What does a dashboard do, why does it matter, what is risked when it is done poorly, what is gained when it is done well — and how is any of that proved?

**Sub-questions.**
1. What *is* a dashboard — where the word and the form come from, how the field defines and classifies it, and what separates it from a report, a data story or an exploratory tool.
2. By what mechanisms does a display reach a decision: monitoring, situational awareness, comparison, accountability, persuasion.
3. Does it work? What evidence exists that dashboards change decisions or outcomes — in healthcare, education, government performance management, business intelligence, and the public dashboards of 2020 — and what evidence of non-use, misuse and harm.
4. Why they fail: Carly's failure register (looks the same every month · a fancy spreadsheet of big numbers · "what am I looking at" · people do not recognise their place · nobody uses it · it breaks · nobody trusts it · an aggregation of the biggest) tested against the literature — does each failure have a named mechanism and a named remedy?
5. What makes one trusted: the UK's own framework for trustworthy statistics; the scholarship on trust in numbers; provenance and transparency.
6. The aggregation problem as a statistical fact, not an opinion: the modifiable areal unit problem, the ecological fallacy, Simpson's paradox, and the treatment of outliers as signal.
7. The critical view: dashboards as instruments of governing, what they make legible and what they erase.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Few; Eckerson; Sarikaya et al. 2019; Bach et al. 2022; Yigitbasioglu & Velcu 2012; Endsley on situational awareness; the clinical-dashboard and learning-analytics reviews; Matheus, Janssen & Maheshwari on public dashboards; Ivanković et al. 2021 and Dong, Du & Gardner 2020 on the 2020 dashboards; the UK Statistics Authority Code of Practice (trustworthiness, quality, value); O'Neill on trust; Porter, *Trust in Numbers*; Scott, *Seeing Like a State*; Kitchin, Lauriault & McArdle 2015; Mattern, *Mission Control*; Espeland & Stevens; Muller; Strathern/Goodhart/Campbell; Openshaw on MAUP; Robinson on the ecological fallacy; Star & Griesemer on boundary objects.

**What I expect to find, so the log can show where I was wrong.** That the field's definitions are loose and mostly practitioner-made; that evidence of decision impact is thin outside healthcare and mixed there; that the 2020 dashboards are the richest recent case on trust; that the aggregation problem has a precise statistical literature the dashboard field rarely cites; and that the critical scholarship on urban dashboards is where Carly's "price of everything, value of nothing" has already been argued academically.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) the dashboard literature proper; (B) does it work — impact, adoption, trust, the 2020 dashboards; (C) the critical and theoretical scholarship; (D) the statistics of aggregation and outliers.

### Threads pulled

*Planned:* all seven sub-questions were researched. *Unplanned, and kept:*
- **Dashboards as boundary objects** (Star & Griesemer 1989 → Turnhout 2009 → van Rij 2026, unread). Became the most constructive idea in the chapter and the theoretical reason co-design precedes screens. Opens a thread for Chapter 12 and the four discovery tests.
- **Merry's *data inertia* and *expertise inertia*** — not on the list; found by the critical-scholarship agent; explains more economically than anything else why dashboards show what already exists as data.
- **Shrinkage has an opposite bias** (Gelman & Price 1999). I expected smoothing to be the fix for small-area volatility; it moves the extremes to the big places instead. Changed the rule from "smooth small areas" to "show the uncertainty, never a point".
- **Robinson re-read** (Subramanian et al. 2009): the paper cited for sixty years as "never trust the area" contains the evidence that the area had a real effect. Turned a caution into a positive truth — a place-level figure can be information about the place.
- **The attribution chain of Goodhart's law**: the famous sentence is Strathern's paraphrase; she credits Hoskin; Campbell said it first. The chapter cites Strathern for the wording.
- **Dashboards as an obstacle to data** (Dong et al. 2022 scraping BI front-ends) → the UK API as the answer → the rule "a dashboard that is not also a data release is a wall".
- **Trust attaches to the producer, not the presenter** — the 85%/32% pattern in the UK confidence surveys, unchanged for a decade.
- **The only manager survey contradicts Few**: dashboards are used to communicate and align, not to monitor.
- **Positive deviance** as a named method for Carly's "outlier that is actually working" — found by the statistics agent; adopted as an Adaptable rule and a prototype flag.
- **Few himself cites Endsley** — so the situation-awareness gap (Level 1 shown, Levels 2–3 needed) is the field's own standard, not one imposed on it.

### Sources read

Full agent returns, with every URL, quotation, strength and the agents' own "left out" and "surprised" notes, are kept whole at `02-research-notes/ch01-agent-returns.md`. The chapter's Sources section lists every source used, grouped, with how it was read. Orchestrator spot-checks at source (6): Few 2004 definition; UKHSA 90.3%/73.4%, Downing Street, 76.5m; Wainer's four quotations; Kitchin et al. "engine" and conclusion; Strathern p. 308; Ivanković 12.7% and seven features. All held. An independent verification agent then re-traced twenty load-bearing items (report summarised below).

### What I learned

- The field's definition problem is real and twenty years old; the honest move is to define by job (data → decision → named reader), not shape.
- Evidence of impact is thin in every sector and mostly weaker designs reporting benefit; the robust findings are about *use* and *reactivity*, not about charts.
- The 2020 dashboards are the richest trust case, and the trust came from conduct — fixed time, simultaneous release, open data, close-to-home, relentless surveying — not visualisation.
- The aggregation problem has a precise statistical literature (Openshaw, Robinson, Simpson, Wainer, Gelman & Price, Goldstein & Spiegelhalter, the OECD/JRC handbook) that the dashboard literature almost never cites. Carly's grievance is mathematics.
- Official statistics suppress small cells by design; a dashboard built on them is blind where small scenes live. This is the statistical root of the suppression-threshold problem carried from version 1.
- The critical scholarship (Kitchin, Scott, Porter, Espeland & Stevens, Merry, D'Ignazio & Klein, the Cultural Value Project) is where "the price of everything and the value of nothing" has already been argued academically — and it also supplies the constructive ideas: document situatedness and uncertainty; counterdata; the boundary object.

### What I left out, and why

- Vendor adoption statistics (Gartner "20%", "BARC 15%") — untraceable or self-interested; the chapter makes their absence a rule.
- Buttigieg et al. 2017 hospital-dashboard review — unreadable from any host; not cited.
- Endsley's critics (Flach; Dekker & Hollnagel) beyond a mention — the construct debate is real but not load-bearing here.
- Few's full thirteen pitfalls — display-level advice; belongs in Chapter 3 if anywhere.
- Mattern's Geddes Outlook Tower — lovely counter-model; parked for Chapter 15 (`[Further research]`).
- The EIS/CSF lineage beyond two sentences — colour.
- Kahneman's retelling of Wainer — not verified at source.
- The Gaming typology in Bevan & Hood — abstract only; moved to a flag.
- Anything about nightlife — excluded by the brief.

### Open questions

Listed in the chapter under "Open questions and flags". The ones that will shape later chapters: staleness (Ch 8); gaming typologies (Ch 7/10); boundary objects applied to dashboards (Ch 12); Porter's mechanical objectivity (Ch 5); the abstract-only sources behind Fixed rules (verification before close).

### Looking back

First chapter of version 2; the backward look is at the archive. Recorded in the chapter: strengthens v1's endorsement and freshness rules; counterbalances v1 Ch 5's comparability gate (needs intervals, not only a note); opens a thread on who speaks for a scene (v1 Ch 7).

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Cold reader (no project context):** could state the decision and name a hard line and an open choice; found section 6 the best teaching. Objections taken: (1) Fixed rules drawn from abstract-only sources without saying so — added the † convention and a flag; (2) "three findings across four fields" overclaimed — reworded: two hold across fields, the third is shown in one and echoed; (3) "quoting a vendor" was polemical — removed; (4) literature-report cadence in section 3 — authors now named in prose, one naming system throughout; (5) aphorism stacking — several closers cut; (6) project-internal terms unexplained — a "Before you start" note added, the three user groups and four discovery tests listed, heartbeat and six kinds of nothing defined at the table, acronyms expanded; (7) repetition — the 85/32 figure now appears twice, not five times; Bevan & Hood moved to a flag; section 11 trimmed; (8) inline evidence annotations read as a system annotating itself — rewritten into prose. Not taken: removing the back-matter apparatus (gaps, conflict log, looking back) — it is the book's method; a one-line explanation was added instead. Checklist and rules table kept as the template requires.

**Verification agent (twenty items at source):** eighteen found verbatim or with trivial truncations; two errors corrected — Coiera's quasi-experimental studies were *not* "without a control" (fixed), and the 2014 confidence-survey figure of 88% measured trust in the ONS as an institution, not its statistics (fixed to the like-for-like 81% → 85%). Also fixed: a Verbert quotation misplaced as Jivet's; the Star & Griesemer "ideal type" paraphrase presented as a quotation (original reached at an open copy, now quoted exactly); the Sarikaya community-safety case attributed to their corpus rather than their literature review; "regions" → "census divisions" for Robinson; Bach dated consistently 2023; ellipses added where quotations were silently truncated. Subramanian et al. remain abstract-only (†).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` staleness · gaming typologies · dashboards-as-boundary-objects (van Rij 2026; Star 2010) · Porter in full · abstract-only sources behind Fixed rules (Gerrish, van der Bles, Ivanković, Kaliisa, Turnhout, Subramanian) · Geddes's Outlook Tower as counter-model (Ch 15)
`[Prototype flag]` measure use from day one · positive-deviance analysis on the project's data
`[Validate with users]` the four discovery tests as boundary-object tests across the three user groups
`[Revisit at end]` the suppression threshold's three consequences · whether "dashboard" survived as the book's word
`[Counterbalance → old Ch 5 / new Ch 7]` comparison of conditions by rate needs intervals

### Rich references for Carly

Five, in the chapter's closing flags: Mattern 2015; Kitchin, Lauriault & McArdle 2015; Wainer 2007; Jivet et al. 2018; O'Neill's fourth Reith Lecture. If only one: **Mattern** — it is the whole argument about the mud, in forty minutes, and it will change how you talk about the Vibe Map to people who expect a dashboard.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- Four research agents in parallel at ~150–185k tokens each, plus a cold reader (~100k) and a verifier (~160k), is the right shape; the verifier earned its cost twice over (two factual errors, one misattribution, one false quotation).
- Brief the research agents to mark abstract-only reads explicitly in a column; it saves the verifier time and the chapter a convention.
- Write the chapter once, cold-read and verify, then rewrite. Do not try to pre-empt the cold reader.
- Chapter 0 needs: a fifth kind (*Practice*) for practitioner sources; the † convention; the flags table already there.

*Entry 1 closed 6 October 2026. Chapter 1 delivered as draft 2.*

## Entry 2 · Chapter 2 — Who reads it, and what turns their decision

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, on Carly's "let's progress".*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** Who reads a dashboard, how does each kind of reader actually take in evidence, and what moves them from reading to deciding?

**Sub-questions.**
1. The readers, as decision-makers: policy officers and elected members; economic development, placemaking, regeneration and inward-investment teams, and the developers they deal with; advocates and trade bodies; funders and investors (public, philanthropic, private); researchers; and the scenes themselves. For each: what decision do they make, what evidence are they trained or required to accept, and what moves them?
2. How a government appraiser reads evidence — the UK appraisal and evaluation frameworks and the evidence hierarchies read for *how they grade and use evidence*, never for nightlife facts: options appraisal, benefit–cost logic, wellbeing valuation, standards of evidence, theory of change.
3. What the evidence-informed-policy research says actually moves a decision: barriers and facilitators to evidence use; the "two communities"; enlightenment versus instrumental use; policy windows; advocacy coalitions; the preference for local, timely, relevant evidence; evidence as ammunition.
4. Persuasion by data: narrative against statistics; the identifiable victim; whether charts persuade more than numbers; what readers do with uncertainty, with rankings, with maps.
5. Place readers specifically: the frameworks and indicators that economic development and placemaking already use; the creative-placemaking indicator debate (Markusen's "fuzzy concepts, proxy data"); cultural vitality indicators; social value in planning and procurement; wellbeing valuation; and the sectors — sport, parks, libraries — that moved their framing from cost to value, and how.
6. Funders and investors: impact frameworks and what they require of evidence; the critiques of impact measurement; what a funder's reader is actually looking for.
7. Advocates: how coalitions build and use shared evidence; the ethics of numbers for advocacy versus numbers for understanding.
8. The scenes as readers: lived experience as evidence; participatory evaluation; recognition as validation.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** HM Treasury Green Book and Magenta Book (as frames); the wellbeing supplementary guidance; Maryland Scientific Methods Scale; Nesta Standards of Evidence; the What Works network; Oliver et al. 2014 systematic review of evidence use; Cairney; Nutley, Walter & Davies; Weiss; Parkhurst; Kingdon; Sabatier's Advocacy Coalition Framework; the Narrative Policy Framework; Small, Loewenstein & Slovic on the identifiable victim; Pandey et al. 2014 on the persuasive power of visualisation; Tal & Wansink; Markusen & Gadwa 2010 and Markusen 2013; Jackson et al. (Urban Institute) cultural vitality indicators; Gehl; Social Value Act and TOMs; HACT social value bank; DCMS Culture and Heritage Capital; Fujiwara / WELLBY; Sport England social value; Fields in Trust *Revaluing Parks*; Impact Management Project; Ebrahim & Rangan; Lowe on outcomes; the lived-experience and participatory-evaluation literature.

**What I expect to find.** That the evidence-use literature says relevance, timeliness and relationships move decisions more than rigour does; that charts persuade more than numbers and that this is a risk as much as an asset; that place-indicator debates have already been fought in creative placemaking and lost on exactly Carly's grounds; that wellbeing valuation is the frame other sectors used to move from cost to value; that funders' frameworks reward theory of change and measured outcomes and are widely criticised for it; and that lived experience is accepted as evidence in principle and discounted in practice.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) how government readers read and grade evidence, and what the evidence-use research says moves them; (B) persuasion by data — narrative, statistics, charts, maps, rankings; (C) place readers — economic development, placemaking, regeneration, investment, and the sectors that reframed cost as value; (D) funders, investors, advocates and the scenes — impact frameworks and their critiques, advocacy evidence, lived experience as evidence.

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 5 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch02-agent-returns.md`). Threads that were not in the frame and were followed:

1. **Both Treasury books were rewritten in 2026.** Agent A found that the Green Book (5 February 2026) and the Magenta Book (May 2026) have replaced the editions most people hold; the 2022 Green Book is withdrawn and the old asset URLs serve the new text. The chapter cites the 2026 editions and uses the 2022 "five questions" only where the new text drops them. The 2026 Green Book contains no mention of the WELLBY or life satisfaction; the £13,000 survives only in the 2021 supplementary guidance.
2. **The Magenta Book's definition of monitoring is a definition of a dashboard.** "Did we do what we said we would do? How are our success metrics changing over time?" — explicitly not evaluation. This became the chapter's central standard and its most important rule (label every panel monitoring or evaluation).
3. **Phil Davies' inverted hierarchy** (Box 6 in Nutley, Powell & Davies 2013): followed to attribution — a 2007 remark by the then Deputy Chief Social Researcher, reproduced by the Alliance for Useful Evidence. Research ninth, behind "Cabbies' evidence".
4. **The identifiable victim effect has not held.** Expected to find Small, Loewenstein & Slovic as a load-bearing result; found instead Lee & Feeley's pooled r = .05 and Maier, Wong & Feldman's preregistered null (n = 1,004) with publication bias in the meta-analysis. The chapter does not lean on Rokia.
5. **Pandey's reversal.** Not in the frame: for readers already against a message, tables out-persuaded charts in all three topics (42.6% v 78.7%). The authors call it uncertain; the hostile cells were small; no replication found. It is the chapter's most useful and least secure finding and is tiered Adaptable as a hypothesis.
6. **Hastings & Weinstein, properly read.** Agent B presented it as "re-sorting into a ranking changed decisions". The orchestrator's spot-check found the authors' own conclusion: the one-page sorted sheet added little over the three-page alphabetical one; "most of the effect on choices may come from providing clear statistics at the time of choice, rather than the exact format". Moved from the rankings section to the timing argument; logged as a gap because the misreading is common.
7. **The ArtPlace vibrancy indicators.** Followed from Markusen 2013 to Gadwa Nicodemus's list of the indicators, to Moss's 2012 "outcomes problem" post, to ArtPlace's own retrospective recording the shift away from the indicators from 2014. The arc closes: dropped within two years of the critique, by the organisation's own account under a new director and a wider model.
8. **Markusen's own example of contested vibrancy is a live music scene** — "youth magnet, older resident nightmare". Quoted as hers; not researched further (nightlife is outside the book's remit).
9. **Two routes out of "cost"** (monetise the benefit; refuse the number and define the construct) emerged from Agent C's sector cases and became the organising idea of section 6, together with headline-to-caveat decay (Fields in Trust's two methods on one survey, £30 against £974; Sport England's caveat-free 2025 release) and property value as the contested indicator.
10. **Ebrahim & Rangan** hand the job of measuring impact to the level that can see the system — the strongest academic warrant found for a shared place- or sector-level evidence base.
11. **Lived experience admitted then re-filed** (Hill O'Connor & Bennett's three Scottish cases) and **recognition as member checking** (Lincoln & Guba; Birt et al.) turned the brief's failure condition "do not recognise their place" into a method with a stated condition: a dialogue that can change the account, before release.
12. **The IMP "What" dimension** already asks how important an outcome is to the people experiencing it — the lived-experience test the policy literature says is missing is written into the investor consensus.

Threads noticed and not pulled: Moynihan and Kroll on managers' use of performance information; Hullman & Diakopoulos on visualisation rhetoric; Muehlenhaus on persuasive cartography; Slovic's psychic numbing; Reinholtz et al. on cumulative versus daily COVID curves; the RICS viability professional statements; the Magenta Book's new QPIE and TIGER supplements; Nesta's 2015 standards for impact investing. All cheap to add later; none changed the chapter's answer.

### Sources read

Fifty-eight items were re-traced by the verification agent; the full list with how each was read is in the chapter's Sources section and in `SOURCES.md` §6. Of the chapter's sources, the research agents read the large majority at source; eleven were abstract-only (marked † in the chapter); five reached the chapter second-hand and are marked so (Weiss 1979; Kingdon via Mintrom & True; Sabatier and Jenkins-Smith & Sabatier via Cairney; Lincoln & Guba via Roller; Cousins & Whitmore and Fetterman via BetterEvaluation; Boy et al. via Liem and Shao). The orchestrator spot-checked ten items before writing: the Green Book 2026 social-value and BCR sentences; the Magenta Book's Figure 1.1; Nutley's Box 6; Kelly's "Welcome to democracy" passage; Pandey's four percentages; Markusen's six sentences; Fields in Trust's figures; Oliver's abstract; NPC's 2013 percentages; Hastings & Weinstein's design and conclusion.

### What I learned

- There is no reader in general, and the eight readers the brief implies are graded by rules that contradict each other; the honest aim for one display is legibility, not persuasion, and even legibility is an aim rather than a demonstrated possibility, because the choice of what to count is made in somebody's grammar.
- To the official reader a dashboard is monitoring, not evaluation; on the Maryland, Nesta and EEF ladders it stands alone on the first or second rung. This is a definition, not a criticism, and it is the single most useful thing a nightlife dashboard can know about its most powerful reader.
- The Treasury's order of preference is pounds, numbers, words — and the BCR is not the decision; the Treasury had to say so in a review because its readers believed otherwise. Distribution across groups and places is now a required question.
- What moves readers is access, timing, relevance and relationships, known mostly from self-report; locally, local beats rigorous; in Parliament, source credibility ranks first and nobody sorts by method.
- Charts persuade the undecided through information; the trivial-graph effect did not replicate; the hostile reader may prefer the table. Numbers move belief, stories move intention, both weakly. People do not scale with magnitude; the denominator is the persuasive unit. Rankings act on the ranked. Maps persuade through normalisation. Quantified uncertainty is cheap. Field effects are real, modest per reader, large in aggregate.
- Place indicators from secondary data describe a place and cannot evidence an intervention (Markusen's three failure modes). Define the construct before the proxy. Every valuation headline loses its caveats downstream; the dashboard is the last place they survive. Property value is the contested indicator.
- Funders require more than they use; their ladders start with an account; impact belongs to the level that can see the system. Lived experience is admitted and re-filed; recognition is a credibility test only as a dialogue that can change the account.

### What I left out, and why

- Weiss 1979 at source — unreachable; quoted second-hand and flagged. Parkhurst's criteria of appropriate evidence — chapter 6 unreachable; omitted. Tal & Wansink's own percentages — not read at source; omitted. A "Dr Foster effect" — no evaluation found; not asserted. Impact & Insight's dimension statements — not on any readable page; not quoted. The Social Value Model's 10% weighting — unverified; omitted. The precise number of IMP signatories — not on the page read; removed in draft 2.
- Section 10 of draft 1 (the failure register re-read) — cut in draft 2 on the cold reader's advice: four rows that repeated the chapter.
- Marshall et al. 2000 — the verification agent could not re-open the JAMA abstract; retained from the research agent's read, marked †.

### Open questions

Whether one display can be legible to eight graders at all (`[Revisit at end]`); whether monetisation ever got a sector funded (`[Further research]`); whether Pandey's reversal replicates (`[Further research]` `[Prototype flag]`); the developer's and trade body's calendars; how UK trade bodies' evidence is received by government; how foundation officers weigh relationships against reports.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter's "Looking back". In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's truths 5, 7, 8, 11, 15 and 16; counterbalances its section 11 (the club flyer persuades the persuadable) and its truth 2 as phrased (a display for everybody is graded by contradictory rules, and there is no neutral layer under the grammars); opens a thread on truth 10 (place indicators reward the already-successful — reactivity of places, not only institutions); opens a thread on truth 8 (Markant: shown uncertainty may harden the hostile reader — `[Counterbalance → Ch 3]`). For Chapter 0, the case for a fifth kind, *Practice*, is now overwhelming and the revision follows this entry. Against the archived version 1: the leverage-points answer is sharpened (a defensible number is one that survives the other coalition's analyst) and its assumption that a strong value headline moves the economic-development reader is weakened.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, fifty-eight items, 5–6 October). The large majority verified exactly, including every Treasury quotation, Box 6, the Kelly interviews, all of Pandey's figures, Markusen's sentences, Fields in Trust's figures, Ebrahim & Rangan's page numbers and the NPC percentages. Mismatches corrected in draft 2: Engel et al.'s first author is Claudia, not Jeffrey, and the 53.2%/44.5% figure pools "mostly urban" with "somewhat more urban"; Schoemaker's EUR 21,307–1,606 range is across activities, not the same activity, and the income-causality sentence is relayed, not the review's finding; the Wellbeing Guidance's "less confidence" bullet was truncated — the full wording is "a one-off cross-sectional analysis of choices which are deliberated", and the adjacent bullet (a marginal change in a global measure such as life satisfaction) is the better hook for a place dashboard, so the chapter now uses it; the Evidence Transparency Framework's scale was paraphrased in quotation marks and the claim that testing and evaluation scored lowest could not be confirmed — both removed; Espeland & Sauder's "artificial lines" speaker is a faculty member, not a dean; Kneale's smoking quotation is from a health and wellbeing board member, not a councillor; the Green Book Review's "BCR thresholds" line is a Treasury commitment about what the Green Book will say; the Inspiring Impact principle begins "If appropriate"; the Createquity title is "Won't", the journal's "would not"; two Sabatier lines are Jenkins-Smith & Sabatier 1993; the optimism-bias quotation was a splice of two sentences; "drop-off over time", "qualitative data and stakeholder voice" and "amenability to local action" were paraphrases in quotation marks. Oliver et al.'s Table 1 was settled (63/54/42; 65/49/46/39/37) and the counts are now printed. Marshall 2000 could not be re-opened and is marked †.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted: the chapter asserted that one display can be legible to all eight readers while its own sources (Parkhurst's issue bias; Sabatier; Kahan; Markusen; the property-value passage) say the first screen is always in somebody's grammar. Draft 2 rewrites the chapter's rule to require that the choice of grammar be made and declared, says in section 1 that legibility is an aim rather than a demonstrated possibility, and flags truth 1's design consequence for demotion if Part 3 cannot deliver it. Its second objection — no reader was interviewed, and two rows of the table have no study behind them — is now stated in section 1 and flagged. Other changes: two quotations that put public health officers' words in councillors' mouths (60-second version; section 1) corrected; three different numbers for Pandey's headline reconciled; terms used before explanation glossed (SMART, SWB, citizen science, NCLB, Dr Foster, Cohen's d, the Matthew effect, the club flyer, "the brief"); study authors named in the body where the truths cited them by surname; the identifiable-victim "collapsed to near zero" softened to what the evidence shows; the vibrancy-indicator abandonment no longer presented as caused by the critique; "every evidence ladder" narrowed to the three examined; "stop making the growth case" replaced with what the source says; superlatives removed; belief four admitted as untested and flagged; truth 14 (field effects) reframed as modest per reader and large in aggregate; section 10 cut; the plain-table rule and the trivial-graph rule re-tiered; a dozen "X is not Y; it is Z" constructions cut or recast; the Fields in Trust thirty-fold spread promoted into the headline-to-caveat finding and truth 15. Twenty-three truths became twenty-one.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` Weiss 1979 at source · Parkhurst ch. 6 · Nutley, Walter & Davies 2007 typology · Lincoln & Guba p. 314 · eleven abstract-only sources · Pandey replication · did monetisation get anyone funded · the developer's and trade body's calendars · UK trade-body evidence reception · foundation officers' weighting · Dr Foster · Boy et al. 2015 · Magenta Book QPIE/TIGER supplements · Sense about Science 2024 stage scores · ONS pride-in-place grouping. `[Prototype flag]` measure which readers open which panels and when · prepare where a comparison would sit · test the plain table on first readers. `[Validate with users]` the reader table itself · chart v table with the three groups · property value with residents and investors · the recognition route with the scene. `[Revisit → Ch 3]` Markant on uncertainty and the hostile reader. `[Revisit → Ch 5]` wellbeing valuation's thirty-fold spread. `[Revisit → Ch 7]` the counterfactual; Maryland Level 1. `[Revisit → Ch 9]` define the construct before the proxy. `[Revisit → Ch 10]` issue bias; numbers for advocacy v understanding; control and voice. `[Revisit → Ch 12]` member checking; the ward. `[Revisit → Ch 14]` Pandey; Liem; Allcott; the flyer against the table. `[Revisit at end]` can one display be legible to eight graders. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1 §11; Ch 14]` the club flyer. `[Counterbalance → design system]` the joy-first register against the hostile reader's table.

### Rich references for Carly

Three worth her own time, from a long list: **Markusen, "Fuzzy concepts, proxy data"** (Createquity, 2012; open) — the whole place-indicator argument in one essay, with her own live-music example; **Kelly et al. 2017** (*Journal of Public Health*; open) — nine pages, and the councillors and officers say it themselves; **Fields in Trust, *Revaluing Parks and Green Spaces*** (2018; open) — one document that shows both valuation methods, the thirty-fold spread and the caveat, which is the whole route-one story in miniature. For building: **Hastings & Weinstein's NBER paper** has the actual information sheets; **Hullman 2020** has the reasons-table; **the DCMS Culture and Heritage Capital framework** has an architecture a value layer could mirror; **Ebrahim & Rangan** is the case for a shared evidence base.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- The spot-check that changed the most (Hastings & Weinstein) was of a claim the research agent had stated confidently and in the direction the chapter wanted. Spot-check the convenient findings first.
- The verification agent's fifty-eight-item brief was long and it completed; the item-by-item form with VERIFIED / WITH DIFFERENCE / UNVERIFIABLE is worth keeping. Ask it explicitly for paraphrases presented as quotations — that question caught five.
- The cold reader's "X is not Y; it is Z" count is a useful tic-detector; run a grep for it before dispatching the cold read next time.
- Chapter 0 needs the *Practice* kind before Chapter 3 is written; do that revision now.
- A chapter of this length (about 160,000 characters) took one writing pass and one revision pass; the template's closing sections (truths, rules, checklist) repeat each other by design, and the cold reader noticed. Consider, for Chapter 3 onward, keeping the truths and the rules and shortening the checklist to the items not already a rule.

## Entry 3 · Chapter 3 — The canonical truths of showing data

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 2 was placed and Chapter 0 revised.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** Of everything the field says about how to show data, what has actually been tested, what survived, and what is folklore — and which of the survivors are hard lines?

**Sub-questions.**
1. Perception and encoding: what people can read accurately from position, length, angle, area, colour and shape (Cleveland & McGill and its replications); what is seen before attention (preattentive features); grouping (Gestalt); what this settles about chart choice and what it does not.
2. Colour: what colour can carry and what it cannot; the rainbow problem; the one standard that says colour never carries meaning alone, and how firm it is; classification breaks on maps.
3. Words: titles, labels, annotation and the message; whether text dominates what is read and remembered; direct labelling against legends; what a title does to a reader's reading of the same chart.
4. The glance: how fast appeal and first judgement form; what drives them; glance cost by chart form; what "glanceable" can and cannot promise.
5. Memorability and ornament: the data-ink doctrine against the chartjunk experiments; what makes a chart remembered and whether that is the same as understood.
6. Axes and scales: truncated axes, dual axes, log scales, cumulative curves, 3D, pies — what the experiments show rather than what the style guides assert; rounding and significant figures; tables against graphs and when each is right.
7. Interaction, defaults and animation: how many readers touch the interaction layer; the default effect and anchoring; "overview first" audited; animation and animated transitions; scrolling narratives.
8. Uncertainty: what forms of uncertainty display people read correctly (error bars, intervals, quantile dotplots, hypothetical outcome plots, icon arrays); what misleads; the trust evidence already in Chapters 1 and 2; and Chapter 2's counterbalance — whether shown uncertainty hardens a reader with strong priors.
9. Literacy: what a general public can read; graph literacy measures; what that implies for a display meant for a dancefloor and a committee room at once.
10. The one accessibility standard this chapter owns (colour alone), with the rest of the W3C material Carly sent handed to Chapter 13, and a first classification of which of those documents are hard lines and which are interpretable.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Cleveland & McGill 1984 and Heer & Bostock 2010; Healey & Enns on preattentive processing; Ware; Franconeri, Padilla, Shah, Zacks & Hullman 2021 ("The Science of Visual Data Communication: What Works"); Brewer and ColorBrewer; Borland & Taylor on rainbow; WCAG 2.2 success criterion 1.4.1 and the WAI tutorials on complex images; Borkin et al. 2013 and 2015; Bateman et al. 2010; Kong, Liu & Agrawala 2018 and 2019 on titles; Stokes & Hearst on text; Tuch et al. 2012; Lindgaard et al. 2006; Blascheck et al. 2018; Tufte's data-ink doctrine and its tests; Correll, Bertini & Franconeri 2020 on truncated axes; Yang et al. 2021; Isenberg et al. 2011 on dual axes; Romano et al. 2020 on log scales; Ehrenberg 1977 on tables and rounding; Vessey 1991 cognitive fit; Aisch on interaction rates; Jachimowicz et al. 2019 (already verified in version 1); Cho et al. 2017; Shneiderman 1996; Craft & Cairns 2005; Hornbæk & Hertzum 2011; van Ham & Perer 2009; Tversky, Morrison & Bétrancourt 2002; Robertson et al. 2008; Heer & Robertson 2007; McKenna et al. 2017 on scrollytelling; Hullman et al. 2015 (HOPs); Kay et al. 2016 (quantile dotplots); Correll & Gleicher 2014; Padilla et al. 2021; Hofman, Goldstein & Hullman 2020; Garcia-Retamero & Cokely on icon arrays; Galesic & Garcia-Retamero 2011 graph literacy; Börner et al. 2016 data visualisation literacy; Okan et al.; the Decision Book's data chapters (as the studio's axioms, to be cited as Nightlife Culture); version 1's Chapter 1 evidence (defaults, anchoring, overview-first, glance), re-verified where it is load-bearing.

**What I expect to find.** That the perceptual hierarchy is the best-replicated thing in the field and settles less than people think; that the colour-alone rule is a genuine Standard and nearly everything else about colour is Adaptable; that text carries the message and is under-designed; that the glance evidence is about appeal, not understanding; that chartjunk is more memorable and not less accurate, which embarrasses both camps; that truncated axes exaggerate even when labelled; that interaction is touched by a minority; that "overview first" is folklore and defaults are endorsements; that uncertainty can be shown in forms people read correctly and that the forms differ in how well; that graph literacy in the general population is lower than designers assume; and that Franconeri et al. 2021 will turn out to be the single best map of the territory.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) perception, encoding, colour, axes and scales, tables against graphs; (B) words, the glance, memorability and ornament; (C) interaction, defaults, animation, overview-first, scrolling; (D) uncertainty display, risk communication, literacy, and the W3C documents classified as hard line or interpretable.

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 6 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch03-agent-returns.md`). Threads that were not in the frame and were followed:

1. **The hierarchy's own authors called its bottom half conjecture.** Cleveland & McGill 1985: "partly conjectural in that we have no controlled experimentation to support them." Pulled from there to Kosara's three pie studies (area, not angle) and to Davis et al. 2022 (individual differences larger than the gaps between pie, bubble and stacked bar). The chapter's treatment of the Decision Book's chart rules follows from this.
2. **Dashboard-specific eye tracking exists** — Yang et al. 2025, 60 people and 1,216 real dashboards: text draws attention out of proportion to its area; the plain number is the most-looked-at object; subtitles outdraw titles; upper-left and title-first. Found while looking for F-pattern evidence; supersedes the F-pattern for this book.
3. **The title decides the reading** — Kong, Liu & Karahalios 2018 and 2019 (nationally representative samples): 65% of recalled messages reflected the title; 72–87% called slanted titles neutral; readers trust the title least and remember it most. Plus Kim, Setlur & Agrawala (n = 2,168): a caption can only redirect to what the picture already makes salient. These became truth 2.
4. **Nobody has run legends against labels.** The field's most-repeated rule rests on a working-memory mechanism, legend re-fixation counts and a meta-analysis from instructional design (Ginns, d = 0.72). Named in the chapter as "Practice backed by analogous Evidence", a sub-kind Chapter 0 may want.
5. **Ajani et al. 2022** — the experiment the chartjunk debate needed: declutter reads as professional; headline + highlight + sentence is what is remembered; trust unmoved by either. Twenty-four people; the cold reader rightly objected to the weight the first draft put on it, and draft 2 re-tiers it.
6. **Truncation: the label does not help** (Correll et al. 2020; Yang et al. 2021; Long & Kay 2024) — and the verifier found the Analysis Function's 2023 checklist already says so for bars. The Decision Book's "may be broken … and labelled" for line charts is too comfortable.
7. **The "85% ignore interactives" figure** traced to Aisch's footnote (one button, a couple of 2015 graphics, a click rate not an ignore rate), Tse's slides (no percentage at all), and replaced with Conlen, Kale & Heer 2019 (median use of optional controls zero; mobile touches less and leaves sooner).
8. **The Jachimowicz quotation.** Agent C reported that version 1's quoted sentence ("individuals commonly perceive defaults as conveying an endorsement by the choice architect") was not in the paper. The orchestrator's re-check found it in the Cambridge Core text; the verifier found it in both the author PDF and the published text, citing McKenzie et al. 2006. The agent's correction was wrong; draft 1 half-repeated it; draft 2 records the episode.
9. **Hofman et al. 2020** — a confidence interval round a mean makes readers overestimate the effect on individuals (86% v 67% v a true 57%); the single most relevant uncertainty finding for a dashboard that shows averages with intervals.
10. **Wintle et al. 2019** — "likely" alone: 32% agreement with the intended band; with a number in brackets beside it, 66%; a separate glossary did not help. A cheap, concrete rule.
11. **Reinholtz et al. 2021** — stock against flow: the same falling data judged riskier as a cumulative curve (d = 0.74). Sibling of Chapter 2's denominator finding.
12. **The W3C classification** — five criteria bite on charts (1.1.1, 1.3.1, 1.4.1, 1.4.11, 2.1.1); plain language is AAA and so guidance; ARIA has no chart role and the 2015 proposal was abandoned; COGA is a Note by its own words; a non-public body is reached through the Equality Act's anticipatory duty with WCAG 2.2 AA as the yardstick. Handed to Chapter 13.
13. **The literacy numbers** — PIAAC 2023 England 21% at Level 1 or below (improving); Skills for Life 2011 49.1% at Entry Level 3 or below (a different threshold); Galesic & Garcia-Retamero's "one third" with low graph literacy and numeracy (Germany and the US); and the OSR's finding that no UK survey captures statistical literacy as such.

Threads noticed and not pulled: Sevi et al. 2020 (a reported null on log/linear and attitudes); Reda & Szafir 2021 "Rainbows Revisited"; Fernandes et al. 2018 (dotplots improve decisions); Schwabish 2021 practitioner companion; Chevalier et al. 2016 animation review; Seyser & Zeiller on scrollytelling; Okan et al. 2016 eye-tracking; Shah & Freedman 2011; Hullman, Adar & Shah 2011 on desirable difficulties. All flagged.

### Sources read

About a hundred and ten sources, listed in the chapter with how each was read and in `SOURCES.md` §7. The large majority at source (author, institutional or publisher copies; the W3C and legislation pages themselves); seventeen abstract-only (marked †); Tufte, Weiss-style secondaries for Borland & Taylor, Vessey & Galletta, Boy et al. 2015, Galesic et al. 2009 and Kinkeldey et al. 2017 marked secondary. The orchestrator spot-checked eight items before writing: Jachimowicz (twice, including the disputed sentence); Correll et al.; Kong 2018; Hornbæk & Hertzum; Stokes et al.; Hofman et al.; Yang et al. 2025. The verifier re-traced fifty-eight numbered items plus four own-voice claims.

### What I learned

- The field's rules are mostly untested; the ones that survive are short: choose the comparison; words carry the message and the title decides the reading; colour never alone and labels at the mark; bars from zero and no label undoes a truncation; uncertainty countable, never a boundary, with the number beside the word; nothing crucial behind a hover, on a phone, for a reader at Level 1; every default is advice.
- The perceptual hierarchy settles precise comparison and nothing else; its lower half is conjecture its authors admitted; pies are read by area; individual differences exceed the chart-type gaps.
- The first glance is appeal, not meaning, and rewards simplicity and resemblance to the thing claimed — an argument for convention at the level of the chart and invention elsewhere, which is where the Decision Book already puts it.
- Memorability is not comprehension; ornament that is the data is free; ornament on the mark costs accuracy; what is remembered is the headline and the highlight.
- Interaction use is low and the famous number is an anecdote; overview-first is a preference; animation is enjoyed and inaccurate except for transitions.
- A confidence interval round a mean overstates the effect on individuals; experts misread error bars; frequency formats and icon arrays help the numerate-but-innumerate and not the graph-illiterate.
- There is no UK measure of whether the public can read a chart.

### What I left out, and why

- Franconeri's last third (uncertainty, summary) — fetch cap; publisher summary used; flagged.
- Tufte, Ehrenberg, Vessey 1991, Lindgaard 2011, Siegrist, Cleveland McGill & McGill 1988 — paywalled; secondaries or abstracts, marked.
- Draft 1's "no comprehensive public assessment" (OSR) — removed: the source does not contain it. Draft 1's Szafir "~25% / thin lines more again" — removed: the figure is not in the paper and the direction for lines was reversed. Draft 1's gap-table claim that no source says a labelled truncation does not help — corrected: the Analysis Function checklist says it.
- The big-number tile, legends against labels, interaction and understanding — no evidence found; stated as gaps and flagged for the prototype.

### Open questions

Legends against labels, directly tested; the big-number tile; whether interacting improves understanding; a replication of Ajani; Sevi on log scales and attitudes; Franconeri's last third; the public's chart literacy in the UK.

### Looking back

Recorded in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's truth 8 and Chapter 2's truth 12 (numeric uncertainty) with the forms and counterbalances both with Markant; strengthens Chapter 1's truth 11 (rankings as screens with intervals) with the caveat that bare intervals mislead; strengthens the denominator findings of Chapters 1 and 2 with stock/flow and 1-in-X; strengthens Chapter 2's "information not aura" and "plain table for the hostile reader"; opens a thread on the reader's schema as part of naming the reader; weakens version 1's Fixed tiering of "label at the mark"; and runs a formal agrees/conflicts/silent-on log against the Decision Book's Chapter 6 — the 3D and pie reasons rest on the conjectural half of the hierarchy; the hierarchy rule should be Fixed only for precise comparison; and the Decision Book is silent on titles, text quantity, truncation-with-label, stock/flow, uncertainty forms, the glance, interaction rates and defaults. For Chapter 0: the *Practice* kind earns its place again, and two markings are proposed — "Practice backed by analogous Evidence" and "Evidence (adjacent domain)".

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, fifty-eight items and four own-voice claims). The large majority verified exactly. Ranked mismatches, all corrected: the OSR quotation "no comprehensive public assessment" does not exist — replaced with the regulator's words and the headline softened throughout; Szafir's "~25%" is not in the paper and "thin lines more again" reversed the finding — corrected; the gap table's "no source anywhere says a labelled truncation does not help" was wrong — the Analysis Function checklist says it, now credited; Talbot's 7.38 is an absolute error, not a bias, and the part-of-whole account is the authors' "one possible explanation" — reworded; Jachimowicz is 58 datasets from 55 studies, and the sentence is in both copies — corrected and the Looking-back narrative rewritten; Conlen's "three quarters reached the end" was true of two articles of three — corrected; Skau's "Nearly all embellishments increased errors substantially" was a paraphrase in quotation marks — replaced with the paper's sentence; the PSBAR exemption wording was GOV.UK's, not the Regulations' — both now given and attributed; Ginns's 0.78 pooled spatial and temporal — clause removed; Tuch used 120 sites, not 200; Correll's Experiment 2 null was p = 0.05 — now stated; Franconeri's colour sentence was clipped before "or allow viewers to change the color palette" and the 37% is a Pew survey — both now said. Eleven further paraphrases in quotation marks (Cleveland's 1.96; Heer & Bostock's 80 pixels; Stokes's "sizable"; the NN/g sentence; Padilla's cone; the Padilla/Kay/Hullman frequency sentence attributed to "other work"; the Analysis Function colour limit; the Garcia-Retamero heuristics; Fagerlin's steps) were unquoted or restored to the source's words.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted: the chapter mocked Bateman's ten people and then tiered rules Fixed on Ajani's 24, Blascheck's 36 on a smartwatch, Lindgaard's 22–40 on websites, Jachimowicz's policy defaults and Conlen's news articles, while marking only "label at the mark" as analogous. Draft 2 marks adjacent-domain and small-sample evidence in the rules table, re-tiers the radial-bar and headline-plus-highlight rules Adaptable (strong defaults), and splits section 13 into seven canonical truths and a record of findings beneath them — the cold reader observed that the 60-second version's closing sentence already was the canon. Other changes: "triples recall" (three times) reduced to what one small study found; "log scales lose six readers in ten" corrected to the actual contrast; "ornament is more memorable and not less accurate" qualified (not when it touches the mark); "most readers touch nothing" → "nothing optional"; the d = 0.68 attached to the right clause; "8% of men cannot see red from green" → have a red–green deficiency; "nearly half of all text gets looked at" replaced with what saliency coverage means; unexplained terms glossed (ROI; TOs/VOs; saliency coverage; HOP before definition; PIAAC; protanopia; VLAT; the design system; the three user groups); the two Yangs distinguished; Brath, Cole, McDowell & Jacobs and Akl named in the body; three unevidenced flourishes cut ("most-cited and least-read sentence"; "the strongest finding … is not about charts"; Few "right about the sample"); a choropleth paragraph added to section 4 and an "interactivity as a good" paragraph to section 9, both promised by section 2 and missing; section 1's claim to "borrow" Franconeri's headings softened; repeated signature phrases reduced.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` legends v labels (direct test) · dual axes · big-number tile · interaction and understanding · replicate Ajani · Franconeri last third · seventeen abstract-only sources · Sevi 2020 · Reda & Szafir 2021 · Tufte at source · Galesic item-level results · COGA's numerical patterns · Fernandes 2018 · Schwabish 2021. `[Prototype flag]` legends v labels on the Vibe Map · big number v sentence · measure the glance (time to first interaction, device) · measure chart literacy with the three groups. `[Validate with users]` the sceptic's uncertainty form · the designer's blind spot (someone who did not choose the default says what it shows) · big number against sentence. `[Revisit → Ch 5]` precision and rounding. `[Revisit → Ch 7]` stock/flow; truncation as effect size; small multiples across places. `[Revisit → Ch 11]` first screen: fast-glance forms, upper-left, title then filters, defaults, mobile first. `[Revisit → Ch 12]` schema and recognition. `[Revisit → Ch 13]` the W3C classification, COGA, the Analysis Function and ONS rules. `[Revisit → Ch 14]` prototypicality v novelty; ornament that is the data. `[Revisit at end]` does "canonical" survive — seven rules and a record. `[Counterbalance → Ch 2 §5]` Markant. `[Counterbalance → Decision Book Ch 6]` the hierarchy's reasons; the line-axis label. `[Counterbalance → design system]` where on a dashboard the joy goes.

### Rich references for Carly

**Franconeri, Padilla, Shah, Zacks & Hullman (2021)** — open access; if one document, this one. **Kong, Liu & Karahalios (2018, 2019)** — the title decides the reading and readers do not notice; the pair is short. **Correll, Bertini & Franconeri (2020)** — labelling a truncated axis does not help; eight pages, clean. For building: **Stokes et al. (2023)** for how much text and where; **Yang et al. (2025)** for where eyes go on real dashboards; **the Government Analysis Function's charts guidance and 2023 checklist** — the UK's own translation of WCAG into chart rules, and the only one.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- The sprint's worst error was a quotation that flattered the argument ("no comprehensive public assessment"), relayed by a research agent with page number and all, and repeated in draft 1 because it fitted. Spot-check the quotations that fit best first.
- A research agent's *correction* of an earlier chapter can be wrong (the Jachimowicz sentence). Treat an agent's "this quotation is not in the paper" as a claim to verify, not a finding.
- The cold reader's symmetry test — does the chapter apply its own evidential standard to the findings it likes? — should be run by the orchestrator before dispatch. Draft 1 failed it; draft 2 passes it only by marking the asymmetry.
- The truths section works better as "seven rules and a record" than as a numbered list of everything; carry that form into Chapter 4.
- Chapter 0 needs two markings added when next revised: "Practice backed by analogous Evidence" and "Evidence (adjacent domain)".

## Entry 4 · Chapter 4 — From data to evidence

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, on Carly's "please proceed". First chapter of Part 1, the analytical foundation.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What has to be true of a number before it can be shown as evidence — and what does a dashboard owe the reader about where it came from, what it counts, what it leaves out, and what it can be compared with?

**Sub-questions.**
1. Grain: the unit of observation and the unit of display — event, venue, person, place, night — and why the choice is made before any analysis; the unit of time (the statistical day, the reference period, a night that crosses midnight as a general measurement problem, not a nightlife fact); tidy-data and statistical-unit conventions.
2. Denominator: a count against what; rates, exposure, population at risk, person-time; the reference class; why the base is the most consequential and least visible choice in a display; per-capita and per-unit pitfalls.
3. Provenance: what the official world requires a number to carry — source, reference period, geography and boundary vintage, method, revision status, disclosure treatment — and the standards behind that (the Code of Practice's quality pillar; the European statistical quality dimensions; the UK Government Data Quality Framework; the Aqua Book on analytical assurance; W3C PROV, DCAT and FAIR as machine forms); data quality dimensions as the literature defines them; "fit for purpose".
4. The six kinds of nothing — zero, free, unavailable, unknown, not collected, suppressed — against what statistics and databases already distinguish: Rubin's missingness mechanisms; Hand's taxonomy of dark data; database nulls and three-valued logic; the official symbols and observation-status codes that statistics offices use for "not available", "suppressed", "negligible", "not applicable"; structural zeros; what imputation does and what a display owes the reader when a value is imputed.
5. Comparability: the European "coherence and comparability" dimension; definitional change, boundary vintages, revisions and the vintage of a number; like-for-like; comparator selection (nearest-neighbour models for places; area classifications); what a comparability declaration must contain.
6. Why aggregation hides the grassroots: skewed distributions and the difference between mean and typical; concentration measures (Lorenz, Gini, top-share, Herfindahl); the long tail and its critics; cumulative advantage; the studio's 78%/32% finding as the worked case (a Nightlife Culture finding, used as illustration, not researched); why a total is a statement about the biggest.
7. Digital-trace and transactional data as evidence: what distinguishes them from surveys and administrative data; the big-data paradox (a huge non-random sample is worth a small random one); algorithmic confounding and drift; the ten characteristics of big data; what this means for ticketing and streaming sources as evidence (method only).
8. Outliers: the statistical definitions and tests; robust alternatives; the artefact/signal distinction and how it is made in practice; positive deviance as a method; the small-denominator outlier (why the smallest places sit at both ends); anomaly as a finding to investigate before a value to trim.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** UK Statistics Authority Code of Practice (Quality pillar, Q1–Q3); OSR quality guidance; Eurostat ESS Quality Assurance Framework and the quality dimensions; Government Data Quality Framework (2020); HM Treasury Aqua Book (2015) and the Macpherson review (2013); Analysis Function guidance on communicating quality, uncertainty and change, and on symbols and shorthand in tables; GSS harmonised standards; ONS revisions policy and geography policy (boundary vintages); W3C PROV-DM; DCAT; the FAIR principles; Wang & Strong 1996 on data-quality dimensions; Wickham 2014 "Tidy Data"; Rubin 1976 and Little & Rubin on missingness; Hand 2020 *Dark Data*; Codd on nulls; SDMX observation status code list; Lorenz 1905; Gini; Anderson 2006 *The Long Tail* and Elberse 2008; Salganik, Dodds & Watts 2006 (MusicLab); Rosen 1981; Meng 2018 "Statistical paradises and paradoxes in big data"; Lazer et al. 2014 (Google Flu); Salganik 2018 *Bit by Bit*; Kitchin 2014 on data kinds; Hawkins 1980; Barnett & Lewis; Grubbs; Tukey 1977; Huber on robust statistics; Osborne & Overbay 2004; Pascale & Sternin on positive deviance; CIPFA nearest neighbours; ONS area classifications; Wainer 2007 (already in the ledger). Version 1's Chapter 3 (map; the Liverpool critique of what a pin is) and Chapter 8 (gap vocabulary; suppression at query time) for the studio's prior positions, re-verified where load-bearing. The prototype repository's data contract and the design system's evidence rules as Nightlife Culture standards.

**What I expect to find.** That the official world already has a precise vocabulary for most of the six kinds of nothing and that the studio's six map onto it imperfectly in instructive ways; that provenance is a settled standard with a settled minimum set and that dashboards routinely drop it; that the denominator and the unit of time are the two choices most often made by default; that "the long tail" is a distribution not a law and that concentration measures say more than a tail metaphor; that the big-data paradox is a canonical truth every transactional source must be read through; that outlier practice in the field is to trim and the evidence says to investigate; and that comparability is where honest dashboards most often go quiet.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) provenance, data quality and analytical assurance standards; (B) missingness, nulls and the official vocabulary of nothing; (C) grain, denominator, aggregation, concentration and digital-trace data; (D) outliers, robust statistics, positive deviance and comparability.

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 6 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch04-agent-returns.md`, 259,000 characters). Threads that were not in the frame and were followed:

1. **The Code of Practice is now edition 3.0** (published 3 November 2025). Q1–Q3 are Standards 6 "Be rigorous" and 7 "Be open about quality"; there are now separate *Standards for Public Use* that bind any public body using numbers in public ("cite sources"; no "undue certainty"); and the voluntary application route is a self-published TQV statement with no audit. Chapter 1 cited the old numbering. Flagged `[Revisit → Ch 1]`.
2. **"Reliability" means revisions.** The GSS defines it as "the closeness of early estimates to subsequent estimated values" and the ESS handbook files it under data revision. The failure register's "reliable" is the ordinary sense; the chapter now says both and asks the book to use one.
3. **A number has three dates.** DCAT 3 separates issued, modified and temporal coverage (a closed interval); the Philadelphia Fed and ALFRED keep vintages; FAIR A2 says metadata should outlive data. Together these make the heartbeat of Chapter 1 the least informative of three stamps. Handed to Chapter 8.
4. **The blank-cell rule is subtler than "never blank".** The Analysis Function permits a blank only where there is exactly one reason for it and the reason is stated above the table; otherwise shorthand on every cell. Found in the spreadsheet guidance, not the symbols page. The strongest single standard for the six kinds.
5. **Every official scheme splits "not collected" into *not applicable* and *not asked*** (AF [z]/[x]; SDMX M/L; Codd I/A-mark; Understanding Society −8/−10; CDC N/U; Eurostat "-"/":"); none has a code for "free" (the ONS treats it as zero price with non-zero quantity); SDMX has K/W for "included elsewhere"; the ABS had "n.y.a." The studio's six are therefore finer than the UK's in one place (unknown v unavailable both [x]) and coarser in two. A seventh-kind decision flagged for Carly.
6. **Codd 1986 and `coalesce(sum(x), 0)`.** The relational model's inventor proposed two kinds of null and condemned zero-for-missing; SQL-92 kept "effectively only one null value"; PostgreSQL's documentation explains the one-line idiom by which "no rows" becomes a displayed 0. The technical mechanism of the failure the brief names.
7. **The one official rule for drawing a gap** (Analysis Function charts guidance): never join across a missing point, "even if the line is dotted or dashed". The Decision Book's DAT-14 ("dashed, hatched, or broken") can be read to permit what the standard rejects — logged as a conflict.
8. **The midnight census is codified**, not accidental (KH03 and the NHS Data Dictionary), and the US literature finds it understates daytime load; the day-ward rule caps occupancy at 100% by definition. The official night time window (6pm–6am) straddles the calendar day — taken as a definition only.
9. **Regulation 696/93 defines eight statistical units** for a "business" (the first draft said seven; the verifier counted). A count of venues is undefined until the unit is named.
10. **Meng's identity and the 2.3 million ≈ 400 result**, driven by a defect correlation of about −0.005, with the paradox's exact wording ("the more the data, the surer we fool ourselves"). The chapter's single most important finding for the ticketing feed.
11. **Salganik's ten characteristics** (open textbook) as a ready-made audit for any feed; the 9/11 pager case as one unit producing a whole trend; Kitchin & McArdle's "exhaustivity" as the claim Meng dismantles.
12. **The streaming literature at two grains**: market-level concentration (Elberse's 78%/32% for Rhapsody; 91% of 3.9m tracks under 100 sales) against individual-level diversification (Datta et al.); and Aguiar & Waldfogel's "top 50" coverage caveat. Elberse's two headline figures happen to be the studio's two figures — noted in the chapter so nobody later mistakes one for a citation of the other.
13. **Gelman & Price: shrinkage flips the artefact.** Raw rates put small units at both ends; adjusted rates hand the top to large units; the funnel is the only picture biased toward neither.
14. **Dawson 2011: Tukey's fences flag ≥30% of normal samples of any size.** An automated alert on box-plot fences has a 30% false-flag floor by construction.
15. **Bakker & Wicherts: 41% of papers claiming no exclusions have degrees of freedom that do not match their N.** The only measured figure on silent removal; indirect, and the authors say so.
16. **Positive deviance has the same undeclared-cut-off disease as outlier removal** (Baxter 2016). Good-end and bad-end outliers need the same declaration.
17. **ESMA's APM guidelines and Tesco's LFL footnote** converge on the statistical comparability list (define; reconcile; comparators; constant; restate). Both regulators are European bodies; the independence is of audience, and the chapter says so after the cold read.
18. **PHE's circularity caution**: employment is 22.5% of IMD, so IMD-matched peers cannot benchmark employment. A general rule: the comparator model must not contain the outcome.
19. **DfE's 2025 statistical-neighbour model** is the only fully published UK comparator method found (23 variables, weighted Euclidean, ten neighbours); CIPFA's official page is a shrug.
20. **Juran's 1975 mea culpa**: the "Pareto principle" is misnamed by its populariser and its curves belong to Lorenz; the Juran Institute's current page does not repeat the confession.

Threads noticed and not pulled: Darwen & Date's full critique and Codd's RM/V2; Little & Rubin's textbook; the ESS QAF v2.0; Goldstein & Spiegelhalter 1996 on league tables; Zimek & Filzmoser on the history of outlier detection; the Retail Think Tank's critique of LFL; OECD inequality indicators (S80/S20, Palma); Coelho & Mendes' actual result; Calderon et al.'s "Time to move past midnight census" editorial; the Bank of England real-time database. All flagged or noted in the chapter.

### Sources read

About a hundred and thirty sources, listed in the chapter with how each was read and in `SOURCES.md` §8. Standards and official guidance dominate (the Code 3.0 pages; the Analysis Function's symbols, spreadsheets, charts and SDC pages; Eurostat's glossary and tutorial; SDMX code lists; the GSS geography and harmonisation pages; PROV, DCAT, FAIR; the AQuA Book 2015 and 2025; Macpherson; the NAO; ESMA; the CDC primer and legends; NCES; Understanding Society), nearly all at source. Seven abstract-only (†): Rubin 1976; Hájek 2007; Simon et al. 2011; Spiegelhalter 2005; Datta et al. 2018; Brynjolfsson et al. 2019; Coelho & Mendes 2019. Six secondary, each named: Pierce et al. 2024 (trade press); Lazer et al. 2014 (two agreeing teaching copies); Elberse 2008 (reprint); Juran 1975 (blog reproduction); Hawkins 1980 and Barnett & Lewis (as quoted in Aggarwal, Hodge & Austin and Osborne & Overbay). Anderson not read and not quoted; Huber not verified and not quoted; Marsh et al. 2004 partial. The orchestrator spot-checked thirteen items before writing, convenient ones first: Code 3.0 Standards 6–7 and seven practices; Meng's 2.3m ≈ 400 and the paradox wording; the MusicLab abstract; Dawson's 30%; the Analysis Function shorthand definitions, "No to NA" and the true-zero rule; the blank-cell rule; Elberse's 78/32 and 24%/91%; Bakker & Wicherts' 41%; Gelman & Price's abstract; Codd's zero passage; Hawkins via Aggarwal; the charts gap rule; the ONS CPI "zero price but with non-zero quantity"; Wickham's "surprisingly difficult". All thirteen held. The verifier re-traced ninety-five numbered items.

### What I learned

- Evidence is data made fit to bear a conclusion, with the fitness shown; the regulator's test is whether a number can "bear the weight of the decisions made using" it; verification (conducted correctly) and validation (the correct analysis) are different failures, and the paradigm case was purpose drift, not arithmetic.
- The five things a number needs — grain, denominator, provenance, the kind of each nothing, comparability — are each written down somewhere official, and a dashboard that shows none of them on the face is below the standard its own sources are held to.
- The grain is a judgement even for the author of the tidy-data paper; "business" is eight things in law; a day is a rule (midnight; 6pm–6am; five Fridays; 23 hours); the same market reverses between the track grain and the listener grain.
- A rate exists to compare unequal populations; the reference class is a choice; the smallest units fill both tails of a raw ranking and the fix hands the top to the largest; the funnel is the honest picture.
- The minimal provenance set is the same in the Code, the European law, PROV, DCAT and FAIR: responsible party; source and why; method; period as an interval; issued and modified; status; quality with error; what was withheld; what it may be compared with; and the record outlives the number. "Reliable" means revisions. No study measures whether any of this changes a reader's decision — the chapter's thesis sits beside a gap it names.
- The official vocabularies of nothing are richer than the studio's six and agree with their premise; the UK bans blanks with more than one meaning and bans "NA"; the database world kept one null and the idiom that prints it as zero; the only gap-drawing rule says never join; imputed values must be flagged with their author; free is a value; suppression and perturbation are two honest trades.
- A comparison is valid to the extent that the difference is in the true values; ten things must be the same or declared; finance's regulators ask for the same list; comparators are models and must not contain the outcome.
- A total is dominated by its largest terms; Lorenz, Gini, top-share and HHI measure the bow; 80/20 is a misnamed empirical pattern; cultural markets are skewed by imperfect substitution and joint consumption, and made more so by visible popularity; the long tail is ones and zeros.
- Size does not cure selection: quality × quantity × difficulty; 2.3 million ≈ 400 at ρ ≈ −0.005; platform data are by-products and drift; the ten characteristics are the audit.
- An outlier is a suspicion of a different mechanism; the standard tests assume shapes cultural data does not have and fire on ordinary samples; the field removes silently and in its own favour; the procedure is investigate, declare, show with and without; the good-end outlier is a hypothesis with the same disease plus the small-base caution.

### What I left out, and why

- Denominator neglect, the ecological fallacy, the modifiable areal unit problem and Wainer's league tables — assigned to Chapter 7; cross-referenced only.
- Anderson's *The Long Tail* — site blocked; not quoted rather than quoted from memory.
- Huber's one-line definition of robustness — unverifiable; Hampel's used.
- The Excel row-limit detail of the PHE case — not in the official statement; "a file-size limit in the loading pipeline" is what the source says.
- A "no report read as zero" dashboard anecdote — none found at source; the CDC dash and SDMX H are the official recognitions of the problem and stand in.
- The ticketing-vendor claim that a £0 ticket is an order — not found at source; treated as the studio's observation, consistent with the ONS method.
- Draft 1's "Users would like prominent, succinct quality information" (the source says "Some users have told us during our designation review that they would like…"); DCAT's "An absent value may indicate continuous updates" (the source is a three-way MAY clause); Salganik's "generally good/bad for social research" (the source says "generally helpful/problematic for research") — all restored to the source's words. Draft 1's "seven" statistical units and its "355 survey returns and 179 candidate attributes" — corrected. Six silent truncations given their ellipses or closed earlier. The Code History Database sentence an agent quoted could not be located — unquoted.

### Open questions

Whether "not applicable" is a seventh kind of nothing or a sub-kind; whether provenance on the face changes a reader's decision (no study found); the data contract's vocabulary against the chapter's mapping table (not re-read this sprint); the defect correlation of the ticketing feed (measurable only by the prototype); Hawkins, Barnett & Lewis, Tukey, Anderson and Lazer at source; the DCAT temporal definition's exact wording; the length of the 60-second version across chapters (the cold reader observed it is ~650 words — a cross-chapter format decision for Carly rather than a Chapter 4 fix).

### Looking back

Recorded in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's "aggregation of the biggest" (arithmetic, instruments, experimental mechanism) and its suppression paragraph (the full vocabulary; suppress or perturb); opens a thread for Chapter 1 (Code 3.0; Standards for Public Use); strengthens Chapter 2's denominator-as-persuasive-unit (it is the methodological unit too) and its "checkable numbers" rule (the provenance set and declaration are what checkable means); counterbalances Chapter 2's plain table (it needs the median and the share); strengthens and counterbalances Chapter 3's "nothing crucial behind a click" (the gap rule is a standard; twenty lines of qualification cannot all be on the face — the fact of qualification must be); weakens version 1's Chapter 8 gap vocabulary (kinds mixed with reasons; "collection break" is [b]; "community-held" is the studio's own and Chapter 10's) while confirming its rules; strengthens version 1's Chapter 3 on what a pin is (a grain); for Chapter 0, uses both proposed markings, proposes "secondary" as a third, and defines "Fixed as a stance" and "Fixed as a procedure" after the cold reader asked; and runs the agrees/conflicts/silent-on log against the Decision Book's Chapters 8 and 9 — agrees on almost everything it has; two conflicts (DAT-14's "dashed"; DTA-19's missing responsible party and dates); silent on the unit, the day boundary, the denominator, the funnel, the six kinds beyond suppression, "No to NA", zero and free as values, imputation flags, "reliable", the declaration, comparator method, concentration measures, the big-data paradox, outlier procedure and positive deviance.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, ninety-five items). Nothing fabricated; the quotations "overwhelmingly verbatim". Ranked mismatches, all corrected: three paraphrases inside quotation marks — the OSR sentence on users (restored to "Some users have told us during our designation review that they would like prominent, succinct quality information…"); DCAT's absent-value note (restored to the three-way clause); Salganik's grouping labels (restored to "generally helpful for research" / "generally problematic for research"). Two numbers wrong — Regulation 696/93 defines eight units, not seven (the local unit of homogeneous production was missed); Wang & Strong's 179 attributes came from a first survey of 137 people, and the 355 returns rated 118 attributes in a second — both corrected. The Blue Book 0.66pp figure was presented as separate from the pandemic 0.7 when it is itself pandemic-inclusive and the source's caveat was dropped — caveat restored. Six silent truncations inside quotation marks — Macpherson's "(Model SRO)"; OSR's "publications and articles to piece the story together"; CDC's "by law, statute, or regulation in the reporting jurisdiction"; Hand's "fewer Twitter users with fewer smartphones to tweet them"; OSR's "that is needed"; Eurostat's "break-free series" (not "time series") — all restored.
