One quotation unlocated (the Code History Database sentence) — unquoted. One definition unverifiable from the page (DCAT *dct:temporal*) — paraphrased and flagged. Pierce et al. dated to its online publication (late 2023). Leys's survey gloss ("every one used…") softened to what the verifier could confirm. The agent-return URL for the ONS error catalogue is dead; the live one noted in the ledger. Understanding Society's "not in this sample" was the chapter's label, not the source's — unquoted.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted whole: draft 1 applied the chapter's first truth to everyone's numbers and then closed the studio's 78%/32% worked case with "None of this weakens the finding" — the move the chapter condemns elsewhere. Draft 2 says the finding is undefined by the chapter's own standard until its unit, period and base are supplied, and flags the prototype to supply them. The symmetry test otherwise found warmth where findings flattered the studio: "in words the studio could have written" (Codd) cut; Darwen's three tables no longer "the six kinds … thirty years before"; the ESMA/Eurostat "independence" qualified (both European bodies; independence of audience, and a reading); the "free" rule and the ESMA rule now marked adjacent domain like the medical and psychological evidence; "accessibility regulation" corrected to guidance; the ten-characteristics audit re-tiered Adaptable (a textbook framework). Other changes: glosses added for the heartbeat, the aggregation of the biggest, the failure register, the sceptical reader, TQV, relvar, Blue Book, the Chapter 16 record; "Fixed as a stance / as a procedure" defined for Chapter 0; the row-count element of rule 1 argued in the text and marked Reading; "natural dates in display" given its sentence; the midnight "trough" claim corrected to what the trade press reports (midnight sits below the late-morning peak; the effect is 5.5%); "three unrelated authorities ban NA" corrected to two; the two "commonest rule" sentences disambiguated; "the only official rule" → "the one official rule found"; the 60-second version's Meng sentence tied to the measured case; the rules table's provenance source line completed; five record bullets that repeated the table cut; section 13's invented denominator and "lead with something else" removed, and its position on the blank small scene attributed to Chapter 1; "That is as far as this book goes on the night" reworded since the window recurs; unsupported superlatives ("most-cited", "clearest … dictionary", "coin toss") cut; "That is the whole chapter in a phrase", "Read that twice", "Declared is the word", "Substitute any platform" cut; the clothing metaphor reduced to one; the "not X; it is Z" construction reduced to one outside quotations; a filled-in ten-line declaration added to section 7 (about eighty words); the UK shorthand's own limit ([x] covers unknown and unavailable) stated in the mapping table; and the chapter's missing load-bearing wall — no evidence that provenance on the face changes a decision — named in section 5, flagged, and the provenance rules marked Standard (adopted) rather than Evidence. Not changed: the length of the 60-second version (consistent with Chapters 1–3; a format decision for Carly) and the checklist (the template asks for it).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` the data contract against the mapping table · a seventh kind of nothing · Hawkins and Barnett & Lewis at source · Pierce and Simon at source · Lazer at source · Elberse at source · Anderson · Huber · ESS Handbook §S.15 · CIPFA's parameters · seven abstract-only sources · DCAT temporal wording · the CHD sentence · does provenance on the face change a decision. `[Prototype flag]` measure the feed's defect correlation against a benchmark · count the nothings per source per month · the false-flag rate of the chosen alert on shuffled data · the 78%/32% as a Lorenz curve with unit and period stated · the provenance-panel probe. `[Validate with users]` which nothing reads as which (labelled v blank) · the provenance panel (which lines are opened and needed) · the seventh kind. `[Revisit → Ch 1]` Code 3.0; Standards for Public Use. `[Revisit → Ch 5]` DD-Type 11; precision; the composite's grain. `[Revisit → Ch 6]` the outlier as the start of finding value. `[Revisit → Ch 7]` ecological fallacy; funnels; the declaration as the method for comparing places. `[Revisit → Ch 8]` three dates v the heartbeat; not-yet; `coalesce`. `[Revisit → Ch 9]` harmonised standards v own questions. `[Revisit → Ch 10]` suppress or perturb; community-held. `[Revisit → Ch 11]` provenance on the face. `[Revisit → Ch 13]` the Analysis Function's three pages as adopted standards. `[Revisit at end]` suppress or perturb. `[Counterbalance → Ch 3 §9]` twenty lines of qualification v nothing behind a click. `[Counterbalance → Decision Book Ch 8]` DAT-14; DTA-19. `[Counterbalance → design system]` a chip set for the kinds of nothing.

### Rich references for Carly

**Meng (2018)** — one identity and one number that change how every platform feed is read; the first ten pages are enough. **Salganik, *Bit by Bit* §2.3** — the ten characteristics, each with a one-line summary, free online; the audit list for the ticketing feed. **The Code of Practice for Statistics 3.0, page 5** — Standards 6, 7, 9 and 10 on one page; the UK checklist of what a number must carry. **The Analysis Function's three pages** (symbols and shorthand; releasing statistics in spreadsheets; data visualisation: charts) — the codes, the blank-cell rule and the gap rule; the design system's empty-state vocabulary is here. **Codd (1986)** — twenty-five pages, and the sentence about zero. **Lorenz (1905)** — eleven pages; the curve in its inventor's words and his warning about averages. For building: **Gelman & Price (1999)** for why raw and adjusted rankings are both wrong; **the DfE statistical-neighbour technical report (2025)** as the template for a published comparator method; **ESMA's APM guidelines ¶20–41** as the shortest enforceable statement of what a defined measure must declare.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- A standards-heavy chapter needs the verifier as much as an experiments-heavy one: three paraphrases, two wrong numbers and six truncations survived four careful research agents and thirteen orchestrator spot-checks. Spot-checks catch fabrication; only a full re-trace catches truncation.
- Run the symmetry test on the studio's own findings *first*. Draft 1 held everyone's numbers to the first truth except the 78%/32%, and the cold reader found it in one read.
- Mark adjacent-domain evidence consistently — including standards from adjacent domains (price indices; financial reporting), not only experiments from medicine and psychology.
- When a chapter's thesis rests beside a gap in the evidence (here: whether provenance on the face changes a decision), say so in the body, not only in the flags, and tier the affected rules as Standard (adopted) rather than Evidence.
- Chapter 0 now owes four things: the two markings from Chapter 3; "secondary" as a reading mark; and definitions of "Fixed as a stance" and "Fixed as a procedure".
- Agent returns should carry URLs that the verifier can reuse; one agent URL was dead within the day (an ONS page moved). Record the live one in the ledger.
- Research agents should be told that "a quotation differing by one word between two reproductions" is itself a finding to report (Hawkins); Agent D did this unprompted and it was useful.

## Entry 5 · Chapter 5 — Building a measure

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 4 and the Part 0 reader. Second chapter of Part 1.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** When several things are counted and one number is wanted, what is actually being built — and what has to be shown with that number for it to be a measure rather than an opinion with decimal places?

**Sub-questions.**
1. What a composite indicator is, when one is warranted and when it is not: the OECD/JRC handbook's steps (conceptual framework → data → imputation → multivariate analysis → normalisation → weighting and aggregation → uncertainty and sensitivity → back to the data → links to other indicators → presentation); its own list of pros and cons; the standing of scoreboards, profiles and dashboards as alternatives to a single score; the argument about "mashup indices".
2. Normalisation, weighting and aggregation as claims: min-max, z-scores, distance to a reference; equal weights as a decision not an absence of one; weights from statistics (principal components, benefit-of-the-doubt), from experts (budget allocation, analytic hierarchy), from the public (stated preference; user-set weights as in the OECD Better Life Index); compensability and the implicit exchange rate between dimensions — the (21,1,1,1) = (6,6,6,6) illustration and Ravallion's "troubling trade-offs"; arithmetic against geometric aggregation and why the Human Development Index switched in 2010; non-compensatory methods.
3. Effective against nominal weights: Paruolo, Saisana and Saltelli's finding that published weights can be "little more than cosmetic"; Becker et al. on closing the gap; what a sensitivity and uncertainty analysis is, what it reports (how ranks move under alternative choices), and why the JRC audits other people's indices.
4. Goodhart's and Campbell's laws at source — the actual sentences, not the slogans — and the evidence that measures change the measured: targets and gaming in English public services (Bevan & Hood; Hood), "rewarding A while hoping for B" (Kerr), rankings and reactivity (Espeland & Sauder, already in Chapter 2), teaching to the test, Muller's *Tyranny of Metrics*; the taxonomy of ways a measure can be gamed; Hand's DD-Type 11 "Feedback and Gaming" (from Chapter 4).
5. Validity and reliability as the psychometric tradition defines them: construct validity (Cronbach & Meehl), Messick's unified view and its consequential aspect, the AERA/APA/NCME *Standards*, Borsboom's critique; convergent and discriminant validity; what "reliable" means here against Chapter 4's official sense (revisions).
6. Validation by recognition: respondent validation and member checking (Lincoln & Guba; Birt et al. on whether it is a tool or "a nod"); participatory and community-based indicator development (Fraser et al. "bottom up and top down"; Reed et al.; Bell & Morse); the evidence on whether co-produced indicators are more valid, more used, or merely more accepted; the studio's own position that a measure of a scene is valid only if the scene recognises itself in it — as a Nightlife Culture kind, tested against that literature.
7. How cultural and place value is currently measured, as published method only: the AHRC Cultural Value Project's components position; DCMS's Culture and Heritage Capital programme; Arts Council England's Impact & Insight Toolkit and the quality-metrics lineage; UNESCO's Culture|2030 indicators; UK and international place composites with public methods (Thriving Places Index, Social Progress Index, OECD Better Life Index, Legatum); the published methodology of any existing nightlife-sector composite, read only for what its method discloses (weights, parts, reproducibility) and never for its findings about places; the Centre for Cultural Value's stance on metrics.
8. Precision and false precision (carried from Chapter 3): significant figures and effective digits; what a decimal place claims; ranks against scores; rounding guidance; the difference between a measure's resolution and its precision.
9. The recipe on the face: what the handbook says about presentation and transparency; documentation lineages from adjacent domains — datasheets for datasets, model cards, the dataset "nutrition label" — as Practice; the Code of Practice's method-transparency practices (Chapter 4); interactive weight-setting as disclosure; the studio's rule that no composite cultural-value score exists without an adopted method, and what "adopted" must mean.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** OECD/JRC *Handbook on Constructing Composite Indicators* (2008) and the JRC Competence Centre's ten-step guide; Saisana, Saltelli & Tarantola 2005 (JRSS A); Paruolo, Saisana & Saltelli 2013 "Ratings and rankings: voodoo or science?" (JRSS A); Becker, Saisana, Paruolo & Vandecasteele 2017; Greco et al. 2019 review; Munda on non-compensatory aggregation; Ravallion 2012 "Mashup indices of development" and "Troubling tradeoffs in the Human Development Index"; UNDP HDR 2010 technical notes (the geometric mean); Böhringer & Jochem 2007; OECD Better Life Index methodology; Goodhart 1975/1984; Strathern 1997 "'Improving ratings'"; Campbell 1979 "Assessing the impact of planned social change"; Bevan & Hood 2006; Hood 2006; Kerr 1975; Muller 2018; Manheim & Garrabrant 2018; Koretz on test-score inflation; Cronbach & Meehl 1955; Messick 1989/1995; AERA/APA/NCME *Standards* 2014; Borsboom, Mellenbergh & van Heerden 2004; Lincoln & Guba 1985; Birt et al. 2016; Fraser et al. 2006; Reed, Fraser & Dougill 2006; Bell & Morse *Sustainability Indicators*; Crossick & Kaszynska 2016; DCMS Culture and Heritage Capital (2021–); Arts Council England Impact & Insight Toolkit documentation; UNESCO Culture|2030; Centre for Thriving Places methodology; Social Progress Imperative methodology; Gebru et al. "Datasheets for Datasets"; Mitchell et al. "Model Cards"; Holland et al. "Dataset Nutrition Label"; Ehrenberg 1977 (already in the ledger); Analysis Function rounding guidance. Version 1's Chapter 6 for the studio's prior positions (the recipe on the face; the exchange-rate refusal test "how many new venues compensate for one forty-year-old institution"; intensity not volume; the forbidden composite across identity and conditions) and the Artists Make Great Places framework's open form question, re-verified where load-bearing. The studio's Vibe Setting Framework is confidential: cited, never reproduced.

**What I expect to find.** That the handbook is candid about composites being as much art as science and that its own list of cons is the best argument for showing the recipe; that nominal weights are routinely not effective weights and that this is measurable; that Goodhart's actual sentence is narrower and more useful than the slogan; that the psychometric tradition already has the vocabulary the studio needs for "validation by recognition" (consequential validity; respondent validation) and a literature sceptical of it; that cultural-value frameworks mostly decline to produce a single score and say why; that the one nightlife composite in circulation does not disclose its weights; and that user-set weights are the strongest existing form of "the recipe on the face".

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) the composite-indicator method — handbook steps, normalisation, weighting, aggregation, compensability, effective weights, sensitivity, HDI, Better Life Index, precision and rounding; (B) Goodhart, Campbell and the evidence that measures change the measured; (C) validity, reliability and validation by recognition — psychometrics and participatory indicators; (D) cultural and place measurement frameworks' published methods, documentation lineages, and the form question (composite, scoreboard, profile).

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 6 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch05-agent-returns.md`, 204,000 characters). Threads not in the frame and followed:

1. **The 20%/21% and 40%/81% figures are the Times Higher Education ranking's, not the HDI's.** Version 1 cited them unattributed; Paruolo et al.'s Table 2 locates them (academic review 0.40/0.81; teacher–student ratio 0.20/0.21). The HDI is the well-behaved case in that paper.
2. **The JRC has revised the ten steps** since 2008 (weighting and aggregation split; "back to the data" gone). Two canonical lists circulate; the chapter cites the 2008 numbering for pages and the web guide for the toolbox, and the verifier found three normalisation and weighting sentences that are the web guide's and not the PDF's — corrected.
3. **A developer disowning importance.** The Global Innovation Index's audit records that since 2012 its weights "were to be used as scaling coefficients and not importance coefficients" — the belief the chapter tests, abandoned in the index's own report.
4. **Ravallion's price list is for the reformed index.** The $0.51/$9,000 figures are computed on the 2010 geometric-mean HDI; the reform moved the price rather than removed it. Draft 1's 60-second version said "before 2010" and the cold reader caught it. The UN's rejoinder — that implied substitution rates are not "values" — is recorded as a live dispute.
5. **Legatum's graded power means** (p = −1 at the top rising to 1 at the bottom) and **the IMD's exponential transformation** ("to reduce 'cancellation effects'") are two published place indices that changed their arithmetic to stop good parts hiding bad ones; the IMD is "specifically constructed to identify deprivation and not affluence", which raised the one-tailed question the chapter now asks of any cultural measure.
6. **Normalisation sets the exchange rate** as surely as weights do (Munda & Nardo's table); the chapter added a worked derivation — one year of operation exchanges for five events per month under equal weights and min-max over 0–40 and 0–200 — after the cold reader asked for one.
7. **Goodhart's law was a joke in a subordinate clause**; Strathern wrote the aphorism and credited Hoskin; Campbell's law is the one with evidence; Campbell's famous Chicago crime example is not in the 1976 paper. The canon is misquoted almost everywhere.
8. **The symmetry on targets.** Bevan & Hood's own acknowledgement of real improvement; Kelman & Friedman's "no evidence for any of the dysfunctional effects"; Propper's England–Scotland; Burgess's Wales league-table abolition; Bevan & Hamblin's "only in England has this target been met". All abstracts; said so.
9. **Manheim & Garrabrant's four variants** — three need no adversary. Lets the chapter describe proxy failure without implying bad faith.
10. **The ONS refuses to combine its four wellbeing questions** ("It is not appropriate to combine these questions") — the official precedent for a felt quality as a panel, cognitively tested.
11. **The *Standards* never mention face validity**, and draw a boundary on consequences (a consequence counts against validity only if it traces to a flaw in the measure). Recognition is face validity and credibility; the studio's single rule became two.
12. **The thinness of the recognition evidence.** Thomas: little evidence member checks improve findings; Reed: "little empirical evidence" for most participation claims beyond durability; Reed, Dougill & Baker: community indicators mostly valid, "not always sufficiently accurate or reliable". de Vente's trade-off: government-run processes earn less trust and more implementation.
13. **UNECE's September 2025 guidelines** — newer than anything in the frame — recommend "that composite indices should be accompanied by dashboards … that give information about the development in the underlying components" and define a dashboard as the form in which the reader "can (and indeed must)" do the deduction.
14. **The ACE quality metrics are a profile instrument by design** ("not a clapometer"; medians and interquartile ranges by respondent group); UNESCO's indicators are a longitudinal self-profile ("change over time in the same place, rather than … ranking").
15. **The sector composite's disclosure.** Fifteen criteria whose names change across editions; "proprietary algorithms"; no scale, weights or aggregation published; a headline (6.10) not equal to any simple mean of its parts (8.58, 5.90, 5.41). Draft 1 over-read this — see the cold read below.
16. **The ML documentation lineage** (datasheets; model cards; nutrition labels) as the form of "the recipe on the face", with Gebru et al.'s insistence that the document is "not intended to be automated" because its point is reflection.
17. **A fetch tool fabricated quotations** (Alkire 2008); Agent C caught it by string search and used nothing from the paper. Method finding.

Threads noticed and not pulled: Kane's argument-based validity; Nevo on face validity; the OECD subjective-wellbeing guidelines' validity chapter (blocked); the CES 2014 recommendations (blocked); Sébastien & Bauler on policy use of composites; Gilmore, Glow & Johanson on Culture Counts; Franco-Santos & Otley on unintended consequences; Böhringer & Jochem's meaningfulness formalism. All flagged.

### Sources read

About a hundred and twenty sources, listed in the chapter with how each was read and in `SOURCES.md` §9. The handbook (Part I at source; Part II via the JRC's web reproduction), the JRC audits, Paruolo, Munda & Nardo, Ravallion, the HDR, Legatum, the IoD, Saisana, Strathern, Campbell, Kerr, Bevan & Hood, Hood, PASC, Koretz, Cronbach & Meehl, Messick (ETS report), the *Standards*, Morse, de Vente, Sen, the AHRC, Knell & Whitaker, UNESCO, Stiglitz–Sen–Fitoussi, UNECE 2025 and the three ML papers at source. Thirteen abstract-only (†). Secondary: Goodhart (three agreeing reproductions); Borsboom; Lincoln & Guba; Thomas (reconstructed); Campbell & Fiske (fragments); the Better Life Index mechanics. The orchestrator spot-checked twelve items before writing, convenient ones first: Strathern's aphorism and Hoskin sentence; Campbell's law; Paruolo's "cosmetic" sentence and THES Table 2; the (21,1,1,1) passage; Ravallion's $0.51; UNECE §4.3/4.4/4.25; Knell & Whitaker's "clapometer"; the sector composite's scores and "proprietary algorithms"; Morse et al. 2002; the ONS "not appropriate to combine"; Bevan & Hood's definitions and spike. Twelve held; Thomas 2017 was unreachable. The verifier re-traced eighty-two numbered items.

### What I learned

- A composite is a model "on the basis of an underlying model" whose construction "owes more to the craftsmanship of the modeller"; three of its six documented risks are conditional on transparency; the handbook's own remedy is the recipe on the face.
- Weights are "essentially value judgements"; equal weights "does not mean 'no weights'" and weight uneven groups unequally; nominal and effective weights "are hardly ever found to match"; a weight can be "little more than cosmetic"; a developer under audit has stopped calling its weights importance.
- Aggregation is a price list: addition is fully compensatory, (21,1,1,1) = (6,6,6,6), the rate is computable in the parts' own units and is set by the normalisation and the weights together; the HDI's reform moved its price rather than removing it; the IMD and Legatum reach for non-additive arithmetic to stop good parts hiding bad ones; a cultural measure must decide which tail it is for.
- A composite audited over its alternatives "is no longer a magic number"; ranks carry intervals and are read within them; two effective digits is now official house style.
- The two "laws" are misquoted; Campbell's has the evidence; targets improved the measured thing in every rigorous comparison found and were gamed at the margin in every audit; three of four ways a proxy fails need no cheat; the independent check was never built and its absence is itself a kind of gaming.
- Validity is of a reading for a use, unitary, from five sources, including consequences that trace to a flaw; recognition is face validity and credibility, which the *Standards* do not count; the people measured have the first claim on what counts, which is legitimacy and not validity; community indicators are mostly valid, not always reliable, and best combined with field measurement; the ONS measures a felt quality as a tested panel it refuses to combine.
- The cultural-value literature prefers evaluation to measurement and declines a single score; the leading UK quality instrument is a profile; the newest official guidance says a composite travels with its dashboard; the one sector composite publishes its parts and not its recipe, and three of five place indices publish no sensitivity analysis.
- The recipe has nine lines; it is a card, not a tooltip; the card is for the builder as much as the reader; and "adopted method" has a definable content.

### What I left out, and why

- Any finding about any city from the sector composite — the brief; the chapter reads its method only and says so, and flags the placement of even that for Carly.
- Draft 1's claim that "no standard mean" recovers the sector composite's headline — withdrawn: a weighted mean, a power mean or indicator-level equal weights would; the finding is disclosure, not arithmetic. Draft 1's harmonic mean (6.26) — corrected to 6.37.
- Draft 1's "validity judgments are value judgments" (Messick) — not in the ETS report read; removed and flagged.
- Draft 1's "before 2010" for Ravallion's $0.51 — wrong; corrected to the 2010 index.
- Holden's "easy to measure" sentence as Holden's own — it is Holden quoting the Cabinet Office; corrected.
- The PASC 80% as material misstatement alone — the source says misstatement or undisclosed data weaknesses; corrected.
- The Doughnut as a profile exemplar — no sentence found at source saying it is not a score; dropped from the chapter.
- The Better Life Index's own methodology — unreadable; user weighting stated at secondary level and flagged.
- The Culture Counts critiques — unreachable; the delivery team's report is cited as such.

### Open questions

The form question, which is Carly's and which the closing chapter should set against every reader and rule; whether the sector composite's method audit belongs here or in Chapter 15; the one-tailed question; whether any cultural-vibrancy measure has ever been asked Borsboom's question; the OECD's validity conclusions on subjective wellbeing; the Better Life Index at source; whether decision-makers prefer composites (no evidence either way); Messick's missing sentence; Reed 2008 and Fraser 2006, which no verifier can reach though their agent string-verified them.

### Looking back

Recorded in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's "a published measure changes what it measures" with Campbell at source and the audited figures, and counterbalances it with the symmetry (real improvement in every rigorous comparison); strengthens Chapter 2 (the official reader's currency is monetary and now built; advocates and composites) and opens the form question as partly a reader question; strengthens Chapter 3 (rank intervals; "show the shape"; two effective digits now official); strengthens Chapter 4 throughout (provenance of parts; normalisation as denominator; the funnel and the rank interval as one discipline; discriminant validity against platform coverage) and opens "reliability" in three senses; weakens version 1's Chapter 6 on the unattributed THES figures and on its arithmetic inference about the sector composite, while confirming its recipe-on-the-face rule, its refusal test and its (21,1,1,1) illustration at source; opens threads for Chapter 0 (three senses of reliability; validity of a reading for a use; recognition as face validity and credibility); and finds the Decision Book silent on composites entirely — the rules table is the candidate chapter.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, eighty-two items). Nothing fabricated; the handbook, Paruolo, Munda & Nardo, Ravallion, the HDR, Legatum, Saisana, Strathern, Campbell, Bevan & Hood, Koretz, the economists' abstracts, Cronbach & Meehl, the *Standards*, Sen, the AHRC, UNESCO, Stiglitz and UNECE all held at source. Five corrections: the harmonic mean of the sector composite's parts is 6.37, not 6.26; the Social Progress Index's 2015 report lists 52 indicators, not 57; Messick's "validity judgments are value judgments" is not in the ETS report read (removed; flagged) and his consequential-aspect sentence was truncated (restored in full); the PASC "over 80%" covers misstatement *or* undisclosed data weaknesses (restored); Holden's "easy to measure" sentence is Holden quoting Kelly, Mulgan & Muers (attributed). Lesser: three normalisation and weighting sentences attributed to the 2008 handbook are the JRC web guide's (re-attributed); the GII "Seven economies" and Knell & Whitaker "self prior scores" quotations closed over deleted text (ellipses added); Kerr's "Nonetheless," restored; the IoD weights sentence describes the 2000 weights (stated as inherited); the CCV sentence is from the launch essay, not the principles (attributed); "both lists" became "six of nine pros and all six cons"; Borsboom's rendering matched to the secondary source; a Trochim phrase restored ("in reality") and a Campbell & Fiske sentence attributed to the paper that quotes it; an unverifiable face-validity definition replaced with Trochim's at source. Unreachable to the verifier: Reed 2008 and Fraser et al. 2006 (no open copy exists; their agent string-verified them), Birt's body text, Thomas's abstract — all marked.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted whole, and it was the chapter's most serious error. Draft 1 ran an arithmetic test on the sector composite — "no standard mean of the three published parts recovers the published whole" — that was false by the chapter's own section 5: a weighted arithmetic mean with about 15% on the top category gives 6.10, a power mean near p = −4 gives 6.09 (the family the chapter praises Legatum for), and equal weights over fifteen criteria in three uneven categories would not be recoverable from category means at all, which is the handbook paragraph the chapter quotes at greatest length. Draft 1 also applied the "recipe not on the face" verdict to that instrument alone while three of the five place indices in its own table publish no sensitivity analysis, and introduced the example as "unusually clean". Draft 2 rewrites the test fairly, states the finding as disclosure (a reader cannot reproduce the number, so cannot see the price list) and not arithmetic, applies the sensitivity verdict to all four, and removes the framing. Other changes: the HDI timeline corrected ($0.51 is the 2010 index; the reform moved the price); "or 9%" in belief three corrected to 21%; "because nobody built the audit" unquoted (it was our phrase); the "ledger" promised in the preamble connected to version 1's list in section 11; the Thriving Places Index no longer called a public body; "nine hospitals" → nine NHS trusts; a misused `[Validate with users]` flag removed; "Fixed as a stance" on a Standard source re-tiered Fixed; the studio's first-claim rule re-described (Nightlife Culture + Reading, with the environmental evidence saying how, not whether); Cronbach & Meehl no longer "the recipe on the face, said in 1955"; the refusal test marked as a values test, not a method test, and section 15's "the aggregation is wrong" reworded to "does not express the studio's judgement"; the chains-versus-grassroots example replaced with a neutral one; "the first candidate … is the scene" softened to one candidate; Reed, Dougill & Baker no longer quoted verbatim in the summary and truth 6 (its abstract was reconstructed); the symmetry finding's abstract-only status stated in the summary; glosses added (heteroskedastic, synecdoche, LSOA, main effect's estimation, the Trochim face-validity wording); "Paruolo's option (b)" named; "Juran-style reproductions none" and "preference-independence passage" removed; the Doughnut source dropped; "honest" cut from seven uses to two; "The sentence that should be printed on every index" and "the recipe on the face, said in 1955" cut; four record bullets that repeated the table cut; a worked derivation of a price list added to section 5 and a paragraph on the one-tailed question's arithmetic, both asked for. Not changed: section 2 (the template asks for the beliefs); the checklist (the template); the length of the 60-second version (a cross-chapter decision for Carly, raised in Entry 4).

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Run the chapter's own rules against its own audits before dispatch.** Draft 1 judged a sector instrument by an arithmetic test the chapter's own section 5 refutes, and judged it alone. The symmetry test must be applied hardest to the instrument the chapter is most tempted to judge.
- A handbook and its web guide are two texts; cite the one the sentence is in.
- When a research agent reports that a fetch tool fabricated quotations, record it and ask every agent to string-verify body-text quotations whose wording matters. Agent C's practice should be in the agent brief.
- Abstract-only evidence that supports a conclusion the chapter likes (the symmetry on targets) needs its status stated in the summary, not only in the sources.
- The 60-second version's length is now raised twice (Entries 4 and 5); it is Carly's call and should not be changed chapter by chapter.
- Chapter 0 owes, in addition to Entry 4's four items: reliability in three senses; validity as of a reading for a use; recognition as face validity and credibility.

## Entry 6 · Chapter 6 — Finding value in the data

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 5. Third chapter of Part 1.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** Before anything is scored, how does one ask of a body of data "how is value created here, and for whom?" — and which of the methods that answer that question can be trusted, at what grain, with how many cases, and against what standard of proof?

**Sub-questions.**
1. Revealed preference: what people's movements and choices show that their answers do not — who comes, from where, how often, how far, whether they return. The travel-cost method and its assumptions; distance decay and gravity models; catchment analysis; repeat visitation and retention measured as survival; the known biases of each (who is counted when movement is the record; Chapter 4's platform caveat).
2. Diversity and access as measurable properties: the general framework for diversity (variety, balance, disparity — Stirling; the ecologists' indices and their critics); programme breadth; price, time and "welcome" as dimensions of access, and how audience research measures belonging; the difference between an audience and a public.
3. Longevity and resilience: survival analysis and business-demography survival rates as method; the concept of resilience (engineering, ecological, adaptive) and how regional economists have operationalised it; red flags of vulnerability — early-warning indicators as a method, their false-alarm rates, and the hollow success: every standard marker met and the construct absent (Chapter 5's construct underrepresentation; Scott's legibility; the measure of a place against the place).
4. Value created and value extracted: who captures the value a place generates — the economists' distinction between creation and extraction (Mazzucato); rent and land value; displacement, measured (the gentrification literature's methods, not its findings about places); additionality, deadweight, displacement and leakage as the appraisal guide defines them; distributional analysis in the Green Book; social return on investment and its principles and critics.
5. Claims about cause: what "because of us" may honestly mean. Pearl's ladder (association, intervention, counterfactual); Bradford Hill's considerations; the Magenta Book and contribution analysis on attribution against contribution; the claim ladder version 1 drew for "origination" (a Nightlife Culture position: the strong claim with its boundary, the weaker claims visibly below) tested against that literature; what a dashboard may say at each rung.
6. Pattern discovery as method: exploratory data analysis (Tukey) and knowledge discovery; the garden of forking paths and the multiple-comparisons problem; spurious correlation; pre-registration and holdout as disciplines; how an exploratory finding is promoted to a confirmed one; "all models are wrong"; the two cultures of modelling; the illusion of progress in classifiers.
7. Anomaly detection as signal (carried from Chapter 4): statistical process control and control charts; surveillance methods (CUSUM; funnel plots; the Bristol and Shipman monitoring); false-alarm rates and the cost of alerting; the contextual anomaly; positive deviance as the good-end search.
8. Small-area and small-n statistics: the law of small numbers; de Moivre's equation and "the most dangerous equation"; small-area estimation (Fay–Herriot; the ONS's methods); shrinkage and borrowing strength (James–Stein; Efron & Morris); what shrinkage does to the small place (Chapter 4's Gelman & Price); when to show a small-n figure, when to show an interval, when to say nothing.
9. How an algorithmic assumption becomes a canonical truth: validation against ground truth; cross-validation and out-of-sample tests; replication; the assumptions log (Chapter 4's AQuA Book); documenting the model (Chapter 5's card); the standard of evidence at which a pattern found in the data may be called a finding about the world.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Clawson & Knetsch and Hotelling on the travel-cost method; Haab & McConnell; Tobler's first law; Huff and Reilly on gravity and catchments; Stirling 2007 "A general framework for analysing diversity"; Hill numbers / Jost 2006; Audience Agency and Arts Council audience research methods; Kleinbaum or Cleves on survival analysis; ONS business demography methodology; Holling 1973; Martin & Sunley 2015 on regional resilience; Kaminsky–Reinhart early-warning indicators and their critics; Scott 1998 *Seeing Like a State*; Mazzucato 2018 *The Value of Everything*; Freeman 2005 and Ding et al. on measuring displacement; HCA/MHCLG *Additionality Guide*; HM Treasury Green Book 2022 distributional appendix; Social Value UK SROI principles and Arvidson et al.'s critique; Pearl & Mackenzie *The Book of Why*; Hill 1965; HM Treasury Magenta Book 2020 and Mayne on contribution analysis; Tukey 1977 EDA; Fayyad et al. 1996 KDD; Gelman & Loken 2013; Benjamini & Hochberg 1995; Nosek et al. 2018 on pre-registration; Box 1976; Breiman 2001; Hand 2006 "Classifier technology and the illusion of progress"; Shewhart/Deming via NHS SPC guidance; Spiegelhalter et al. 2003 on Bristol and Shipman monitoring; Tversky & Kahneman 1971; Wainer 2007 (ledger); Rao & Molina 2015; ONS small-area estimation guidance; Efron & Morris 1977; Gelman & Price 1999 (ledger). Version 1's Chapter 6 for the claim ladder and the dynamic census as Nightlife Culture positions; the studio's "hollow success" and "value created and extracted" framings as the brief states them.

**What I expect to find.** That revealed-preference methods are older and better theorised than their dashboard uses suggest, and that each carries an assumption about who is observed; that diversity has a general framework the sector does not use; that resilience is three concepts wearing one word; that "because of us" has a well-charted ladder and most dashboards speak from its top rung with bottom-rung evidence; that pattern discovery is where the forking paths are and that promotion to a finding needs a holdout; that surveillance statistics have solved the alerting problem the sector has not posed; and that small-area methods make the small place visible at the price of pulling it toward the average, which Chapter 4 already saw.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) revealed preference, diversity and access — travel cost, distance decay, catchments, retention as survival, Stirling's diversity, audience and belonging research; (B) value, cause and attribution — creation against extraction, displacement measured, additionality and the appraisal guides, SROI and its critics, Pearl's ladder, Hill, Magenta Book and contribution analysis; (C) pattern discovery, validation and anomaly as signal — EDA, KDD, forking paths, multiple comparisons, pre-registration, Box, Breiman, Hand, SPC and surveillance, CUSUM, Bristol and Shipman; (D) small-n and small-area statistics, longevity, resilience and the hollow success — law of small numbers, de Moivre, small-area estimation, shrinkage, survival rates, resilience concepts, early-warning indicators, Scott's legibility.

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 6 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch06-agent-returns.md`, 155,000 characters). No agent had web search for this sprint — the shared budget was exhausted before the first call — so every source was reached by direct address, and the scholarly web (Project Euclid, JSTOR, PNAS, PMC, Science, Taylor & Francis, Wiley, ScienceDirect, the IMF) proved closed to a fetch tool. The verifiable record of this chapter rests on AAAI, OUP abstracts, PLoS, arXiv, NIST, gov.uk, NHS England, DTIC, Eurostat, the ONS, the Census Bureau, Econlib, Scholarpedia, two authors' personal sites, a Swedish course mirror, a McGill course folder, a libcom scan and a joke site. Threads not in the frame and followed:

1. **Two UK method texts changed under us.** The gov.uk address that carried the Magenta Book 2020 now serves a May 2026 edition; the Green Book was reissued on 5 February 2026, "radically shorter and simpler", with "evaluation" dropped from its subtitle, the quintile weights table replaced by a formula ((national median ÷ group median)^1.3) and a new requirement that weighted estimates be presented "alongside unweighted estimates". The 2020 and 2022 texts are withdrawn and their archived copies returned 403. Any chapter citing "Green Book 2022" from the live link is citing the wrong document. `[Revisit → Ch 2]`
2. **The Additionality Guide is withdrawn (24 May 2022) and still hosted**, and nothing has replaced its definitions; the chapter uses it as the definitional text and says so.
3. **The ONS's "footfall" is already phone data** — BT Active Intelligence on the EE network, 24 million devices, residents and workers excluded — and its card-spend indicator comes with the Revolut caveat in the ONS's own words. Chapter 4's platform caveat now has the national statistics office as its instance.
4. **The Audience Agency entered liquidation on 10 September 2026.** Audience Spectrum and Audience Finder, the sector's yardsticks for "the audience against the public", have an uncertain custodian. `[Revisit → Ch 15]`
5. **The Participation Survey's 91%** rests on a list that includes reading; the response rate is 25%. A gov.uk listing shows a "Community and Engagement Survey 2025/26" that may be its successor — not verified.
6. **Benhamou & Peltier's supplied-against-consumed diversity** maps onto programme against audience: a two-sided diversity measure.
7. **Business deaths are not final until the third year** because of reactivations — a closures indicator is provisional for two years.
8. **Easton et al.'s tripartite confound** — involuntary displacement, voluntary outmigration, incumbent uplift — which any mobility indicator conflates; and the discovery, from the verifier, that one of the sentences the chapter had credited to them is their quotation of Baeten et al.
9. **"Displacement" is two words**: the appraisal guides' economic displacement (a deduction) and the urban literature's residential displacement (people). The cold reader caught the chapter using one for both.
10. **Hill's "glitter of the t table"**, and his closing licence to act on incomplete knowledge.
11. **Granger does not dispute "it is only Granger causality"**; the OECD's "leading" is earned by a turning-point procedure the chapter could not read. The studio's two-track "dynamic census" became a hypothesis display.
12. **Fayyad et al. call blind pattern-search "data dredging" and "dangerous"** in the founding paper of their own field.
13. **Google's holdouts "wear out"**, and Hand's nonstationarity — the two reasons a dashboard cannot be its own test and "validated" carries a date.
14. **Box's aphorism is a section heading** in the 1979 report and absent from the 1976 paper it is cited to.
15. **The AQuA Book 2025 has no instruction to validate a model against outturn**; its companion defines validation as "the agreement of those judged competent". The verifier qualified the negative finding with the Book's provisions on data drift and calibration, and the chapter now says so.
16. **The NHS run rule is "between seven and nine … you should decide"**, and NIST's average run length (≈371) puts a number on the false alarms of a correctly set chart.
17. **Spiegelhalter's Bristol and Shipman abstract is hedged three times** ("could have"; "depending on the data source and the comparator"; "might have been useful").
18. **About 40% of MSOAs in England and Wales have no respondents at all** in the income survey; the ONS warns that its modelled intervals "should not be used to measure change" — a direct prohibition on trend arrows for modelled small-area figures.
19. **SAIPE states the shrinkage weight in one sentence** (sampling variance ÷ total variance); Gelman's Lac Qui Parle County (two observations, "an implausibly high estimate") is the compact example; Efron & Morris 1975 carries the baseball case in the authors' words where the 1977 magazine article was unreachable.
20. **Official rules withhold small numbers for two reasons** — confidentiality (NHS 1–7; Analysis Function 2/3/5) and precision (OPN base > 10; ASHE CV bands) — and one ASHE table colours both the same.
21. **Scott's forestry failed "in the second generation"**: the hollow success is partly a timing problem.
22. **The fetch tool's 125-character quote cap.** Agent D's reader stated outright that it operates under a 125-character quote limit and offers to paraphrase; on a first pass it merges or reshapes sentences to fit. This, not fabrication, produced the Alkire episode recorded in Entry 5; a YES/NO presence prompt defeats it. Entry 5's "fabricated" should be read as "reshaped by the reader's cap".

Threads noticed and not pulled: Tukey's exploratory/confirmatory in his own words; Tversky & Kahneman's opening; Holling's definitions; Hotelling's letter; Kaminsky's signals approach and noise-to-signal ratio; Tuckman & Chang's four criteria; Geurs & van Wee's four components; the Warwick Commission's 8%; Nosek's prediction/postdiction; the Open Science Collaboration's 36%; Rao & Molina; Gelman & Hill on partial pooling; Fujiwara's SROI reply; Stiglitz on rents; the OECD turning-point method; a commercial-displacement method; positive deviance at small n; funnel plots (Chapter 7); survival analysis for organisations in the peer-reviewed literature. All flagged.

### Sources read

About a hundred and ten sources, listed in the chapter with how each was read and in `SOURCES.md` §10. At source and string-checked: Fayyad, Pearl, Fujiwara & Campbell, the EPA guidelines, the ONS (TTWAs; mobile data review; real-time indicators; business demography and QMI; income QMI; fuel poverty; OPN; ASHE), Clark et al., Wesolowski, Stirling, TfL, Ipsos, McMillan & Chavis and Peterson et al. (author's site), DCMS (Participation Survey; Taking Part), the Audience Agency, Eurostat (survival; small-area guidelines), Walker et al., Martin & Sunley (working paper), Scott (libcom scan; Cato Unbound), the FDA, Fleming & DeMets (abstract page), Ricardo, Mazzucato & Ryan-Collins, the Additionality Guide, the Green Book 2026, the Magenta Book 2026, the SROI Guide and SVI, Easton et al., Hill, Mayne, Scholarpedia (Granger), the OECD CLI page, COS, Gelman & Loken (both versions), Benjamini & Hochberg, the ASA release, Vigen, Goodfellow et al., Google MLCC, Ioannidis, Box 1976 and 1979, Breiman, Hand, the AQuA Book, Glover, NHS Making Data Count, NIST, Salmon et al., Wainer (2009 chapter), SAIPE, Efron & Morris 1975, Gelman 2006, NHS England disclosure, the Analysis Function. Abstract-only (†): Hernán, Arvidson, Freeman, Spiegelhalter, Kaminsky, Berg & Pattillo, Tuckman & Chang, Jost, Hill 1973, Benhamou & Peltier, Poor & Smith, Huff, Geurs & van Wee, Martin 2002. Secondary: Tobler and Hotelling (Wikipedia); Mazzucato (blurb); the forester within Scott; Freeman's method (Easton); Baeten (Easton). Metadata only: Yule, Granger & Newbold, Ding et al., Bedate et al., Holling. Unreached: seventeen works named in the chapter's flags.

### What I learned

- Revealed preference is older and better theorised than its dashboard uses: distance is a price under four stated assumptions; the method "only measure[s] use-value"; a visitor who has not returned is censored, not lost; and the national footfall figure already carries the platform caveat Chapter 4 warned of.
- Diversity has three properties and every index is a weighting of them; supplied and consumed diversity differ; access can be scored and the scorer names the blind spot; welcome has an instrument, validated for neighbourhoods; the public, by the national yardstick, is almost everyone.
- Survival is a cohort figure with a two-year revision lag; resilience is three concepts; early-warning systems have a 24-month window and a limited out-of-sample record; the hollow success is legibility in Scott's sense and the unvalidated surrogate in medicine's, and it is partly a timing problem.
- Value created and extracted: Ricardo's arrow; every activity figure is gross until the Additionality Guide's deductions are shown; distributional analysis is weighted beside unweighted with the weight on the face; SROI's own guide forbids comparing its ratios; residential displacement is measurable only as proxies that cannot tell pushed from chose from stayed; and "displacement" is two words.
- "Because" lives on a ladder; a dashboard is a rung-one object whose verbs are alongside, after and where; "contributed to" is a second-rung verb earned by a theory of change whose alternatives have been tested; Hill's viewpoints are questions and a licence to act; "leading" is measured, not declared.
- A pattern found is a hypothesis with a date; the garden forks even for the scrupulous; the error-control choice is the designer's and neither option makes every highlight real; holdouts wear out; a canonical truth is two tests, out-of-sample and the agreement of the competent, and the described are among the competent.
- Two points tell you nothing; the data sets the limits; the run rule is a policy; a correct chart false-alarms once in 371 points on one rule; anomalies are seasonal; surveillance claims state comparator and boundary.
- Small places sit at both tails; "small" is a precision property; two in five small areas have no respondents; the sound estimate borrows strength and is shown doing so; a modelled interval cannot carry an arrow; an asterisk means secret or noisy and must say which; a complete count's interval comes from its history.
- Beneath every statement of value, a ten-line claim record, most of whose lines will say "not done".

### What I left out, and why

- Any wording of Tukey, Tversky & Kahneman, Holling, Hotelling, Nosek, the Open Science Collaboration, Efron & Morris 1977's body, Kaminsky's signals approach, Tuckman & Chang's criteria, Geurs & van Wee's components, the Warwick 8%, Rao & Molina or Gelman & Hill — unreached; nothing quoted from memory.
- Draft 1's "contributed to" as a first-rung verb — withdrawn; it is second-rung and conditional. Draft 1's pass of the studio's claim ladder on Hill's fundamental question — withdrawn; the main alternative (the trip was coming anyway) was unaddressed, and the repair is a stated-preference question that must be marked as one.
- Draft 1's glosses on Gelman's county ("most dangerous place in the state"; "radon capital") — cut to the source's "implausibly high estimate". Draft 1's invented SROI ratio in quotation marks — cut. Draft 1's "first sentence" of the Additionality Guide — second sentence; corrected. Draft 1's Arvidson wording — corrected from the Lund record. Draft 1's Baeten-via-Easton attribution — corrected. Draft 1's Tuckman & Chang criteria from general knowledge — cut.
- Draft 1's statement that the 2022 Green Book published a quintile table — unreached; restated as the brief's expectation.
- Draft 1's "England's small areas" — England and Wales. "Three weeks" — four.
- The Plochmann name within Scott — the verifier could not confirm it; the chapter says "a forester whom Scott quotes".
- Fleming & DeMets' middle sentence, Box 1979's report wording, Freeman's method from Freeman — single-source; marked or replaced.

### Open questions

Whether the book adopts the Additionality Guide's gross-to-net sequence as a house standard given that the guide is withdrawn; whether "two tests" becomes the book's definition of a canonical truth (Chapter 0 currently leaves it to the reader); who the competent are for the prototype's patterns; the identifying threshold a scene's own knowledge sets; the error-control choice for highlighting; the alert policy's two numbers; the sense of resilience; the retention window; how long a pattern waits; whether a shrunk figure is recognisable to the people it describes; a method for commercial displacement; the OECD turning-point procedure; the seventeen unreached works.

### Looking back

Recorded in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's reactivity truth with Scott's forest and counterbalances it (the measure changed the thing, nobody gamed, the failure took a generation); strengthens Chapter 2's official-reader finding and opens the 2026 edition thread; strengthens Chapter 3's time-series and uncertainty rules with the control chart, the seasonal baseline and the no-arrow rule; strengthens Chapter 4 at four points (platform caveat; censoring; outlier procedure; parameter-level provenance), counterbalances its outlier rule at small n, opens the reference-period thread; strengthens Chapter 5 (surrogate grading for the card; Wainer behind the one-tailed question; the symmetry test run on the studio's own forms) and counterbalances its recognition rule with shrinkage; strengthens version 1's forms and weakens their labels (claim ladder; dynamic census; ledger); opens threads for Chapter 0 ("reconstructed"; "Fixed as a procedure"; the definition of a canonical truth). The Decision Book agrees in Chapters 5, 6, 8, 10 and 15 and is silent on the rest; the rules table is the candidate chapter on claims about value.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, about 250 items, presence-check method throughout). The large majority held. Seven substantive mismatches, all corrected: a sentence credited to Easton et al. that is their quotation of Baeten et al.; Arvidson's abstract misquoted where the Lund record carried the correct text; glosses on Gelman's county that the paper does not support; the Additionality Guide's definition called its "first sentence"; the AQuA Book's negative finding stated more strongly than its §5.3 and §9.5 allow, and a source typo silently corrected; Stirling's first sentence in the working paper's wording rather than the article's; Box 1979's body wording unresolved between the garbled report scan and the published chapter (the chapter's wording now used, and said). Also: an invented SROI ratio in quotation marks; the 2022 Green Book table asserted about an unreached text; Tuckman & Chang's criteria from general knowledge; the SROI principles' wording attributed to the Guide when it is the site's; "the night that didn't originate" for "what the night didn't originate"; "wears out" for "wear out"; a dropped symbol in the Eurostat fragment; fourteen for seventeen; three weeks for four; England for England and Wales. Fourteen single-pass markers lifted because the verifier confirmed the sentences (the Magenta Book's conditions; "Not adequate"; the SROI displacement sentence; Mazzucato & Ryan-Collins; COS; the ASA's 2, 5 and 6; Box's mice and tigers; Breiman's 2%; Hand's nonstationarity; Glover; NIST's 3σ; the NHS rules; Salmon et al.; Efron & Morris's efficiency). Lac Qui Parle County confirmed in Minnesota from the paper. Nothing was fabricated.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted whole: draft 1 licensed "contributed to" from observational data alone, in the rules table, the fifth truth and the checklist, while section 8's own close placed it on the second rung and the Magenta Book it quoted required "Ruling out of alternative causes in order to claim impact"; and it banned "associated with" on a reconstructed abstract while handing out the softer causal verb free. Draft 2 moves "contributed to" to the second rung everywhere, gives the first rung its own verbs (alongside, after, where), and makes the Hernán rule an Adaptable default about stating the question rather than a ban on a phrase. Its second objection was also accepted: the studio's claim ladder was passed on Hill's temporality and fundamental question too easily — a ticket preceding a journey does not show it preceded the decision to travel, and the main alternative (the trip was coming anyway) was unaddressed; the repair, a survey question, is stated preference about a counterfactual and is now marked as such. Also accepted: "displacement" used in two senses as one (split throughout); the count of tests for a canonical truth drifting between one, two and four (now two tests and a two-line record); Ricardo's arrow applied to a scene as if shown (now the chapter's reading, with the empirical claim handed to section 8's rules); the illustration's slips into answering (the rent sentence; the "contributed to" awarded before the test; a threshold fixed; "nothing is ranked"); the identifying threshold said to be "lower" in one place and "higher" in another; the hollow success and anomaly absent from the seven truths (folded into the third and sixth); kind and tier labels that disagreed between truths and rules (NHS guidance is now Practice; the claim record and the common-cause rule are "Fixed as a stance"; the two-tests rule split into its Evidence and Nightlife Culture parts); three Fixed rules with no checklist item (added); a reader's objection that nine venues are a count, not a sample (a new subsection); the 371 figure covering one rule and assuming a long normal series (said); the deductions list varying from three to five (now four plus multipliers throughout); terms used before definition (a glossary paragraph in "Before you start"); no worked example (a filled claim record added to section 12); process voice ("the agent's first reading"; "not string-checked") removed from the teaching prose where the verifier had confirmed the sentence; a dozen flourishes and "not X; it is Y" tics cut; six sentences that explained the sector to itself cut. Not accepted: that all forty-three rules being Fixed in some form makes the tier uninformative — the tier distinguishes Fixed from "Fixed as a stance" and "Fixed as a procedure", which is the distinction Chapter 0 set up, and several rows carry Adaptable parameters; and that the 60-second version is too long — raised in Entries 4, 5 and now 6, and Carly's call.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` seventeen unreached works · the 2026 editions of the Magenta and Green Books against the editions earlier chapters cite · fourteen abstract-only and five secondary sources · commercial displacement · the OECD turning-point method · positive deviance at small n · survival analysis for organisations · the Audience Agency's successor arrangements. `[Prototype flag]` the deadweight question, marked as stated preference · measure the lead · count the comparisons · date the patterns · set and print the alert policy · shrink and show. `[Validate with users]` recognition in a shrunk figure · the identifying threshold · who the competent are · the welcome instrument's wording. `[Revisit → Ch 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 9, 10, 11, 13, 15]` as listed in the chapter. `[Revisit at end]` the gross-to-net arithmetic as a house standard · the two-test definition of a canonical truth. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1; Ch 4 §outliers; Ch 5 §recognition; version 1 Ch 6; Decision Book Ch 15]`.

### Rich references for Carly

**Hill (1965)**, the open JRSM reprint — six pages; the nine viewpoints, the caveat, the glitter of the t table and the licence to act. **The HCA *Additionality Guide* (2014)** — the whole gross-to-net vocabulary and the ready-reckoner warning in one official document, withdrawn and irreplaceable. **Easton, Lees, Hubbard & Tate (2020)** — every quantitative displacement method with its failure mode, open access, and the tripartite confound in one sentence. **NHS *Making Data Count* (2019)** — the two-point fallacy, common and special cause, the limits and the rules in plain English; the single most useful practitioner document this book has found for a live dashboard. **Gelman & Loken (2013/2014)** — the garden, in two open versions. **Hand (2006)** — why the clever model's advantage is mostly illusory, with numbers. **Eurostat (2019)**, *Guidelines on small area estimation* — one PDF for the whole small-area question. **The Government Analysis Function's disclosure-control policy store** — the UK rulebook for small cells, with reasons. **Scott, *Seeing Like a State***, introduction and chapter 1 — legibility and the forest. **Fujiwara & Campbell (2011)** — the UK's own plain statement of revealed against stated preference. **Stirling (2007)** — variety, balance, disparity. **Peterson, Speer & McMillan (2008)** — the eight-item welcome instrument.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **The fetch tool's reader has a ~125-character quote cap and will merge sentences to fit it.** This is the mechanism behind Entry 5's "fabricated" quotations. Every agent now string-verifies by a YES/NO presence prompt; the verifier used the same method for 250 items and it held. Put it in AGENTS.md.
- **A sentence inside a source's own quotation marks needs its own attribution.** The Baeten-via-Easton error would have survived a presence check, because the string is present; only a reader of the surrounding page catches it. Ask verifiers to report whether a quoted sentence is itself a quotation.
- **A vivid gloss is a claim.** "The radon capital of Minnesota" was mine, not Gelman's. The verifier is right to treat the chapter's paraphrases of a source as checkable against it.
- **The symmetry test caught less than the cold reader did.** I ran it on the claim ladder and still passed the studio's form too easily. The next chapter's symmetry test should be written as a list of the standards the chapter has applied to others, each then asked of the studio's own positions in turn, before drafting.
- **Check the verb against the rung before the truths are written.** The "contributed to" inconsistency came from writing section 8's close correctly and the truths from memory of it.
- **Without web search, say so in the frame and expect a longer unreached list.** Seventeen works this sprint; the chapter is weaker for it and says so. Ask Carly whether a library pass is worth scheduling before Part 1 closes.
- Chapter 0 now owes: Entry 4's four items; Entry 5's three; and from this sprint "reconstructed" as a mark, "Fixed as a procedure" as a tier, and the two-test working definition of a canonical truth.
- The 60-second version's length is raised a third time; still Carly's call.

## Entry 7 · Chapter 7 — Comparison, time and intervention

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 6. Fourth chapter of Part 1.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** When a dashboard sets one thing beside another — this place beside that one, this month beside last, the year before a change beside the year after, what is beside what might be — what does each comparison honestly show, what does it hide, and what may be said from it?

**Sub-questions.**
1. Comparing places without a league table. What a ranking does to the ranked (reactivity; mission drift; the *Doing Business* post-mortem); why ranks are mostly noise at small differences (rank intervals — Chapter 5's; league-table critiques; the funnel plot as the alternative, which Chapters 5 and 6 both promised to this chapter); standardisation and risk adjustment — what it means to compare "like with like" (direct and indirect standardisation; expected against observed); similar-area comparators (statistical nearest neighbours; how the official local-statistics services choose peers); position within a distribution; partial orders and the honest "incomparable"; peer choice and its gaming; Chapter 4's ten-line comparability declaration applied; Chapter 6's shrinkage and small-n rules applied to comparison across sizes; version 1's identity/conditions split (identity never ranked; conditions compared through a gate) as a Nightlife Culture position, tested.
2. Change over time and seasonality. Decomposition into trend, season and remainder; the official practice of seasonal adjustment and what it assumes; calendar effects; same-period-last-year against moving averages, and the lag each introduces; base effects and the percentage change of a percentage; index numbers, rebasing and chain-linking; nominal against real; revisions and vintages (Chapter 4's); regression to the mean as the commonest false "change"; structural breaks and when a series is no longer one series; cohort against period; how long a series must be before a trend may be read (Chapter 6's run rules carried).
3. Tracking an intervention and its ripples. Before/after and its threats to validity (history, maturation, regression, selection); interrupted time series; difference-in-differences and the parallel-trends assumption, including what breaks it under staggered adoption; synthetic control and its requirements; regression discontinuity; matching; placebo and falsification tests; spillovers and displacement as a violation of "no interference" (Chapter 6's displacement); statistical power and the minimum detectable effect — what a small place can and cannot show; the Magenta Book 2026 on quasi-experimental designs; the evidence-grading scales used by the UK's What Works centres (method only); what each design may honestly claim (Chapter 6's rungs applied).
4. Scenario and prediction. The form "x policy change gives y benefit at z risk" and its honesty rules: appraisal practice (optimism bias; sensitivity and switching values; ranges not points — Green Book 2026); forecast evaluation (fan charts; calibration; scoring; the official forecasters' own evaluation reports); communicating uncertainty about numbers to the public (the experimental evidence on whether it costs trust); calibrated language (the climate panel's likelihood scale); scenarios as distinct from forecasts; model uncertainty (Chapter 4's AQuA Book; Chapter 6's known wrongness); prediction intervals that widen; the dashboard that shows a projection.
5. Reading market movement. What the movements a dashboard might show actually are — prices, counts, indices, search interest, transactions — and the disciplines for reading them: index construction and base periods; seasonally adjusted against raw; nowcasting with search and transaction data and its documented failures; leading, coincident and lagging indicators (Chapter 6's "leading is measured"); concentration and churn measures; survivorship; signal against noise at high frequency; the real-time indicator caveats the ONS already publishes (Chapter 6).

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Espeland & Sauder 2007 on rankings and reactivity; the World Bank's 2021 external review of *Doing Business* and the CEPR commentary; Goldstein & Spiegelhalter 1996 "League tables and their limitations"; Spiegelhalter 2005 "Funnel plots for comparing institutional performance"; the CIPFA Nearest Neighbours model and the ONS/LG Inform similar-authority methods; Fattore et al. on partial orders; ONS and PHE guidance on standardisation; Cleveland et al. 1990 (STL); the ONS seasonal-adjustment guidance and X-13ARIMA-SEATS; Hyndman & Athanasopoulos, *Forecasting: Principles and Practice* (open); Barnett, van der Pols & Dobson 2005 on regression to the mean; the ONS/ILO consumer price index manuals on index numbers; Campbell & Stanley 1963; Bernal, Cummins & Gasparrini 2017 on interrupted time series; Card & Krueger 1994; Angrist & Pischke; Goodman-Bacon 2021 and Callaway & Sant'Anna on staggered difference-in-differences; Abadie, Diamond & Hainmueller 2010 and Abadie 2021 on synthetic control; Lee & Lemieux 2010 on regression discontinuity; the Magenta Book 2026 quasi-experimental chapter; the What Works Centre for Local Economic Growth's Maryland Scientific Methods Scale; the Green Book 2026 on optimism bias and sensitivity; the OBR's forecast evaluation reports; Britton, Fisher & Whitley 1998 on the Bank of England fan chart; van der Bles et al. 2019 and 2020 on communicating uncertainty; Spiegelhalter 2017; the IPCC uncertainty guidance note (Mastrandrea et al. 2010); Tetlock & Gardner on calibration and Brier scores; Wack 1985 on scenarios; Choi & Varian 2012 "Predicting the present"; Lazer et al. 2014 on Google Flu Trends; the ONS faster-indicators programme; Hirschman–Herfindahl concentration. Version 1's Chapter 5 for the identity/conditions split, the place-centred default, the peer-choice idea and the "no view survives cropping as a ranking" rule — as Nightlife Culture positions to be tested, not assumed.

**What I expect to find.** That the case against league tables is strong and one-sided but that the alternative — the funnel plot, the peer group, the distribution — has its own assumptions (which expected value; which peers; who chose them) that a dashboard must declare; that seasonal adjustment is a model with choices, not a cleaning; that regression to the mean explains more "change" than any intervention; that the intervention designs form a ladder of their own with the counterfactual's credibility as the rung, and that most of them need more cases or more years than a place has; that the honesty rules for prediction are well developed in official forecasting and almost unused on dashboards; that communicating uncertainty does not cost trust as much as practitioners fear; and that "market movement" on a dashboard is usually an index whose base and adjustment are undeclared.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) comparison without a league table — reactivity, *Doing Business*, league-table critiques, funnel plots, standardisation, nearest neighbours, partial orders; (B) change over time — decomposition, seasonal adjustment, calendar effects, moving averages, index numbers, nominal/real, regression to the mean, structural breaks, series length; (C) intervention designs — threats to validity, interrupted time series, difference-in-differences and staggered adoption, synthetic control, regression discontinuity, matching, placebo tests, spillovers, power, the Magenta Book, evidence-grading scales; (D) scenario, prediction and market movement — appraisal honesty rules, forecast evaluation, fan charts, uncertainty communication, calibrated language, scenarios against forecasts, nowcasting and its failures, indices and base periods, leading/coincident/lagging, concentration and churn.

### Threads pulled

Four agents returned on 6 October (returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch07-agent-returns.md`, 271,000 characters). No agent had web search; everything was reached by direct address, and the official and open record proved richer for this chapter than the journals would have been — the UK's own appraisal, evaluation, statistical and forecasting bodies have written down most of what the chapter needed. Threads not in the frame and followed:

1. **The *Doing Business* panel's actual recommendation** — "Remove the aggregate index and country rankings" — and its reasons (arbitrary weights; aggregation "invites … external interference"), with the discovery that the panel made eleven recommendations and that the "small changes … large swings" sentence is its summary of earlier reviews. The successor, B-READY, dropped the aggregate score and kept quintile bands, which forced the chapter to say what a band is.
2. **The band distinction.** The cold reader found the chapter forbidding bands while recommending the ONS's "higher / lower / similar" and the funnel's "alert / alarm". Draft 2 distinguishes bands of position (quintiles: where a place stands among the others) from categories of statistical separation (how far a place is from its own expectation; they move with the place). The distinction is the chapter's and is marked as a reading.
3. **The chart standard against the statistical guidance.** The Analysis Function says "Always rank bars by value"; the SHMI page says "It is inappropriate to rank"; a UKSA board minute says comparability "should not be reduced to league tables". The funnel reconciles them: order as a reading aid, position against limits as the finding.
4. **The APHO rule that indirectly standardised ratios are compared with the standard**, not with each other — except under two conditions the verifier found the first draft had cut off. The SHMI's "inappropriate to rank" is given on its page without a reason; the chapter's inference is now marked as one.
5. **Three published UK peer methods** (CIPFA's forty metrics with distance and number unstated and similarity "a subjective process"; the ONS's k-means clusters and twenty nearest neighbours by Euclidean distance, added February 2025; the DfE's ten weighted neighbours with closeness labels and a stated assumption) — and the ONS's own three-word comparison text by median absolute deviation, which shows no rank.
6. **Self-chosen comparators** — measured only in executive pay, at abstract, with qualifications the verifier restored ("Although peers are largely selected based on characteristics that reflect the labor market…"; "particularly in firms outside the … S&P 500"; "Preliminary evidence"). The cold reader was right that draft 1 made this a verdict; draft 2 makes it a caution and lowers the gap's cost from red to amber.
7. **Partial orders** as the method for saying "incomparable", with the method's own limit ("When the number of incomparabilities overwhelms comparabilities…").
8. **The textbook and Eurostat disagree on turning points** (read them from the trend-cycle; withhold the trend-cycle's end-point); the ONS's practical compromise is the three-month rate.
9. **Eurostat's three-year floor and seven-year "short" for seasonal adjustment**, its admission that one new observation can revise several years, and the ABS's warning that adjusting a non-seasonal series manufactures a season.
10. **The ECB's base effect**, two definitions; the ONS's "arbitrary" reference date; the BLS's reason index points are not percentages; the ILO's five-year weight rule; the ONS's chain-linking twice a year.
11. **Hyndman's "misleading and unsubstantiated"** on minimum-sample rules of thumb and "no justification for the magic number of 30" — there is no number of observations before a trend may be read.
12. **Campbell and Stanley introduce before-and-after "as a 'bad example'"** in 1963, with eight named rivals.
13. **Bernal's "impact model a priori"** — Chapter 6's forking paths closed in advance — and the Cochrane floor of three points each side against Bernal's "no fixed limits" (admissibility against power).
14. **Parallel trends "cannot be tested"**, pre-trend checks are "not tests of whether parallel trends is true", and Roth's finding that passing the pre-test can make the bias worse; Goodman-Bacon's negative weights under staggered adoption, with his own caution that the design is not at fault.
15. **Abadie's six contextual requirements** as a checklist for any single-place comparison; the placebos in time and in space; the Magenta Book 2026 naming synthetic control as the answer to the place-selection problem ("Places are not selected for investment randomly…").
16. **Lee and Lemieux dispute the "only at the threshold" reading** that the Magenta Annex states as fact; both shown.
17. **The ladders.** What Works Growth's Maryland scale with its Level 3 floor and DiD capped at 3; EEF giving DiD four padlocks; Nesta's "will not evidence direct causality". Draft 2 attaches a verb to each rung (after; departed from its trend; contributed to; caused) and names the What Works scale as the one the UK applies to local growth, so the claimant does not pick the kinder ladder.
18. **The Green Book 2026's optimism-bias duty by the body's own record**, with the 2002 table as fallback and the discovery that the table covers costs and durations only; the switching value; "Risk: The effect of uncertainty on objectives."
19. **The Bank of England's fan chart** — built "without suggesting a degree of precision that would be spurious", recommended for removal by Bernanke in April 2024, still published to February 2026, gone from April 2026, replaced by a baseline and two scenarios introduced with a sentence on what they do not cover. Draft 1 said "abolished in 2024"; the verifier corrected it. The OBR still publishes its fan with a caveat that its width "does not take into account the current level of uncertainty".
20. **Communicating uncertainty costs little stated trust** (van der Bles 2020, at abstract: "only a small decrease … mostly for verbal uncertainty communication"); verbal probabilities are heard differently even with a key (Budescu, reconstructed; Wintle: 66% against 32% only when the number is bracketed in the text); the IPCC's two scales; the MoD yardstick that leaves gaps between bands.
21. **Calibration and sharpness** as separable virtues of a projection; the Brier score; the Futures Toolkit's "Scenarios are not predictions"; the Uncertainty Toolkit's even number of scenarios and "front load the important caveats"; "Projections are not forecasts"; Code of Practice 3.0's "placing undue certainty" named as misleading use.
22. **Nowcasting predicts the present** (Choi & Varian's own words); Google Flu "more than double" (Lazer reporting *Nature*); the ONS's fast indicators "should not be used to predict GDP on their own"; the Conference Board's "Historically" and "generally", and its own admission that the real-time leading index "rarely provided adequate signals".
23. **Concentration thresholds are American**; the CMA applies none; no official UK "churn".

Threads noticed and not pulled: usability of funnel plots and partial-order displays for lay readers; the OECD/Eurostat turning-point procedure; later staggered-DiD estimators (Sun & Abraham; de Chaisemartin & D'Haultfoeuille; Borusyak et al.); Rambachan & Roth's sensitivity bounds; the ONS's own minimum series length; UNECE's *Making Data Meaningful* on small-base percentages; a public-sector rule on pre-specifying comparators; the Mott MacDonald 2002 study itself. All flagged.

### Sources read

About a hundred and ten sources, listed in the chapter with how each was read and in `SOURCES.md` §11.
