At source and string-checked: Espeland & Sauder's open book chapter; the World Bank's statement, the External Panel Review and B-READY; the IoD 2019 FAQ; Goldstein & Spiegelhalter's typescript; Marshall & Spiegelhalter; Leckie & Goldstein; the C3 Monitor 2019 and its 2017 annex; the SHMI page; the UKSA minute; the Analysis Function's chart standard and its guidance on change; APHO briefings 2 and 3; the PHE funnel and standardisation guides; HQIP; the ONS mortality guide, clustering and nearest-neighbour methodology, Explore Local Statistics QMI, seasonal-adjustment page, retail QMI and bulletin, CPI manual and brief guide, crime QMI, TCSEW comparability, revisions policy, business demography and population projections; CIPFA and Oflog; the DfE neighbour model; Bruggemann & Carlsen and Carlsen & Bruggemann; FPP3 and Hyndman & Kostenko; Eurostat's seasonal-adjustment and revision guidelines and two explainers; the Census Bureau and ABS pages; two ECB boxes; the ILO manual; BLS; HM Treasury's deflators guide and optimism-bias guidance; the Commons Library guide; Bland & Altman; Morton & Torgerson; Code of Practice 3.0; the Philadelphia Fed; Columbia's APC primer; Campbell & Stanley (SFU scan); Bernal et al. and Lopez Bernal et al.; Cochrane EPOC; Card & Krueger; the DIME wiki; Huntington-Klein; Cunningham; Goodman-Bacon; Roth; Abadie, Diamond & Hainmueller 2010 and 2015; Abadie 2021 (manuscript); Lee & Lemieux; the Magenta Book 2026 and Annex A; What Works Growth; J-PAL; EEF; Nesta; the Green Book 2026; the OBR's FERs and EFO; Britton, Fisher & Whitley; the Bank's 2024 and 2026 reports; Bernanke; van der Bles 2019; the IPCC guidance note; Wintle et al.; the MoD; Good Judgment Open; the WMO verification page; the Futures Toolkit; the Uncertainty Toolkit; Choi & Varian; the ONS Data Science Campus; the Conference Board; DOJ/FTC; the CMA. Abstract or summary only (†): Espeland & Sauder 2007; Espeland & Stevens; Sauder & Espeland 2009; Mogstad et al.; Bird et al.; Spiegelhalter 2005 (both); Fattore 2016; Faulkender & Yang; Bizjak et al.; Barnett et al.; van der Bles 2020; Gneiting et al.; Callaway & Sant'Anna; Lazer et al.; Spiegelhalter 2017. Reconstructed: Budescu et al. Secondary: Wikipedia for survivorship bias and the Brier range. Unreached: twenty-two works named in the chapter's flags.

### What I learned

- A comparison is a claim about what is held equal, and the official methods for holding things equal — standardisation, published peer sets — are better than the sector's practice and come with rules the sector breaks (a standardised ratio is not ranked; peers are not chosen by the compared).
- Rankings are reactive and noisy; the field's largest index was told to remove its rankings; ranks of a few large units can survive their intervals and ranks of many small places do not; a band of position is a rank with fewer rows, and a category of statistical separation is not.
- The funnel is the form the public-health world replaced the league table with, and it reconciles the chart standard's "sort by value" with the statisticians' "do not rank".
- Every trend line is a smoothing someone chose; seasonal adjustment is a revisable model with a three-year floor that can manufacture a season; the same month last year is slow at turning points and carries the base month; an index is change from an arbitrary base; regression to the mean is the first explanation for a change in a place selected for being extreme; there is no magic number of observations.
- Before-and-after is a "bad example" with eight rivals; the designs that do better each add a comparison with an untestable assumption, a placebo test and a rung; the verbs are after, departed from its trend, contributed to, caused; spillover and displacement are the ripple every design assumes away; a small effect in a noisy series from few places cannot be seen.
- A projection is a labelled range with its assumptions, its switching value and the maker's own record; the fan chart's inventors judged named scenarios the better form; a numerical range costs little stated trust and a vague word more; "likely" needs its number in the sentence.
- Nowcasts predict the present; "leading" is a hedged past record; borrowed thresholds name their jurisdiction.
- Beneath every comparison, a ten-line comparison record — the fourth record the book has asked for.

### What I left out, and why

- Any wording from the twenty-two unreached works; nothing quoted from memory.
- Draft 1's "abolished in 2024" — wrong by two years; corrected to the dated sequence. Draft 1's attachment of the OBR's chart caveat to the Bank's fan — separated.
- Draft 1's truncations (APHO's second condition; Bizjak's qualifications; HQIP's "that are associated with the performance indicator"); its "five recommendations"; its credit of the "small changes" sentence to the panel rather than the earlier reviews it was summarising; its four authors for a two-author paper; its March 2026 for February 2025; its "a thousand simulations" for two thousand; its inserted "leading"; its "measure" for "measures"; its agent's gloss in quotation marks — all corrected.
- Draft 1's "contributed to" for any comparison-group design and no verb for the others — replaced by a verb per rung. Draft 1's ban on bands without saying what a band is — replaced by the position/separation distinction. Draft 1's red-costed verdict on self-chosen peers — lowered to amber with the evidence's qualifications shown.
- Draft 1's "one city in five moves three places or more" — restated as an inference from interval widths. Draft 1's "the SHMI rule follows from that arithmetic" — restated as the chapter's reading.
- The Mann–Kendall test; Athey & Imbens; Roth et al. 2023; Spiegelhalter 2017 — read, not cited.

### Open questions

Whether lay readers can read a funnel or "incomparable"; which peer method and how many peers for a field of twenty-odd districts; whether to standardise a scene's rates and against what; the smoothing window and the end-point; which growth rate leads; which design when a cause is wanted; how much to adjust for optimism before the studio has a record; whether the Vibe Map projects at all; whether the four records (provenance set, recipe card, claim record, comparison record) are one document; an official rule on small-base percentages; a public-sector rule on pre-specifying comparators; the OECD turning-point procedure; the twenty-two unreached works.

### Looking back

Recorded in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's reactivity truth with the *Doing Business* post-mortem; strengthens Chapter 2's official-reader finding with three official methods and opens the thread that the official forecasters now disagree on the form of a projection; strengthens Chapter 3 with the funnel and the end-point rule and counterbalances its small multiples with the chart standard's sorting rule; strengthens and extends Chapter 4's comparability declaration into the comparison record and counterbalances it (identical indicators, incomparable places); strengthens Chapter 5 as promised (rank interval and funnel are one discipline; the C3 figures given exactly); strengthens Chapter 6 throughout (ladders as rungs; placebo as holdout; funnel as shrinkage; regression to the mean as the one-tailed search's false positive; spillover and displacement with a method) and counterbalances its control chart (the history is revised); strengthens three of version 1's positions and weakens three; opens Chapter 0's thread on the four records and the "Fixed as a procedure" tier. The Decision Book agrees in Chapters 6, 8, 10, 15 and 16 and is silent on the rest; the rules table is the candidate chapter on comparison.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

**Verification** (independent agent, about three hundred items, presence-check method with decoy strings). The great majority held. Fourteen substantive problems, all corrected: the fan chart "abolished in 2024" (still published to February 2026; first absent April 2026) and the OBR's caveat attached to the Bank's chart; three quotations cut before a qualifying clause (APHO; Bizjak; HQIP); the External Panel Review's eleven recommendations given as five, and a sentence it was summarising from the 2008 and 2013 reviews given as its own; Marshall & Spiegelhalter credited with two commentators as co-authors; the ONS nearest-neighbours output dated March 2026 instead of February 2025; the C3 Monitor's two thousand simulations halved; a word inserted into a Conference Board sentence; an agent's gloss in quotation marks; "measure" for "measures"; "five experiments and" one field experiment for five including; the 3-of-34 interval named Wilson's when it was the exact binomial; Espeland & Stevens's definition credited to Espeland & Sauder. Twenty-two "(read once)" sentences all confirmed, and the mark is now defined in the conventions paragraph and kept as a record of where the first draft was thinner. Nothing was fabricated.

**Cold read** (agent with no project context). Its strongest objection was accepted: the chapter forbade "ordered bands" and then recommended the ONS's three words and the funnel's alert and alarm, which are ordered categories; draft 2 distinguishes bands of position from categories of statistical separation, says why the second is not a league table, and runs the symmetry test on the funnel and the three words as public measures that will themselves be gamed. Also accepted: the before/after verbs were inconsistent (an uncontrolled interrupted series was "the first form with a claim attached" while the rule allowed no verb beyond "after", and the ladders were said to agree on a shape they do not) — draft 2 attaches a verb to each rung and names which ladder governs; the self-chosen-peers verdict rested on two abstracts from another field and was costed red — lowered to amber with the qualifications quoted, and the amended position now admits that choosing the similarity variables with the scene is the compared choosing one level up, with the published-once-and-fixed method as the difference; the hard line "on any measure" rested on one worked example turned into "the standard analysis" — restated with Mogstad's and the C3 Monitor's counter-evidence (few large units survive their intervals; many small places do not) and tiered "Fixed as a stance"; "beats and ahead are rank words" was claimed as a standard from the sections above when it was the chapter's own addition — now said to be, and the chapter's own two uses of "ahead" removed; "a nominal property cannot be ordered" passed version 1's identity axes though two of its examples can be ordered — corrected to a declared designation; the optimism-bias table covers costs and durations and was applied to benefits — corrected; "(read once)" was undefined; † was missing from six abstract-only sources in the body (van der Bles 2020; Bird et al.; Espeland & Stevens; Gneiting; Callaway & Sant'Anna; Lazer); the 60-second version stripped every mark and conflated the two fan charts; the illustration made design decisions (a funnel for every measure; twenty or ten peers; automatic adjustment at three years; two scenarios or four; the generic uplift) — each softened to the method's requirement with the parameter open; the comparison record lacked lines for cash/real, survivors, breaks, the leading record and why a place was selected — added to lines 1, 2 and 7; a Fixed growth-rate rule contradicted an open choice — split; the even-number rule's tier contradicted its text and the Bank's practice — made a stance with the Bank's alternative named; terms used undefined (badge rule, forking paths, holdout, price list, one-tailed search, dynamic census, the face, one tap, the three user groups, SHMI, SPC, QMI, EPR, C3, SMS) — a glossary paragraph added; four sources listed and not cited — marked "read; not cited"; a dozen flourishes and process-voice phrases cut; "honestly" removed from the question. Not accepted: that "the dashboard that cannot fill it says so" needed a rule — it is now in the first rule's text; that all thirty-six rules being Fixed in some form makes the tier uninformative — the tiers distinguish Fixed, Fixed as a stance, Fixed as a procedure and Adaptable parameters, which is Chapter 0's scheme.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` twenty-two unreached works · usability of funnels and "incomparable" for lay readers · an official rule on small-base percentages · a public-sector rule on pre-specifying comparators · the ESS guidelines' later editions and the ONS minimum series length · the OECD/Eurostat turning-point procedure. `[Prototype flag]` build the published peer method · draw the funnel for every rate · record the impact model before the data · start the projection record now · measure the base effect. `[Validate with users]` the three words · incomparable · peers · scenarios against a fan. `[Revisit → Ch 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 15]` as listed in the chapter. `[Revisit at end]` the four records as one document · whether the Vibe Map projects at all. `[Counterbalance → Ch 4 §comparability; Ch 6 §anomaly; version 1 Ch 5; Decision Book Ch 15]`.

### Rich references for Carly

**Goldstein & Spiegelhalter (1996)**, with the printed discussion — the fullest argument for intervals on ranks; the Bristol typescript is open. **APHO Technical Briefing 2 (2007)** — ranking's flaws, common and special cause, funnels and over-dispersion in a few plain pages; the best single document for comparison without a league table. **The *Doing Business* External Panel Review (2021)** — reactivity as an institutional post-mortem, with "Remove the aggregate index and country rankings" as a heading. **The ABS's *Time Series Analysis: The Basics*** — why "same month last year" is weak, in one official page. **Eurostat's ESS guidelines on seasonal adjustment (2015)**, items 4.1, 6.1 and 7.2 — each practice graded best, acceptable, to be avoided. **Bland & Altman (1994)** — regression to the mean in one open page. **Bernal, Cummins & Gasparrini (2017)** — the before/after-with-trend logic in eight open pages. **Abadie (2021)**, sections 4–5 — the contextual requirements as a checklist for any single-place comparison. **The Magenta Book (May 2026)**, pp. 56–57 and 64–65, with Annex A — the UK's current voice on counterfactuals and place-based ripples. **Bernanke's 2024 review**, Box 1 and Recommendations 7 and 11 — the argument that named scenarios beat a probability fan, and the Bank's April 2026 report as the result. **Van der Bles et al. (2019)**, §3–4 — what a caveat is about. **The OBR's forecast evaluation report (July 2025)**, chapter 2 — a worked record of forecast against outturn.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **A recommendation is not its implementation.** "Abolished in 2024" came from reading Bernanke's recommendation as the event; the Bank published the chart for two more years. Date the act, not the advice.
- **A quotation that ends at a comma needs the clause after it read.** Three truncations this sprint each removed a qualification that mattered (a second condition; a hedge; a scope). Ask verifiers to report the sentence's end as well as its presence.
- **A source summarising earlier sources is the earlier sources.** The panel's "small changes" sentence was a summary of 2008 and 2013 reviews; a chapter that credits it to 2021 has lost the provenance.
- **The symmetry list must include reactivity.** Draft 1's list of standards omitted the chapter's own first finding, and so never asked whether the funnel and the three words would be gamed. Write the list from the section headings, not from memory of them.
- **Name the verb at every rung before the truths are written.** The same error as Chapter 6's "contributed to", one rung further up.
- **Define every mark in the conventions paragraph the first time it is used.** "(read once)" was used twenty-seven times before it was explained.
- **The 60-second version carries the marks too.** It was read by the cold reader as the chapter's firmest statement and had none of the body's †s.
- Chapter 0 now owes: Entries 4–6's items; "(read once)" as a mark; the four records (provenance set, recipe card, claim record, comparison record) and whether they are one; and the growing use of "Fixed as a procedure".
- The 60-second version's length is raised a fourth time; Carly's call.

## Entry 8 · Chapter 8 — Automation without admin

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 7. Fifth and last chapter of Part 1.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What does it take for a dashboard to stay alive — refreshed, reliable, fast on a phone, its maps drawn — without a person tending it every day; what are the technological boundaries of that, what does each cost, and where does it break?

**Sub-questions.**
1. What "living" means. Freshness as a property with a standard vocabulary (the data-on-the-web best practices; catalogue metadata for update frequency and last modified; last *revised* as distinct from last *updated*, after Chapters 4, 6 and 7); the heartbeat as a display element (version 1's "data to Thursday"); staleness that degrades visibly; the observatory case — a maintained container over unmaintained contents — as the founding failure; data and link decay as measured (reference rot; dataset withdrawal); what a "living series" commits its publisher to.
2. APIs and what they commit to. What an interface promises and does not (terms, authentication, pagination, rate limits and their HTTP semantics, versioning, deprecation and its notice periods); the UK government's own API standards; the public APIs a place dashboard would draw on — national statistics, geography, police, transport, companies, open data portals — with their stated limits; the ticketing and streaming platforms' public interfaces as *technology* (what they expose, at what rate, under what terms, and how they have changed), never their data; platform dependency as a boundary (Chapter 4's platform caveat; Chapter 6's liquidation); the difference between an API that exists and one that can be relied on for five years.
3. Refresh, caching and the heartbeat's mechanics. HTTP caching and revalidation; stale-while-revalidate; incremental against full refresh; scheduled jobs and the ways they fail silently (a stopped clock; a zero that is a null — Chapter 4); the last-successful-run timestamp; monitoring (service levels and indicators; the "golden signals"; error budgets); graceful degradation; the architectures that need no server to tend — precomputed artefacts served statically, scheduled rebuilds, "baked data", scraping to a version history — and what each gives up.
4. Reliability and what breaks. Availability as the product of dependencies' availabilities; the chain from source to screen and its weakest link; schema drift; silent failure; the maintenance burden as the dominant cost of software; the bus factor; what an unmaintained dashboard looks like after a year (the observatory; Chapter 6's orphaned yardstick); the documentation that outlives the maintainer (Chapter 4's provenance set; runbooks); the human in the loop that no automation removes — someone reads the alerts.
5. Performance on a phone. The web's own thresholds (largest contentful paint; interaction; layout shift); the measured weight of the median page; the performance inequality between devices; the UK public sector's progressive-enhancement principle and its measured reason (the share of visits where scripts fail); mobile coverage and data cost as a boundary; what a dashboard built of charts and maps weighs, and what that excludes.
6. Map rendering. Raster against vector tiles; who serves the tiles and at what cost and under what licence (the national mapping agency's open data and plans; the open street map's usage policy; commercial providers' pricing); the static alternative (single-file tile archives on a plain host); boundary files and their size, simplification and vintage (Decision Book Chapter 8); choropleths on a phone; accessible maps; what a map costs to keep alive against what a chart costs.
7. Caching, rate limits and the cost of "no admin". The published limits of the services above, as numbers; what they imply for how often a dashboard may ask and how it must store the answers; the economics — a live query against a nightly bake; what "automation without admin" can mean in practice and what remains a person's job.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** W3C *Data on the Web Best Practices* (2017) and DCAT 3 on accrual periodicity and modification dates; the ODI on data quality; Zittrain, Bowers & Stanton 2021 on link rot and Klein et al. 2014 on reference rot; GDS *API technical and data standards* and the Service Manual on progressive enhancement, performance and the 2013 JavaScript-failure measurement; RFC 9111 (HTTP caching), RFC 5861 (stale-while-revalidate), RFC 6585 (429) and RFC 9110 (Retry-After); the Google SRE book chapters on service level objectives and monitoring; Nygard on stability patterns if open; the published usage policies and rate limits of TfL's Unified API, Companies House, Nominatim, the OpenStreetMap tile servers, Ordnance Survey's Data Hub, the ONS and Nomis APIs, data.gov.uk, the Police API; the public documentation of Skiddle's, Ticketmaster's, Eventbrite's, Spotify's, YouTube's and MusicBrainz's interfaces — terms, limits, deprecations (Spotify's November 2024 restriction; Eventbrite's 2020 removal of search; Twitter's 2023 pricing) — read as technology; Google's Core Web Vitals thresholds; the HTTP Archive *Web Almanac*; Alex Russell on the performance inequality gap; Ofcom *Connected Nations*; the Mapbox Vector Tile specification; MapLibre; Protomaps/PMTiles; Mapshaper and TopoJSON on simplification; Willison on git scraping and baked data; GitHub Actions' scheduled-workflow limits; version 1's Chapter 8 (freshness as a display element; staleness degrades visibly; the observatory) and Chapter 1's observatory audit as house positions; the Vibe Setting Framework's "maintained or extractive" requirement (house, confidential, cited not reproduced).

**What I expect to find.** That freshness has a vocabulary in the catalogue standards that dashboards do not use; that link and data decay are measured and large; that the government's own API standards are good and its APIs uneven; that the platform interfaces a place dashboard would want are the least stable dependency it could choose, and have each been narrowed within the last five years; that the reliability engineering literature has the vocabulary (service levels, error budgets, golden signals) for the heartbeat and that none of it removes the person who reads the alert; that the static, precomputed architecture is the one that most nearly means "no admin" and that it trades liveness for it; that the phone is the binding constraint on weight and the map the heaviest thing on the page; and that the cost of a map is a licence and a tile bill, where the cost of a chart is nothing.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) freshness and decay — the data-on-the-web and catalogue standards, update and modification metadata, link and dataset rot, the living series' commitments; (B) APIs and platform dependency — the UK API standards, the public and open-data APIs with their limits, the ticketing and streaming interfaces' terms, limits and deprecations read as technology; (C) reliability engineering and the no-admin architectures — caching RFCs, rate-limit semantics, SRE service levels and monitoring, graceful degradation, baked data, git scraping, scheduled jobs and their limits, availability arithmetic, maintenance cost; (D) phones and maps — Core Web Vitals, the Web Almanac, the performance inequality gap, progressive enhancement and the JavaScript-failure figure, Ofcom coverage, tile formats, tile servers' policies and prices, static tiles, boundary simplification and vintage, accessible maps.

### Threads pulled

- **Freshness has a vocabulary that dashboards do not use.** DCAT 3 separates issued, modified and the period covered; "frequency" is defined three incompatible ways across DCMI (items added), the UK and US profiles (published) and DCAT-AP (updated); and the standard's own usage note admits an absent modification date means one of three opposite things. The US profile lets the "modified" field hold a repeating interval — a promise in place of a fact. Pulled into section 4 and truth 1.
- **The UK has official dashboard guidance, and it is demanding.** The Government Analysis Function's *Building and managing dashboards* (6 February 2026): a dashboard "is a digital service", "not an appropriate project for a single person or for temporary staff", must "Always make it clear which dates or period" it covers, logs changes with change notes, and is retired with a banner "at least six months before" and an archive including the code. One agent could not find it at guessed URLs; another did. It became the frame for the service record.
- **The open licence disclaims supply.** The OGL's last line — "The Information Provider does not guarantee the continued supply of the Information" — read via SPDX because the National Archives page loops. TfL's licence repeats it. The chapter's distinction between a dependency on *supply* and a dependency on *terms* grew from this.
- **The platform terms describe dashboard-making clause by clause.** Spotify Developer Policy III.13 ("creating new or derived listenership metrics, benchmarking … usage statistics"), III.5 (no integration with another service's content), Terms IV.3 (no storing or aggregating); Ticketmaster's "applications that make a large number of calls … not primarily in response to direct user actions"; Skiddle non-commercial by default with limits "continually changed" and documentation in a repository archived April 2024. Read as technology only; no listing fetched. Four UK ticketing platforms publish no developer documentation that could be found; Eventbrite's site is script-only.
- **How interfaces close.** Facebook 4 April 2018 ("Starting today" / "effective today"), Spotify 27 November 2024 ("Effective today"), Spotify 15 May 2025 (one month; organisations only; 250k MAUs), Spotify February/March 2026 (development mode narrowed), Twitter 2023 (via TechCrunch; the company's post is closed to the tools) and X's current pay-per-usage page, Google Maps' contractual twelve months with security and economic-burden exceptions, GDS's six months as "should try" for live interfaces only. Written up as three patterns with notice following the stated reason — on a handful of cases, and said so.
- **The ONS retirement procedure.** `Deprecation`, `Sunset` and `Link` headers, "rolling blackouts", then 404; no notice period. The most explicit UK public procedure found. The IETF header RFCs it implements were not fetched `[Further research]`.
- **The police snap-point method.** Incident locations moved to a 2012 list (refreshed 2022) of points placed on street centres and named premises including "Nightclub", catchments of at least eight addresses. Quoted as the publisher's method text only; the chapter's inference that premises at snap points appear to be where incidents happen is marked as its own. Bears on Chapter 4's provenance set and Chapter 10.
- **Availability is a chain, and the product rule is nowhere printed.** Treynor et al. give the intersection rule and the "extra 9"; no open source prints the multiplication, so the chapter does its own (0.999^10 = 99.0 per cent = 3.65 days) and says so.
- **"200 with the wrong content" is an error.** The SRE golden-signals definition. The argument against uptime monitoring for dashboards rests on it, with the workbook's freshness SLO forms ("The oldest data is no older than Y") and "Stale data is almost always better than incorrect data" (three passes).
- **Scheduled jobs fail quietly, in their own documentation.** cron's manuals define no failure; GitHub's schedule "may be dropped", is disabled after 60 days without activity in a public repository, notifies "the user who last modified the cron syntax", and is now timezone-aware (the brief's "UTC only" was out of date). The SRE cron chapter's "independent monitoring of the effect of cron jobs" and the heartbeat/dead-man's-switch practice became truth 5.
- **The protocol already keeps a stale copy.** RFC 9111's fresh/stale/freshness lifetime/Age; RFC 5861's `stale-while-revalidate` and `stale-if-error` (Informational). AWS names "Invalidating or emptying local state as a result of a failed refresh" as an anti-pattern. Standard and practice agree: keep the last good copy, say its age.
- **Baked data and git scraping, in Willison's words, with his own boundary** ("at most a few times a day"). PMTiles as the map's baked form; the free static hosts' edges (25 MiB per asset; 1 GB per site; Netlify's paused state); no availability commitment establishable for any free tier (GitHub's SLA page returned 500).
- **The maintenance burden could not be grounded in the classic figure.** No open primary for "60–80% of lifecycle cost"; GAO's 80 per cent is of federal IT *spending*. The chapter asserts only that operating costs more than making. The research run's own drift — roughly a quarter of the brief's URLs and phrasings moved or misremembered — is used as first-hand evidence, labelled as the agents' count.
- **The phone.** Core Web Vitals thresholds and their achievability basis (10 per cent of origins; 2.5 s "consistently achievable"); Web Almanac 2024 and 2025 (medians 2,311 KB and 2.6 MB; 43–44 per cent and 48 per cent passing on mobile; the two editions disagree by a point about 2024); Russell's budgets (~1.3 MiB with 650 KiB script; 2.5 MiB with 100 KiB; "15-25% as fast"); Google's 2016 "53% … likely to be abandoned" with its footnote; GDS progressive enhancement and Herlihy's 2013 "1.1% … (1 in 93)", 0.9 of it involuntary; Ofcom 96/88 per cent landmass, 18 per cent smartphone-only, 30 vs 9 per cent by social grade. Two public data front doors (OS plans; ONS geography portal) return nothing to a reader without scripts.
- **The map.** Vector against raster as device work against server work (the vendor's "more processing power" sentence confirmed with a decoy); four tile supplies with four endings (OSMF "no SLA or guarantee" and no offline use; OS keys, limits and logo; Mapbox/MapTiler metered with a pause; PMTiles self-hosted and read-only); ONS boundary products BFC 127,631,360 bytes against BSC 1,671,168 in the same record type (~76:1; the first draft divided unlike records and got 68:1 — caught by the verifier); wards change "on the first Thursday in May"; Mapshaper's "small polygons can disappear entirely"; MapLibre ~287 KB gzipped against Leaflet 42 KB; no published measurement of bytes per map view; WCAG 2.5.1/2.5.7 use maps as their worked examples; the Analysis Function's "downloadable data table" and five blues.
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No design system reached says a dashboard shows its last refresh; no measured study of stale-dashboard distrust (Fogg 2002 and Nielsen 1996 are the nearest); no measured study of UK open-data staleness (the instruments — GODI, the Barometer — have themselves stopped); no bytes-per-view for a map.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §12. Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch08-agent-returns.md`. The orchestrator spot-checked twelve sources the same day (Analysis Function dashboards guidance; GitHub schedule docs; Spotify Policy III.13/III.5; OGL via SPDX; Treynor et al.; Ticketmaster terms; ONS bots page; OSMF tile policy; Herlihy 1.1%; Web Almanac 2024 page weight; SRE workbook "Stale data"; Pew 2024) — all held except one Web Almanac sentence the fetch tool had reshaped (corrected to "In October 2024, the median page weight for a mobile page was 2,311 KB."). The verifier re-traced about 400 strings on some 125 pages.

### What I learned

That "no admin" is a sentence with a person in it: automation moves the job from doing the refresh to reading the ticket, re-pointing the job and deciding what happens when a source closes, and the UK's own guidance says a dashboard is not a one-person project. That freshness is a declared lifetime — three dates and a frequency — and a single "last updated" stamp is ambiguous by the catalogue standard's own admission. That the open public interfaces permit everything and promise nothing about supply, while the platform interfaces promise nothing and forbid, clause by clause, storing, deriving, combining and calling on a schedule — two kinds of dependency that belong on two kinds of line. That availability is a chain, that the baked architecture moves the chain from read time to build time, and that the protocol and the cloud provider's own anti-pattern list agree on the degraded mode: keep the last good copy, show its age, never blank. That schedulers fail quietly in their own words and the watch must be independent and must check the effect. That the phone to budget for is a seventh to a quarter as fast as the builder's and that one visit in about a hundred never runs the script. That a map is a licence, a tile supply and a vintage, that the renderer alone can be two-fifths of the script budget, and that a map needs a table twin by the accessibility standards' own examples. And that the service record — ten lines, written before launch, with a retirement plan and a "not promised" line — is the fifth record beneath a dashboard's numbers `[Revisit at end]`.

### What I left out, and why

- RFC 9110 §8.8.3 and §13, DWBP BP21/22/27/28 and preservation, DCAT 3 §6.6–6.7, §11–12 and `accrualPeriodicity`: beyond the fetch tool's truncation. Sibling texts and official profiles used; marked.
- Eventbrite, OS Data Hub plans, ONS Open Geography Portal terms: script-only shells. Nothing printed from memory.
- The National Archives OGL page: redirect loop; SPDX reproduction used and said so.
- Twitter's and Reddit's 2023 announcements in their own words; Tidelift 2024; GitHub SLA; Bank of England real-time database; JMIR 2021; ISO/IEC 25012; Lientz & Swanson, Boehm, Glass; Nygard ch. 5; AWS Builders' Library; Mapbox terms; Portal Watch papers; ONS BGC/BUC size records (rate-limited): unreached.
- The reader's invented sentences for unseen DWBP and DCAT sections, caught by the presence check and discarded.
- Any listing, event, track or venue from any platform; anything about what the dashboard should look like; the creative decisions (vector or raster; which tile supply; the words beside a figure) — recorded as choices, not made.
- The 60-second version is again long (about 870 words); the question of its length stands with Carly (raised in Entries 4–7).

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: the retirement header RFCs; measured evidence that stale dashboards cost trust `[Validate with users]`; a measured study of UK open-data staleness; bytes per map view `[Prototype flag]`; whether to call the platform interfaces at all `[Prototype flag]` and Chapter 10; the degraded mode seen and the sixty-day disabling tested `[Prototype flag]`; the four-part freshness statement against a single date `[Validate with users]`; vector or raster as experienced `[Validate with users]`; five records as one `[Revisit at end]`; platform terms as consent questions `[Revisit → Ch 10]`; a live measure of a vibe accepting the chain arithmetic `[Revisit → Ch 9]`; whether a studio's free public page is "commercial" under the non-commercial clauses (a legal reading the book cannot give).

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's first-screen freshness and extends it to four parts; counterbalances Chapter 1's observatory (the standard applies to the studio first) `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`; strengthens Chapter 2 with the operations reader's methods and turns its smartphone-only readers into a number and a budget; strengthens Chapter 3's "show the shape" by pricing the map and counterbalances any rule that assumes a script `[Counterbalance → Ch 3]`; strengthens Chapter 4 throughout (revision status → three dates and a frequency; platform caveat → dependency on terms; "a zero that is a null" → the named anti-pattern) and opens the overlap between the provenance set and service-record line 2; strengthens Chapter 5's kept recipe and opens a Goodhart thread (a reliable year trains readers); strengthens Chapter 6's liquidation caution with the terms and its small-number rules with snapping, and counterbalances its "contributed to" ladder (a claim is re-earned when its source closes) `[Counterbalance → Ch 6]`; strengthens Chapter 7's history rule with the archive of fetches and its season rule from the operations side; qualifies version 1's Chapter 8 (data's date, not page's; visible degradation stops short of silent disappearance) and weakens "no admin" as a slogan; opens threads for Chapter 0 (five records; three near-equal Fixed tiers; "Standard" used for specifications, licences and terms), Chapter 9 (a live vibe measure's cost) and Chapter 10 (terms as consent). Decision Book: agrees with DTA-05/06, BRF-15, BRK-18, DSH-03/13/15/18; conflicts with DSH-21 (degraded state is a Fixed procedure, not Decorative); no rules on freshness display, dependencies, refresh, watch, objective, budget, progressive enhancement, tile supply or retirement — logged as a candidate chapter, *Running it*. Design system: agrees on provenance and the Phone/Screen-reader tests; no freshness component, degraded-tile state, map component, twin, byte budget or script-free requirement — logged, not designed.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Dispatched together, as in Entries 6 and 7. The **verifier** re-traced about 400 strings on some 125 pages and reported ten wrong, one unverifiable, ten truncated before a qualifying clause, several case-only, and five numbers restated loosely; it also confirmed, on independent passes, most of the sentences the first draft had marked "(read once)", so those marks were lifted (89 in draft 1; 4 remain). Corrected: a spliced Willison quotation that ran two sites together (the ~22 MB database is Mozilla's, the daily action is datasette.io's); the boundary ratio, which had divided a WFS record by a Feature Layer record (now BFC 127,631,360 against BSC 1,671,168, ~76:1, and the 60-second version's "generalised" corrected to the 200-metre product); "the 200-metre file is most of" a megabyte budget (it is larger than it; the answer is a district cut, not the national file); "Web performance is tied to what devices and networks people can afford" attributed to the 2025 Almanac (it is 2024's); the "enable JavaScript" string attributed to the ONS portal (OS only; ONS returned a title); "idempotent mutation" attributed to the SRE cron chapter (it is the workbook's); the ONS retirement guide's title; the Service Manual page's title ("robust", not "resilient"); YouTube's "100 search.list calls" attached to the revision history (it is on Getting started); Mapbox's 12-hour session attributed to the pricing page (it is the glossary); the ONS calendar's "Provisional" label (not found); WCAG 2.5.7 and 2.5.1 closed before "or the functionality is determined by the user agent…" and "unless a multipoint or path-based gesture is essential"; RFC 9111 §4.3.3 closed before "subject to its constraints on doing so … or retry the validation request"; Pew's "as of October 2023"; GitHub's "In a public repository" in the 60-second version; Ticketmaster's "Further, we reserve" and the 60-second version's dropped "applications that make a large number of calls"; Russell's "~" and "Conservatively"; Facebook's "So starting today"; Code of Practice 3.5 and 9.7 tails; Geisser's second sentence. Numbers: "four to six times" → "four to nearly seven"; "a fifth to a quarter" → "a seventh to a quarter"; "one in three" → "three in ten" and "poorest households" → "social grade DE households"; 287/650 recomputed in KiB as 43 per cent and "half" → "over two-fifths"; "sixteen months" → "fifteen"; "about a megabyte" replaced throughout by Russell's own two budgets. Glosses: Willison's off-the-hour cron given his own reason ("feels polite") rather than GitHub's; "the most widely used commercial map service" and "the commonest free scheduler" removed; "1.1% of visits" → "1.1% of people". The Spotify non-commercial clause re-pointed from the Terms to Policy §IV.2.

The **cold reader** (no project context) said the chapter decided that "a dashboard cannot run itself" and named the hard line (last good copy kept and shown with its age; never blanked) and the open choice (vector or raster) correctly. Its strongest objection — that the chapter converts "not an appropriate project for a single person" into paperwork, that the "one ticket a month" payoff is unevidenced, and that the heartbeat monitor is a new dependency the symmetry test does not read — was accepted: the expectation is now labelled an expectation for the prototype to test, the monitor is written into truth 5, the rule and the service record as a dependency with terms and an owner, and the rule-count objection stands as a fair description of a Fixed-heavy chapter. Changed on its findings: "heartbeat" was used for both the date on the face and the monitoring ping — now "stamp" for the first, "heartbeat" for the second, both defined in Before you start; "tile" was used for map tiles and dashboard panels — now "panel" for the second; "page" (pager alert) glossed; alert/ticket/alarm made consistent ("ticket" throughout; the one alert is the page lying about its date); bus/truck factor, SLO/SLA, SDA, MAUs, social grade, KiB/MiB defined; the section 11 observatory paragraph had inverted version 1's position (the observatory was the founding failure, not the model) — rewritten; the freshness lifetime's clock fixed (counted from the newest figure's publication date) and the objective, filled example and illustration aligned to it; "If it is, the page is in error" → "If it is older"; the ping direction corrected (the job pings the monitor); the ONS 120/10s against 200/min explained; Ofcom's 96 against 88 per cent explained; "most public interfaces call themselves beta" → "several"; the Nominatim exception to "every rate is far above"; the TfL quarterly-job slip; the sixty-day rule's "repository activity" left open rather than asserted; the real-time flood feed excepted from "every source publishes monthly, weekly or daily"; the correction/revision distinction argued in section 4 so the rule has a body; the "not done" rule grounded in "Users build on the reality of what you offer"; cross-references fixed (section 5 for the licence; section 8 for caching; section 3 for retirement; Chapter 0 for the symmetry test); section 11's verdict set extended to "counterbalances"; claims softened where the evidence was a handful of cases ("each been narrowed" now names the streaming and social platforms; the closing pattern "on the handful of cases here"; "the literature is unanimous" → "the practitioner literature agrees"; the YouTube "while this chapter was being researched" → four months before); several unsourced superlatives and sector-explaining asides removed; the illustration stripped of design choices (no `/table` address, no "basement", no prescribed words beside a figure, the boundary cut rather than a named product); the rules table re-tiered where the kind was a reasoned model (Russell) or an adopted government standard (progressive enhancement). Not changed: the 60-second version's length (with Carly); the number of rules; the clincher sentences, most of which were left.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×5 (the unreached list; single-pass and secondary; stale-dashboard trust evidence; UK open-data staleness; bytes per map view). `[Prototype flag]` ×4 (bytes per view; whether to call the platforms; the degraded mode seen; the sixty-day disabling). `[Validate with users]` ×3 (reading "data to [date]"; the four-part statement; vector or raster as experienced). `[Revisit at end]` ×1 (five records). `[Revisit → Ch 9]` ×1; `[Revisit → Ch 10]` ×1. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`, `[→ Ch 3]`, `[→ Ch 6]`. `[Rich reference]` ×7.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **Government Analysis Function, *Data visualisation: building and managing dashboards* (6 February 2026).** Half an hour. The one official UK text that treats a dashboard as a service from launch to retirement — including "not an appropriate project for a single person" and the six-month retirement banner. It is the frame for the service record and the sentence the studio's plan has to answer.
2. **Treynor, Dahlin, Rau & Beyer, "The Calculus of Service Availability" (*ACM Queue*, 2017).** Short and open. The chain rule and "design for graceful degradation" in the engineers' own words.
3. **Simon Willison, "Git scraping" (2020) and "The Baked Data architectural pattern" (2021).** The no-admin architecture, its benefits and its boundary ("at most a few times a day") from the person who named it. Read with the GitHub schedule documentation beside it for what the posts do not say.
4. **RFC 9111 §4.2–4.3 and RFC 5861.** Two short normative texts that give the whole vocabulary of fresh, stale, lifetime, and "serve the old copy if the new one fails".
5. **The Spotify sequence** (blog 27 November 2024; blog 15 April 2025; the February 2026 migration guide; Developer Policy III.13). A dated primary case of an interface narrowing in three steps and a clause that forbids derived metrics outright. Worth reading before any decision about streaming data.
6. **Alex Russell, "The Performance Inequality Gap, 2024".** The one source that turns phones and networks into a byte budget, with its working shown; the "15-25% as fast" sentence is the one to remember.
7. **The OpenStreetMap Foundation's tile and Nominatim usage policies.** Two pages in operator's language: free data, not free servers; four requests a minute for scheduled scripts; no offline use.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Two senses of one word are a defect the writer cannot see.** "Heartbeat" and "tile" each carried two meanings through a 34,000-word draft and only the cold reader noticed. Before drafting, list every term of art the chapter will use and give each one sense; where a house word (version 1's "heartbeat") collides with a field's word, rename the house one.
- **The index-based lift.** When a verifier confirms sentences the first draft marked "(read once)", lift the marks by counting occurrences, not by matching text — and check the first and last indices by hand, because a definition sentence that quotes the mark can shift the count by one (it did).
- **A ratio needs like records.** The boundary-file ratio divided two different record types from the same portal. Any ratio from two API records states the record type of both.
- **Attribute each sentence to its edition.** Two editions of the same yearly report sat side by side and a sentence from one was credited to the other. When two editions are in play, every quotation names its year.
- **A splice is a fabrication.** Two true halves of two different sentences about two different sites made one false sentence. Where a quotation is built from fragments, the fragments stay separate on the page.
- **The watch is a dependency.** The remedy a chapter recommends is itself a thing with terms, a price and an owner; the symmetry test applies to it.
- **The research brief's expectations are a fallible memory, not a baseline.** "Drift" should be reported as "moved or misremembered", with the agents' count, not as a measured rate.
- **Define the clock.** A lifetime, a lag or an age needs the event it counts from stated once and used everywhere; the cold reader found four different clocks in one draft.
- **Dispatch the cold reader and the verifier in one message.** Again the right call: the two reports arrived within five minutes of each other and the revision was one script.
