## Entry 9 · Chapter 9 — Measuring a vibe

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 8 closed Part 1 and the reader was rebuilt. First chapter of Part 2; the book's central chapter.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** How might one measure a vibe — a felt quality of a place: its atmosphere, its welcome, its liveliness, the sense that something is happening here — in a way that is evidence and not decoration; by which families of method; what each needs and can claim; and how any such measure is validated, above all by whether the people who make the place recognise themselves in it? The book does not answer this for any place. It sets out the methods, their requirements and their failure modes.

**Sub-questions.**
1. What a "vibe" is as a construct. The literatures that have tried to name it: atmosphere as *made* (Böhme; Michels's empirical programme), as *perceived* (the soundscape standard's "as perceived or experienced and/or understood by people, in context" and its eight-attribute circumplex, "vibrant" among them), and as *excess* (Anderson's affective atmospheres; the dancefloor ethnography's warning that felt togetherness "may hide exclusions and injustices") — version 1's three registers, to be re-tested against the sources rather than assumed. Sense of place, place attachment and place identity as measured constructs (scales, their reliabilities, what they predict); "vibrancy" and "vitality" in urban studies (Jacobs operationalised; vitality indices from mobile and transaction data); how other sectors have put a number on atmosphere — retail atmospherics, the servicescape, stadium atmosphere scales, festival experience, destination image — and what those instruments can and cannot claim. Chapter 5's first instruction applies: define the construct before the proxy.
2. The co-produced family. Participatory mapping and PPGIS (what it measures, its sampling, its known biases); community indicators; participatory statistics and "most significant change"; photovoice; self-placed distributions (the human dot plot as a collection instrument; dotmocracy; the human histogram) and what self-placement is evidence of; feedback loops — "what this changed" — in participatory evaluation and constituent-voice practice, and whether closing the loop measurably changes participation or trust; member checking and the legitimacy/validity split from Chapter 5; opt-in participation's selection problem and brigading (online polls; moderation designed in).
3. The relational and behavioural family. Two-mode networks and the duality of persons and groups; co-attendance and co-appearance as data, and why co-occurrence is not a social tie (the data contract's caution, and the network literature's own); scenes as measurable (Silver & Clark's *Scenescapes* method); urban vitality from mobile-phone, check-in and transaction data (De Nadai et al.; Cranshaw's Livehoods; Sulis et al.) and what such traces describe (Chapter 4: the platform's users); repeat visitation, dwell and return as revealed behaviour (Chapter 6's first rung); third places and attempts to count them; what a live source of behaviour costs (Chapter 8).
4. The affective and sentiment family, and its limits. Sentiment and emotion from text at place scale (the Hedonometer; Mitchell et al.'s geography of happiness; Quercia et al.'s urban perception work; Place Pulse) and the evidence that such estimates do and do not track surveys (Jaidka et al. 2020); the demographic and dialect skew of the sources; the facial-expression critique (Barrett et al. 2019) as the general lesson about inferring feeling from signal; audio and music-feature proxies (the soundscape measurement standard; sound level; "valence/energy" features — which Chapter 8 found withdrawn from the platform that supplied them); experience sampling by location (Mappiness; the day-reconstruction and experience-sampling methods; experienced v remembered) as the method that asks the person at the moment.
5. Validating a felt measure. The official precedent: how a national statistics office built and tested subjective wellbeing questions (ONS4; the OECD guidelines; Dolan, Layard & Metcalfe), including cognitive testing and response-process evidence; the standards for a new instrument (AERA/APA/NCME's five sources, from Chapter 5; DeVellis on scale development; reliability, test–retest, known-groups, convergent and discriminant validity); what "the scene recognises itself" is in these terms (face validity, member checking, legitimacy) and what it is not (validity by acclaim); the three readers' (Chapter 2) likely reading of a felt number; standardisation for a felt measure — what a place's "expected" vibe would even be (Chapter 7's thread); whether a felt measure can be a surrogate for anything (Chapter 6's thread).
6. When the felt and the counted disagree. Mixed-methods integration (Fetters, Curry & Creswell: confirmation, expansion, discordance) and pre-committing to report discordance; the economically marginal, culturally load-bearing case as a method shape, not a finding; what a dashboard owes when its layers disagree.
7. What the measure does to the measured. Reactivity of a felt measure (Chapter 5's Goodhart); the extraction the scene itself named (house framework, confidential); consent at scene level and the community-held layer — handed to Chapter 10, but the measure's design cannot wait for it; and the cost in attention and in supply (Chapter 8) of any live felt source.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Böhme 1993 and Michels 2015 on atmospheres; Anderson 2009; Sumartojo & Pink *Atmospheres and the Experiential World*; Edensor; Garcia-Mispireta *Together, Somehow* (method only); ISO 12913-1:2014, ISO/TS 12913-2:2018 and ISO/TS 12913-3:2019 (definition; data collection; data analysis; the circumplex; medians and IQRs); Axelsson, Nilsson & Berglund 2010 on the soundscape circumplex; Russell 1980 on the affect circumplex; Scannell & Gifford 2010; Lewicka 2011; Jorgensen & Stedman 2001; Williams & Vaske 2003 on place attachment scales; Kotler 1973 atmospherics; Bitner 1992 servicescape; Uhrich & Benkenstein 2010 stadium atmosphere; Getz on festival experience; Jacobs 1961 and the vitality operationalisations (Sung, Lee & Cheon 2015; Delclòs-Alió & Miralles-Guasch 2018; De Nadai et al. 2016; Sulis et al. 2018); Brown & Kyttä 2014 and 2018 on PPGIS; Kosmala et al. 2016 on citizen-science data quality; Davies & Dart 2005 *Most Significant Change*; Wang & Burris 1997 photovoice; Chambers 2007 participatory numbers; Keystone/Feedback Labs on closing the loop; Birt et al. 2016 member checking (already in the ledger); Fishkin on deliberative polling; Bethlehem 2010 on opt-in web surveys; Breiger 1974; Borgatti & Halgin 2011; Silver & Clark 2016; Cranshaw et al. 2012; Oldenburg 1989; the Hedonometer (Dodds et al. 2011); Mitchell et al. 2013; Quercia, Schifanella & Aiello 2014 and 2015; Salesforce/Naik et al. Place Pulse; Jaidka et al. 2020 PNAS; Blodgett et al. 2016; Barrett et al. 2019; Mohammad 2022 on ethics of emotion measurement; MacKerron & Mourato 2013 (Mappiness); Kahneman et al. 2004 (DRM); Csikszentmihalyi & Larson 1987 (ESM); ONS *Personal well-being user guidance* and the 2011–12 question development; OECD 2013 *Guidelines on Measuring Subjective Well-being*; Dolan, Layard & Metcalfe 2011; AERA/APA/NCME 2014 (ledger); DeVellis *Scale Development*; Willis on cognitive interviewing; Fetters, Curry & Creswell 2013; Reed et al. 2025 on participatory mapping; version 1's Chapter 4 *The felt layer* (archived) as the house positions to re-test; the Vibe Setting Framework (confidential; cited, never reproduced).

**What I expect to find.** That "vibe" is not one construct but at least three, and that the literatures measuring each have different instruments, units and validation histories — the perceived register the best-instrumented (an international standard with a circumplex, medians and interquartile ranges), the made register observable but rarely scaled, the excess register resistant to number by its own theorists' account. That the official precedent for a new felt measure (national wellbeing) shows what it costs to do properly — years of cognitive testing, four questions, a 0–10 scale, published distributions and not means — and that this is the yardstick. That co-produced methods are strongest on legitimacy and weakest on selection, and that "what this changed" has little measured precedent but a clear theory in constituent-voice practice. That relational measures are rich and that the co-occurrence caution is the network literature's own, not only the data contract's. That sentiment-at-place methods track surveys weakly and skew by who posts, and that the facial-expression critique generalises: a signal is not a feeling. That experience sampling by location is the method closest to asking the person at the moment, and the most demanding of them. That discordance between felt and counted is a reportable result in mixed-methods doctrine. And that the question "does the scene recognise itself" is face validity plus legitimacy — necessary, not sufficient — which Chapter 5 already found, and that this chapter must say what else is needed.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) the construct — atmosphere, affect, sense of place and vitality across the literatures and sectors, including the soundscape standard and the atmospherics scales; (B) the co-produced family — participatory mapping, community indicators, participatory statistics, self-placed distributions, feedback loops, selection and brigading; (C) the relational and behavioural family — two-mode networks and co-occurrence, scenes, urban vitality from traces, check-ins, revealed return, third places; (D) the affective and sentiment family and validation — sentiment-at-place and its evidence, the facial-expression critique, experience sampling by location, the national wellbeing precedent, the OECD guidelines, scale development and the five sources of validity evidence, mixed-methods discordance.

### Threads pulled

- **"Vibe" is three constructs, and only one has an instrument.** The three-register split from version 1 held against every reachable source, with one correction: in the perceived register's standard, "vibrant" is not a measured dimension but a derived diagonal (pleasant + eventful, via the ISO/TS 12913-3 formulae, read through the authors' software tutorial), and the one study of how people use the scales found the vibrant–monotonous axis the least evenly read (Lionello et al. 2021). Böhme and Anderson were closed; their words reached the chapter through Michels, marked secondary.
- **The soundscape standard as the nearest worked example.** ISO 12913 Parts 1–2 confirmed current; Part 3 (2019) withdrawn 8 May 2025 and replaced by a 2025 edition — a drift caught at the catalogue. The SSID protocol: in-situ questionnaire paired with a recording at the respondent's side, "a minimum of 100 responses" per location, convenience recruitment of those present. The protocol's authors argue against the median and for the "percent of people likely to perceive". Lineage traced to Axelsson 2010 (100 Swedish students; pleasantness 50%, eventfulness 18%, familiarity 6%) and Russell 1980 (36/34/343 students in Vancouver).
- **Place attachment as "tested before it was counted".** Williams & Vaske 2003: twelve items, two dimensions, alphas 0.81–0.94, four items enough, and the ceiling — "relatively low variance and high scores" among people already at the place. The UK's official belonging item (Community Life Survey) defines its place in walking minutes and, as far as the technical report shows, was not cognitively tested.
- **The contrast case turned.** "Feeling of safety" is not only a different construct; the official single item was shown by new questions run beside it (Gray, Jackson & Farrall 2008) to "magnify" everyday fear, and the office dropped the questions for two years. A vibe item without the same tests would be no better placed.
- **The participatory family's own evidence on selection.** Brown 2017: effort highest among those with livelihoods at stake; people mark near home; internet panels raise response and lower spatial quality. Kahila-Tani et al.: participation can become "a small-group elitist activity". Reed et al. 2025 (read for method only): twelve people, "We make no claim" of representing a community. Citizen-science reassurance (Kosmala) rests on a ground truth a felt quality lacks; participation inequality (Haklay 2016; Nielsen 2006) transfers whole.
- **The human dot plot, tested.** The living histogram's bimodality is an artefact (Schilling et al. 2002); spectrum lines are described by their own guides as conversations in which positions move; a private mark is a single item of a non-concrete construct (Diamantopoulos et al. 2012); visible estimates narrow without improving (Lorenz et al. 2011). Weakened as a measure, kept as a form.
- **"What this changed" has a literature.** Arnstein's consultation rung (attitude surveys; "participated in participation"); Fung's third dimension; the Cabinet Office consultation principles (how many, what they said, how it informed, within twelve weeks); the Gunning principles; Sjoberg, Mellon & Peixoto's Fix My Street result (a fixed first report "associated with a 57 percent increase" in a second); Cleary & Balmer (tailored report-back beats generic, which did worse than none); Boyle & Harris's co-production boundary (asking is not co-producing); the CARE "Responsibility" principle. No study of a "you said, we did" notice as such.
- **Co-presence is not a tie, in the network literature's own words.** Génois & Barrat 2018 (six datasets, five settings, both kinds of data): co-presence "(by definition) much denser", surrogate networks "only partially able to identify the most central nodes", "not easily usable" for surrogate contact data. Eagle et al.'s 95% came from off-campus, off-hours proximity. Neal et al.: a co-occurrence count is tested against a null before it is an edge. McPherson et al.: baseline v inbreeding homophily. Davis, Gardner & Gardner's original inference rested on "consistently" and ethnography (Freeman — the verifier could not re-open it).
- **Scenes, traces and the proxy nobody checked.** Silver, Clark & Navarro code amenities 1–5 on fifteen dimensions with r = .8 inter-coder rule and validate "by 'face validity'"; their own question — can "organizational directories" reach "the experiential meaning of place?" — answered by their reviewer "No and yes". De Nadai et al.: 77% of variance in an "assumed" proxy, one operator, no validation against people, blind to day and night. Livehoods: the only trace study that went back to residents (27, recruited via social media), naming majority, sharing, demographic, experimenter and confirmation bias and a hole where one of the poorest districts was. Sulis et al.: a check they call "incongruous" (r = 0.43). Hecht & Stephens: 24.4× Foursquare users per capita in urban counties. Orstad et al.'s systematic review of 85 studies: perceived and objective place "related but distinct constructs", 8.2% associated with the same outcome.
- **Signals.** Dodds et al.'s own three limits ("we quantify only how people appear to others"); Mitchell et al. 2013 Spearman 0.328 against Gallup; Jaidka et al. 2020 (reconstructed): dictionaries "inconsistent", supervised models up to r = 0.64; Eichstaedt → Brown & Coyne sign flip; Blodgett's parser recall 64.0 v 80.4; Place Pulse 1.0 raters 76% male, median 28, 91 countries; Barrett et al.'s three shortcomings; Mohammad's ceiling ("what one is trying to convey or what is perceived"); the AI Act's recital 44 naming the same three shortcomings (the match is the chapter's reading) and Article 5(1)(f) for biometric inference at work and in education. Sound: sources over levels (the review reporting Axelsson); 52% v 21%. Song features: valence defined, not derived; Park et al. never say "valence"; the endpoint withdrawn (Chapter 8).
- **Experience sampling.** ESM/DRM validity history; Mappiness (1 million responses, 20,000 participants, within-person; "all Apple iOS users"); Urban Mind (108 people; "cannot be considered representative"); the National Academies: "often regarded as the gold standard" and not yet for flagship surveys.
- **The official precedent in full.** ONS4's exact questions (stems with anchors), cognitive testing, context/placement/mode effects; bands and the two reasons for them (respondents' mid-point; policy interest in the extremes); the floor of 50 / numerator 5; CV bands; "cannot be answered by proxy"; compare within an authority over time or with similar authorities; Dolan, Layard & Metcalfe's three accounts; OECD 2013 on scale, labels, order, context, mode — and a 2025 update with a shortened core module; the framework developed from 34,000 responses and revised after 118, with under-represented groups sought (the latter sentence is the user guide's, not the review's — caught by the verifier).
- **Validation.** The 2014 Standards are open: validity for an interpretation and a use; unitary; five sources that "do not represent distinct types". Cronbach & Meehl's root text. DeVellis & Thorpe's nine steps (cognitive interviewing now its own step); Boateng's conventions (10:1; 100/200/300/500; 0.70/0.90; 5–15 interviews). Face validity "at best a very weak kind of evidence" (open textbook) and measurable (Nevo, reconstructed). Member checking's own cautions (Birt; qualres.org on Lincoln & Guba; Candela). Formative v reflective (Jarvis; Diamantopoulos & Winklhofer). The chapter's own extension, marked: recognition gathered from the described as item judgement and explained reading is content and response-process evidence plus legitimacy.
- **Discordance.** Fetters, Curry & Creswell's fit, three outcomes, and the options for discordance including "challenging the validity of the constructs"; O'Cathain's four cells with silence.
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No empirical study of dot-voting or spectrum-line bias; no ticketing-records network study with validation; no measured "you said, we did" effect; no study comparing crowdsourced place scores with residents' own ratings; no sentence in De Nadai on representativeness.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §13. Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch09-agent-returns.md`. The orchestrator spot-checked fourteen sources the same day (ONS user guidance, FAQ and QMI; Génois & Barrat; Silver, Clark & Navarro 2010; SSID protocol; Lionello et al.; Williams & Vaske; Fetters et al.; the 2014 Standards PDF; Sjoberg et al.; the MSC guide; Barrett et al.; Orstad et al.) — forty strings, all held. The verifier made about 150 fetches across some 115 sources.

### What I learned

That the word carries three constructs and the literatures of all three refuse a blended number. That the perceived register has a tested way of asking — attributes, in situ, paired with the moment, a hundred answers, the spread reported — and that its word "vibrant" is a derived diagonal on the instrument's least evenly read axis. That a participatory measure is evidence of what participants placed, by the people who turned up, and becomes evidence about the place only when selection is stated and a spread shown; and that it becomes co-production only when what it changed is shown back. That co-presence is not a tie, that traces describe platforms' users, that signals are "how people appear to others", and that each is at most an indicator to be validated against a descriptor in the place and population concerned. That the official precedent for building a felt measure is complete and demanding — cognitive testing, context and mode, bands and why, a floor of fifty, comparison within history or declared peers, built and revised by asking — and that nothing in it is beyond a studio in principle. That validity belongs to an interpretation for a use and is argued from five sources, of which recognition by the described supplies two and legitimacy, and that recognition is gathered as member checking rather than announced. That discordance between felt and counted is a result with a procedure and a silence cell. And that the official floor and the brief's refusal of "the biggest" pull against each other for the smallest rooms — a conflict recorded, not resolved.

### What I left out, and why

- Böhme, Anderson, Lewicka, Jorgensen & Stedman, the ISO texts, Uhrich & Benkenstein, Jaidka's full text, MacKerron & Mourato's full text, OECD Chapter 1, Barrett's closing sections, the ONS 2011–12 testing reports, Ostrom 1996, Couper, Borgatti & Halgin, Borgatti, Carley & Krackhardt, Meng 2018 at source, Lazer's body, Crandall's body, Feld's body, Falk, Peterson & Kern, Kelley, Messick, Lucas & Donnellan, the Cochrane response-rate review, Moseley para 25, MSC Chapter Six: unreached; nothing quoted from them.
- Two praise-quote phrases on the Garcia-Mispireta page, first attributed to the publisher's description: removed on the verifier's finding.
- Version 1's illustrative finding about a place (the discordant case): the method shape kept, the finding dropped.
- All findings about any place in Reed et al., Livehoods, Sulis, De Nadai, Rhubart and Solymosi: not read for or reported.
- Any proposal of items, words, bands or display for the prototype: recorded as choices.
- The 60-second version is again long (~900 words); the question stands with Carly (raised in Entries 4–8).

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: the unreached canon; an empirical study of dot-voting bias; a validated ticketing-records network study; a measured "you said, we did" effect `[Prototype flag]`; cross-language and indoor/night validity of the soundscape attributes; known-groups and convergent tests `[Prototype flag]`; the ceiling among those present `[Prototype flag]`; the attribute words and the bands `[Validate with users]`; member checking as experienced `[Validate with users]`; the floor against the small room `[Revisit at end]`; six records `[Revisit at end]`; consent, the community-held layer, CARE and the AI Act boundary `[Revisit → Ch 10]`; the display of a spread, bands, sample and discordance `[Revisit → Ch 11]`; member checking when the members are a scene `[Revisit → Ch 12]`.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's recognition rule and gives it a procedure and a standing, and counterbalances its first-screen furniture with "who was asked and who was not" `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`; strengthens Chapter 2's three readers (the sceptic's questions are the five sources; the official reader has ONS4) and opens a thread from "being answered predicts taking part again"; strengthens Chapter 3's "show the shape" with the official reason for bands and adds the discordance matrix as a display object; strengthens Chapter 4 (platform caveat → measured skew; co-appearance caution → the network literature's own; a seventh neighbour to the six nothings, *silence*); strengthens Chapter 5 (construct before proxy; descriptor/indicator/index; reflective v formative named) and counterbalances its "recognition is necessary and not validity" to "two sources and measurable" `[Counterbalance → Ch 5]`; strengthens Chapter 6's "who is in the record" and small-number rules, and counterbalances its first rung for felt things (stated return did not predict revealed return; return is not attachment) `[Counterbalance → Ch 6]`; strengthens Chapter 7's own-history and peers rules in the office's words for a felt measure; strengthens Chapter 8 (every trace or signal is a dependency on terms; the song-feature source has closed; respondent burden is the attention cost) and opens whether measure-record line 12 and the service record's "what this changed" are one field; strengthens version 1's Chapter 4 in four positions, weakens the human dot plot as a measure, qualifies "validation by recognition" and "measure the vibe instead of safety"; opens threads for Chapter 0 (six records; "reconstructed"; "Standard (method)/(law)"; the measurement vocabulary), 10, 11 and 12. Decision Book: agrees with DTA-05/06, DSH-12/15/16, BRF-04; no rules on felt measures at all — candidate chapter *Felt data*. Design system: agrees on provenance and the "Dancefloor"/"Partner" tests; no felt-distribution component, sample-on-the-face, discordance matrix, "what this changed" element, testimony attribution, or "unvalidated indicator" status.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Dispatched together. The **verifier** made about 150 fetches across some 115 sources with control strings and reported nine wrong, fourteen truncated or glossed too strongly, two attributions blurred across documents, four markings wrong, three unverifiable, and some thirty "(read once)" marks it could lift. Corrected: Park et al.'s "these measures track the expression of affect" refers to earlier methods, not the study's own (the sentence had been reversed); two phrases on the Garcia-Mispireta page were a reviewer's praise, not the description's (removed); the OECD 2025 quotation had dropped "subjective well-being" and needed †; "six settings" is six datasets from five; "Christian Michels" is not on the page (the PDF prints "C. Michels"); Krueger & Schkade's 0.64 is a diary measure of net affect, not a single item; Sobolevsky does mention cash; "a third of the rank order" is not what a Spearman 0.328 means; the filled example's "9 per cent" arithmetic; the MSC "not-for" list, which the guide introduces as things MSC "can be used to address" where "there may be other less time-consuming ways" (the rule, the 60-second version, §5 and the flag reworded); the ONS question stems, which carry their anchors before the question mark; Génois & Barrat's conclusion, scoped to simulating contagion; Fumagalli's "when telephone interviews are included"; Frank's ArtPlace quotation, which continues with the funder's confidence in its measures; Chambers's two continuations; the National Academies' "are often regarded as"; Aletta's review reporting Axelsson rather than finding; Haklay's "can be fairly accurate"; Blodgett's metric (labelled recall); Place Pulse's 76% applying to the first dataset and "most of whom did not live there" unsupported; "the poorest district" → "one of the poorest"; Sulis's third check being mapped points of interest, called preliminary; Russell's 343 "students"; the ONS review and user guide run together; the Silver & Clark 2014 sentences attributed to 2010; † added to Kosmala, Beatty & Willis, Candela and Haklay 2016; Barrett's "more than a thousand studies" removed (not on the page as served); the AI Act's match to Barrett marked as the chapter's reading; Freeman recorded as reached by the agent and not re-opened by the verifier.

The **cold reader** (no project context) said the chapter decided that "vibe" is three things, only the perceived one gets numbers, and participation is evidence about participants until selection is stated — correctly — and named the hard line (the floor; the registers never collapsed) and the open choice (standard words or the scene's own). Its strongest objection had three parts and each was acted on. First, that a felt–counted disagreement is given three meanings with no rule for choosing — answered by distinguishing the convergent test of a *trace* against a descriptor (the trace fails) from the integration of two *descriptors* (discordance, reported), and by adding the interval-at-n sentence to section 13. Second, that the imported bar cannot be met and the floor of fifty removes exactly the small rooms the brief protects — accepted: the tension is now in section 14 and a `[Revisit at end]` flag, with the rule that an under-floor room is shown as present and unmeasured, never as absent. Third, that the chapter measures what attendees perceive and files what communities create under an unscored panel — partly accepted: the made register's method is now stated as "description with makers named; if a number is attempted, formative", and section 16 keeps the register choice open while section 17 marks its own choice as illustrative. Also changed on its findings: eight words used in more than one sense — "register", "measure", "instrument", "indicator", "co-produced", "recognition", "record", "face" — defined in Before you start and made consistent, with section 5 renamed *The participatory family*; "the brief", "the house framework" and "the data contract" defined; "one of five sources" → "supplies two of the five"; "never a point" → "never a point alone"; "A place does not have a vibe" → "a vibe score"; the four cells and the three outcomes distinguished; centrality rule and checklist aligned; "trace shown as a vibe" → "as an indicator of a vibe"; the made-register number reconciled with the formative paragraph; the reflective example corrected (calm and vibrant are not on one axis); "a line for each of the five" mapped to lines; ESM distinguished from the in-situ questionnaire; "settled" → "stated"; the cut-points' two grounds; "designed with 34,000" → the framework developed from; the illustration stripped of a wording and an expected finding; sector-supplying examples marked as the chapter's; the duplicate Decision Book and design-system paragraphs collapsed into the conflict log. Not changed: the 60-second version's length (with Carly); the number of rules; the clinchers, most of which stay.

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×6. `[Prototype flag]` ×6 (private v public self-placement; null-model test of co-occurrence; a "you said, we did" experiment subject to Chapter 10; known-groups and convergent tests; the ceiling; the human dot plot's conditions). `[Validate with users]` ×3. `[Revisit at end]` ×2 (the floor and the small room; six records). `[Revisit → Ch 10]`, `[→ Ch 11]`, `[→ Ch 12]`, `[→ Ch 4]`. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`, `[→ Ch 5]`, `[→ Ch 6]`. `[Rich reference]` ×8.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **Mitchell et al. 2020, the SSID protocol, with the Soundscapy tutorial.** The one complete worked example of turning a felt quality of a place into an in-situ measure: the eight attributes, the hundred answers per location, the recording beside the respondent, the formulae, and the authors' own case against reporting a median. Read it to see what "asking well" costs.
2. **ONS *Personal well-being user guidance*, *frequently asked questions* and the QMI.** The exact questions, the cognitive testing, the context and mode effects, the bands and the two reasons for them, the floor of fifty. An afternoon; it is the yardstick any vibe measure will be judged against by the official reader.
3. **AERA/APA/NCME *Standards* (2014), Chapter 1** — open at testingstandards.net. Validity belongs to an interpretation for a use; five sources of evidence. The sentence to carry: "It is the interpretations of test scores for proposed uses that are evaluated, not the test itself."
4. **Génois & Barrat 2018** (open). The direct test of co-presence as a proxy for contact. The data contract's "co-appearance is not a social tie", with numbers.
5. **Brown 2017**, *Transactions in GIS* (abstract; the full text is a library pull). Who marks, how much, and where, from the field's own leading researcher — the three findings every participatory map should print beside itself.
6. **Davies & Dart 2005, the MSC guide** (open). A complete participatory procedure that says what it is less suited to and builds feeding back into the method; Chapter Six on validity and voice still needs a full copy.
7. **Sjoberg, Mellon & Peixoto 2017** (abstract). The behavioural case for "what this changed": being answered predicts taking part again, at n = 399,364, in Britain.
8. **Williams & Vaske 2003** (open PDF). What "tested before it was counted" looks like for a felt bond with a place — and the ceiling you will meet when you ask people who are already there.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Give every measurement word one sense before drafting, and check the family names against the glossary.** Section 5 was named by a word the glossary reserved for something stricter; eight words carried two senses each. The term list should be written first and the headings checked against it last.
- **A praise quote on a publisher's page is not the description.** Two phrases were taken from a reviewer's blurb. On a publisher page, quote only the description and say so.
- **"These measures" needs its antecedent.** A sentence contrasting earlier methods with the study's own was read backwards. Where a quotation begins with a pronoun or a demonstrative, read the sentence before it.
- **A not-for list is read with its lead-in.** The MSC guide's list of things MSC "can be used to address" less efficiently became "not for" in four places. Quote the sentence that introduces a list before quoting the list.
- **Two documents from one publisher are two sources.** The ONS review and the ONS user guide; Silver & Clark 2010 and 2014. Each sentence names its page.
- **A stem is not a question.** The ONS questions carry their anchors before the question mark; the stems with "?" are not on the page.
- **The verifier's unreached list is the ledger's.** Freeman was reached by the agent and not by the verifier; the ledger now says both.
- **Record a tension the chapter cannot resolve as a tension.** The floor of fifty and the brief's refusal of "the biggest" conflict for the smallest rooms; a `[Revisit at end]` flag and a holding rule (present and unmeasured, never absent) is the right shape, not a quiet choice.
- **Dispatch cold reader and verifier together.** Again the right call; two reports, one script, and the reports found different things.
