## Entry 10 · Chapter 10 — Safety, ethics and consent

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 9. Second chapter of Part 2.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** How does a public dashboard tell the story of the communities in its data without exposing or exploiting them — what the disclosure methods do and cost, what UK law requires of a private platform, what consent and withdrawal mean when the unit is a scene and not a person, who may speak for a scene, how hostile contributors and gaming are met, and what the data-justice frames add — and where the lines are that are not crossed?

**Sub-questions.**
1. Disclosure control, in plain terms and at query time. What k-anonymity, l-diversity and t-closeness promise and fail at; what differential privacy promises, costs and has cost (the US census controversy and its accuracy evidence); how the UK census protects small cells (targeted record swapping; cell-key perturbation; fixed classifications; the declared no-cumulative-tracking trade-off); what re-identification has been shown to do with location and behaviour (four spatio-temporal points; fifteen attributes; the taxi and fitness-map cases); differencing attacks in interactive table builders; intruder testing; the official thresholds Chapter 6 already carries and how they are set; synthetic data; group privacy — harm to a group from an aggregate that identifies nobody.
2. UK law for a private platform. UK GDPR and the DPA 2018: personal data and its edges; special-category data (sexual orientation, ethnicity, religion, health) and inference; lawful bases — consent (and withdrawal "as easy as to give") against legitimate interests and the three-part test; the research provisions; data protection by design; the DPIA and the ICO's high-risk list (location tracking, innovative technology, vulnerable people, invisible processing); the ICO's 2025 anonymisation guidance and the motivated-intruder test; pseudonymisation; the Data (Use and Access) Act 2025 changes; PECR for analytics; what "public but not publicised" is in law.
3. Other legal and regulatory boundaries. The Equality Act 2010 for a private service provider (Part 3; indirect discrimination; harassment; positive action; the public-sector duty that does *not* apply); the Online Safety Act 2023 and whether a small interactive site with contributed content is a user-to-user service, with Ofcom's duties for small services; the Children's Code for a service "likely to be accessed by children"; the EU AI Act boundary Chapter 9 found; the open licences' personal-data exclusion (Chapter 8).
4. Consent, withdrawal and voice at scene level. Collective consent and community consultation in research ethics (genomics; participatory research guidelines); the CARE principles in full and Indigenous data sovereignty as the model, with care about borrowing; participatory data stewardship (the Ada Lovelace ladder: inform, consult, involve, collaborate, empower); data trusts and cooperatives; contextual integrity (information flows appropriate to their context) and the "but the data is already public" critique; withdrawal against the archive (Chapter 8 commits every fetch; Chapter 9's "what this changed" is a record that outlives the consent); who speaks for a scene — representation, gatekeeping, the one per cent (Chapter 9); the "community-held, not published" layer and its precedents outside culture (sensitive-species location obscuring; archaeological site withholding; crime-map snapping — Chapter 8).
5. Harm. Typologies of data harm (privacy harms; the DPIA harm list); the harms a map does to a place — territorial stigmatisation, the historic redlining maps and their measured long-run effects, crime maps and property prices, "coordinates for speculation" (data read as an investment signal; the gentrification-indicator literature read as a risk); harm by visibility to informal or underground scenes; harm by omission (the unlit-is-not-dead rule; Chapter 4's nothings); the ecological fallacy as a harm when a place is labelled by its average.
6. Hostile contributors and gaming. Content moderation as a discipline (its "virtues"; reporting; rate and reputation); brigading (Chapter 9's cases); safety-by-design principles; harassment and outing risk for marginalised communities when a space is made findable; location-privacy failures of apps serving such communities (trilateration cases) read as technology; the audit as an ethical duty (Chapter 5).
7. The frames. Data justice (Taylor; Dencik et al.); data feminism's seven principles (open text); design justice (open text); responsible-data practice (do-no-harm policies); the Belmont principles and the UK research-ethics framework as the baseline; what each frame adds to the lines, and what none of them settles.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Sweeney 2002 (k-anonymity); Machanavajjhala et al. 2007 (l-diversity); Li, Li & Venkatasubramanian 2007 (t-closeness); Dwork 2006 and Dwork & Roth 2014 (differential privacy); Abowd 2018 and the 2020 Census TopDown documentation; Ruggles et al. 2019; Kenny et al. 2021 (*Science Advances*) and Santos-Lozada et al. 2020 on census accuracy; ONS Census 2021 statistical disclosure control methodology and the flexible table builder's protections; GSS/ONS disclosure control guidance for tables; Elliot, Mackey & O'Hara, *The Anonymisation Decision-Making Framework* (UKAN, 2nd ed. 2020); ONS intruder testing; Narayanan & Shmatikov 2008; de Montjoye et al. 2013 and 2015; Rocher, Hendrickx & de Montjoye 2019; Culnane, Rubinstein & Teague 2017; the Strava heat-map case (2018) and the NYC taxi hashes (2014); Taylor, Floridi & van der Sloot (eds) *Group Privacy* 2017; Mittelstadt 2017; UK GDPR Articles 4, 6, 7, 9, 25, 35, 89 and Recital 26; DPA 2018 Schedule 1; ICO guidance on consent, legitimate interests, special category data, DPIAs, research, and *Anonymisation* (28 March 2025); the Data (Use and Access) Act 2025; PECR; Equality Act 2010 Parts 3 and 11 and EHRC's services code; Online Safety Act 2023 and Ofcom's illegal-harms and small-services guidance; the ICO Children's Code; Carroll et al. 2020 and GIDA; OCAP; Ada Lovelace Institute 2021 *Participatory data stewardship*; ODI on data trusts; Nissenbaum 2004/2010; boyd & Crawford 2012; Zimmer 2010; Metcalf & Crawford 2016; ESRC framework for research ethics; ICPHR 2013 participatory-research ethics; the Belmont Report; eBird's sensitive-species policy and iNaturalist's coordinate obscuring; Historic England on withholding site locations; Citron & Solove 2022; Wacquant 2007; Nelson et al. *Mapping Inequality* and Aaronson, Hartley & Mazumder 2021 on HOLC; Glaeser, Kim & Luca 2018; Grimmelmann 2015; eSafety Commissioner's Safety by Design; Hoang et al. on trilateration; Taylor 2017; Dencik, Hintz & Cable 2016; D'Ignazio & Klein 2020 (open); Costanza-Chock 2020 (open); Responsible Data / Oxfam 2015; version 1's Chapters 4, 7 and 8 (archived) for the house positions (threshold three; secondary suppression; fixed categories; suppression at query time with the reason; the adversarial filter walk; consent to be found; the money view shows only consented scenes; community-held as a kind of nothing); the Vibe Setting Framework (confidential; cited, never reproduced); the house's Form 696 history — Carly's knowledge, entering as frame, not researched.

**What I expect to find.** That the disclosure methods are well understood and that their costs are measured — differential privacy's accuracy cost at small geographies is documented and contested; k-anonymity fails against background knowledge; the UK census's approach is pragmatic and declares what it does not do — so the chapter's job is plain terms and the query-time rule, not new method. That re-identification from location and behaviour is easier than intuition says (four points; fifteen attributes) and that a scene is a small population with high background knowledge, which Chapter 6 already found. That UK law gives a private studio a workable basis (legitimate interests with a DPIA, or consent) and some hard edges (special-category inference; children; the Online Safety Act if contributions are hosted). That "consent at scene level" has no legal form and several ethical precedents (community consultation; CARE; participatory stewardship), none of which settles who speaks. That the community-held layer has clean precedents in conservation and heritage — known, held, not shown — that a dashboard can borrow. That maps have measurably harmed places and that the harm runs through labelling and through speculation, which bears on Chapter 7's comparisons and Chapter 11's first screen. That the frames (data justice, data feminism, design justice) sharpen the questions and leave the lines to be drawn by the people in the data. And that the floor-of-fifty against the small room, carried from Chapter 9, is answered here partly by the disclosure methods (perturbation; ranges) and partly by the community-held layer, not by choosing one.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) disclosure control and re-identification — the methods, their costs, the census precedents, the attacks, group privacy; (B) UK data-protection law for a private platform — UK GDPR/DPA, special category and inference, lawful bases and withdrawal, DPIA, the ICO's 2025 anonymisation guidance, research provisions, the 2025 Act, PECR; (C) consent, voice and governance at community level — collective consent, CARE and its borrowing, participatory stewardship, data trusts, contextual integrity and "already public", withdrawal against the archive, the community-held precedents, who speaks; (D) the other boundaries and the harms — Equality Act for a private provider, Online Safety Act and Ofcom's small-services duties, the Children's Code, harm typologies, maps that harmed places, speculation, moderation and safety by design, trilateration cases, the justice frames.

### Threads pulled

- **The statutory map moved under the chapter.** Agent B's headline: Article 89 and DPA s.19 omitted on 5 February 2026 (S.I. 2026/82) and replaced by Chapter 8A, Articles 84A–84D ("RAS purposes"; the two exclusions — substantial damage or distress; measures or decisions about a particular data subject — survive, with a minimisation requirement); Article 14(5)(b) omitted and the general exemption now carrying a duty to publish (14(7)); the research and statistics definitions and broad consent in Article 4(2)–(7); the office of Information Commissioner abolished and the Information Commission in place from 30 September 2026 (S.I. 2026/1015), six days before the read. The regulator's pages were part updated (lawful bases, purpose limitation, cookies) and part not (research, consent, DPIA, transparency, special category), most "under review". The verifier then found a further move the agents had missed: OSA s.10(3A), inserted 29 June 2026 by the Crime and Policing Act 2026, a 48-hour outer limit for intimate-image reports. Rule: every statutory citation carries the date read; the record is re-read on a schedule.
- **The anonymisation question and the statistics question became one question.** New Article 4(5): statistical purposes require "aggregate data that is not personal data" and no use "in support of measures or decisions with respect to a particular data subject". A dashboard's outputs are statistical only if anonymous and only if not used to act on a person; Chapter 1's definition (a decision for a named reader) sits on the second limb. Recorded as open, for counsel.
- **Two ICO inference passages pull in different directions.** For anonymisation, a correct "educated guess" from anonymous information is not a disclosure; for special category data, an *intended* inference is Article 9 processing "regardless of how confident you are". The brief expected a certainty test; the page has an intention test. The separating variables, on the regulator's own wording: intention; attachment to an identifiable person. Onlookers' inferences go to fairness. The house's "who is in the room" vocabulary sits on this line.
- **Consent as a basis for what is published.** The ICO's valid-consent page (4 August 2026; research heading): "If you would not be able to fully action a withdrawal of consent … then you should not use consent as your lawful basis"; "There is no exemption to this for scientific research"; "no such thing as 'evolving' consent". Article 8A(4): data collected on consent cannot take the research-and-statistics compatibility route. The cold reader objected that the chapter's rule ("not consent for anything published") was the chapter's legal reading presented as the regulator's; draft 2 states both readings (consent with a declared cut-off is the funder's and the biobank's own practice) and takes the second as a house stance.
- **The disclosure toolkit, with prices attached.** Sweeney (87% is a 1990 summary-table estimate in a working paper); ℓ-diversity's two attacks; t-closeness's own limit; swapping 7–10% and ~14% of cells perturbed in Census 2021; "protects by doubt, not absence"; the census builder caps variables, perturbs the same cell the same way, and "will not provide details of which rule"; parameters deliberately kept private (EAP168). Abowd & Hawes: "suppression rules disproportionately impact small population groups and small areas" — the brief's "aggregation of the biggest" as a statistical mechanism. Synthetic data (six levels; ε = 1 for the Turing pilot).
- **Formal privacy's promise and non-promise.** Dwork & Roth: "a promise, made by a data holder, or curator, to a data subject"; the smoker passage; "a definition, not an algorithm"; group privacy deteriorates as kε (the agent's "weakens" was not the book's word — caught by the verifier). Census 2020: ε = 19.61, "exponentially higher" than 12.2 after accuracy objections; Kenny et al.: undercounts mixed-race and mixed-partisan precincts yet "does not prevent accurate predictions of an individual's race and ethnicity"; Ruggles; boyd & Sarathy "epistemic". Reconstruction: Dinur–Nissim Ω(√n); 7.7 billion statistics; 219 million / 52 million (Hawes — the 46/71/38/17% are not on the page); 97 million exact, 3.4 million reidentifiable at 95% (abstract); 44% unique on sex and single year of age at block level.
- **The query-time problem.** ONS's most direct guidance on online tools sits in the births-and-deaths policy §4.4: stricter than static outputs; "A minimum cell count equal to five is suggested"; registration; a log by user audited for suspicious activity; a legal-obligations acknowledgement; "If possible", a limit on similar tables. The census builder states none of that and relies on consistent perturbation, caps and unpublished rules. Two models, not one. Hoang et al.'s trilateration case (read as technology): hiding one field did not stop location "up to the victim's building". ONS intruder testing: "friendly intruders", "around a half day", confidence per claim, a small number of low-confidence correct claims is a good result. ADF: "not a property of the data"; the data environment; ten components. ICO release model: "when you release information publicly, you lose control of the data".
- **Consent at community level has no legal form and a consistent ethics literature.** Dickert & Sugarman: consultation is not consent; valid community consent needs "a legitimate political system"; "disease-based communities and many social groups typically lack a political structure". UNESCO 6(3) and CIOMS: community agreement never substitutes for individual consent. DGA Recital 31: rights are personal and cannot be waived. The CBPR guide names collective consent and ties it to renegotiation. Three words kept apart — consult, govern, agree — and "consent at scene level" retired.
- **The sovereignty models, and the care in borrowing.** CARE's authors extend the principles as "a normative foundation" to "minority groups, distinctive communities, or other collectives" — more permissive than expected; FNIGC says OCAP is "specifically First Nations—not Indigenous" — narrower than expected, and therefore stronger; Kukutai & Taylor ground the claim in "inherent rights to self-determination as sovereign entities"; Te Mana Raraunga 5.2: "Less defined types of consent shall be balanced by stronger governance arrangements". Rule: questions and structure, never authority. Rambaldi et al.'s "Who? and Whose?" questions; Ada Lovelace's five levels and the redefinition of legitimacy; data trusts (the ODI's definition changed between 2018 and 2019); gatekeepers (McAreavey & Das); the slogan "nothing about us without us" is not in the Design Justice principles and its origin is not on Charlton's publisher page.
- **Withdrawal does not reach the archive.** ESRC's declared cut-off; UK Biobank's three tiers and "it would not be possible to remove data from research that had already taken place"; Zenodo's tombstone; TNA's temporary takedown; Internet Archive's "no guarantees"; Pro Git "only add data"; GitHub's current wording ("You cannot remove sensitive data from other users' clones") — the "consider … compromised" sentence is gone from the page.
- **Known but not shown has precedents, and a form.** eBird (400 km² cells; hidden from site-level output; reviewers still see; partners consulted); iNaturalist (0.2° cells; private setting; taxon geoprivacy); NBN Atlas (lists agreed with statutory bodies; partners "do not have to make their own assessments"); Treasure Act code (10 m square; findspots not public at inquest); EIR reg 12 (presumption of openness; named harm; public-interest weighing — by analogy); ICO 2014 crime mapping (heat maps and blocks; "not 'fair game'"); Redfin's decision not to add crime data. The cold reader's strongest objection: a held status marked on a single room is itself a disclosure. Draft 2 moves the status to the category, coarsens the units, tests the held layer by the walk against public lists, and restates the forum's agreement as a condition the house sets on itself rather than a consent.
- **Harm to places is named by the statistical system, not the data-protection regulator.** Neither the ICO's DPIA harm list nor its 2022 taxonomy names stigma of place; UKSA's geospatial ethics guidance does ("blight a geographic area or community"; "redlined"; the ecological fallacy; "Red traditionally spells danger"; "Missing data is not the same as a zero value"; "should not necessarily be used as a rationale for avoiding local or small area data altogether"); UKSA public good: "harm can also include things like stigmatisation". HOLC: effects 15–30% / 40% (working paper, not the published abstract) against Fishback's 4–20% (a different quantity — bias in construction). Crime maps: a 2012 review (police.uk published since December 2008 — the first draft's "2011" was wrong); Braakmann's price of street-level risk (not a test of publication); ICO 2014 foresaw estate agents and insurers. Speculation: Glaeser, Kim & Luca — venue data "a leading indicator for housing price changes" (the 29.2% is in a different paper, on nowcasting local activity — caught by the verifier); Barron et al. Visibility: NCC's 135 third parties; Datatilsynet's 65 million NOK upheld October 2025 (complainant's account; the authority's page unreached); Jernigan & Mistree (full text open, not abstract); Ruberg & Ruelos.
- **The other boundaries.** Equality Act s.29 "for payment or not"; s.29(8) excludes religion or belief and sexual orientation from Part 3 harassment; s.149 does not reach a private service unless a public function; s.20(6) accessible format; the 2018 regulations exclude "online maps and mapping services" and bind public bodies (WCAG 2.2 AA is named by the GDS guidance, not the regulations — caught by the verifier); Sch 25 hosting. OSA: s.3(1) definition; Sch 1 para 4 limited-functionality exemption turns on "relating to provider content"; no size exemption ("services run by micro businesses"); three months from launch; low-risk / multi-risk / large (7 million) tiers; measures for all (named individual; moderation; complaints; terms); £18m / 10%. Children's Code "more probable than not" against OSA's "significant number … based on evidence about who actually uses". PECR Sch A1 para 5: "how your service is used, not about who uses it".
- **Moderation and the frames.** Grimmelmann's four verbs and four adverb pairs; Discourse trust levels as pricing ex ante; Pew (LGB adults "seven-in-ten"; 51% severe) and Amnesty (76% changed how they use platforms) on who is driven away; eSafety's three principles. Taylor's three pillars and the group level; Dencik; Data Feminism's seven; Design Justice's "impact on the community over the intentions of the designer"; Responsible Data's "unintended consequences"; Nissenbaum's two norms; boyd & Crawford's "in public" v "being public" (published heading "does not", draft "Doesn't"); Metcalf & Crawford on public data's secondary uses; NSDEC; MRS; AoIR "guidelines, not recipes".
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No ICO statement on anonymisation of location data or small communities as such; no published protocol for intruder-testing an interactive view; no verified case of a published map withdrawn; no example of a data agreement between a community group and a public body; no ONS sexual-orientation geography floor established; no source on whether a dashboard is research or statistics; the ESRC framework has no sentence for a body without an ethics committee.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §14. Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch10-agent-returns.md`. The orchestrator spot-checked seventeen sources the same day (Art 84C; Art 4; ICO anonymisation effectiveness; ICO consent — two pages; ICO special category; ONS births and deaths policy; ONS Census 2021 protection; Dwork & Roth; Kenny et al.; Rocher et al.; ADF 2nd ed.; Equality Act s.29; OSA Schedule 1; Dickert & Sugarman; Ada Lovelace; eBird; UKSA geospatial ethics — three pages) — about a hundred strings with controls; every quoted string found, four on a sibling page of the one first tried. The verifier made about two hundred fetches and confirmed some six hundred strings, figures, dates and section numbers.

### What I learned

That "exposing" and "exploiting" belong to two literatures, that the law protects persons and the ethics literature protects participants, and that a dashboard about a community is about neither. That anonymity is a property of data in an environment and a public release is the environment in which control is lost, so the test is an intruder who knows the place, and for a scene that is a regular — and, the chapter adds, a public body with power over the venues. That identity, attribute and group disclosure are three things, that the toolkit addresses two, and that the strongest formal tool protects participation and not description, so the mixed-race precinct paid the accuracy cost and got no privacy benefit. That a filter is a table builder, that the UK's most direct official guidance on such tools asks for a higher floor, registration, a log and a limit on similar tables, and that the national census builder does none of that and relies on consistent perturbation and unpublished rules. That the law moved in four places between the brief and the read, and a fifth between the agents and the verifier. That consent is a person's act, that communities are consulted, govern and agree, and that the models for a community holding its data are cited for their questions and never borrowed for their authority. That withdrawal is graded and the irreversible part is named. That "known but not shown" has a form in conservation, heritage and environmental law — category-level status, coarsened units, named process, public reason, review date — and that a status marked on a unit is a disclosure. That harm to places runs through representation, is named by the statistical system and not by the data-protection regulator, and has been measured in maps that identified nobody. That a free, small, non-commercial site is inside data-protection and equality law and inside online-safety law if the public can write what the public sees. And that the book cannot say whether a dashboard is research or statistics in the statute's sense; counsel can.

### What I left out, and why

- Weijer & Emanuel; Cooke & Kothari; Banks et al.; Walter & Suina; Floridi 2014; the Group Privacy body; Golle; Ohm; the map re-identification papers; the census intruder-test results; Kosinski et al.; Gillespie; Mapping Inequality; Cortright; Wacquant's bodies; Robinson's conclusion; the EHRC code; the PAS policy; Historic England; Datatilsynet's decision; the Synack write-up; the UK Web Archive policy; Oxfam's text; NIH/CDC definitions; WP248rev01; the Protection of Children Codes; OSA Schedule 3; EqA s.19A and Sch 3; DPA Sch 2 Pt 5; the re-identification offences; Articles 10 and 22A–22D: unreached or not pursued; nothing quoted from them.
- Journalism on Strava, AOL, the Journal News map and police.uk's launch: blocked; the AOL case recorded through an encyclopaedia and the private person not re-identified by the chapter.
- The invented text the fetch reader produced (an FDA definition; an ODI six-part list; Nissenbaum page numbers): caught and not used.
- Any finding about a place, scene or community in any case read (Grindr, Livehoods-type studies, the crime-map price study, the Chicago block groups, the HOLC cities): read for mechanism only.
- Any legal advice: the chapter reports and marks open questions for counsel (the research-provisions fit; a nameable business; information law and partners).
- Form 696: Carly's knowledge; enters as frame in §18 only; not researched.
- The 60-second version is again long (~1,400 words before the truths were split into sentences); the question stands with Carly.

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: the unreached canon; a protocol for intruder-testing an interactive view `[Prototype flag]`; the floor per view by knowability `[Prototype flag]`; overlapping-view limits in practice `[Prototype flag]`; the three nothings on one face `[Prototype flag]` `[Revisit → Ch 11]`; the money view as advocacy or listing `[Validate with users]`; who governs and who stands at the door `[Validate with users]`; withdrawal tiers said back `[Validate with users]`; kind not rule `[Validate with users]`; disagreement within a scene `[Validate with users]`; a nameable business; information law and a public-body partner; release cadence and differencing across time; the repository's never-commit list; broad consent; benefit; a minimum version; the research-provisions fit `[Further research]`; seven records and the room under both floors `[Revisit at end]`.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's trust failure and opens a thread on its definition against "measures or decisions" `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`; strengthens Chapter 2's readers with "who could find themselves in this?" and the official reader as a potential intruder; strengthens Chapter 3's shape and colour rules with the stigma warnings and adds the three nothings as a display object; strengthens Chapter 4 and discharges its "community-held" carry-forward, counterbalancing "says why" to "says the kind" `[Counterbalance → Ch 4]`; strengthens Chapter 5's legitimacy with the Ada Lovelace definition; strengthens Chapter 6's knowability rule in the regulator's words and counterbalances its single threshold with a floor per view `[Counterbalance → Ch 6]`; strengthens Chapter 7's no-ranking rule with measured map harm and the price-signal evidence; strengthens Chapter 8 (terms as transparency duties; the service record's "kept under review"; the analytics rule; the committed repository as the reason withdrawal is graded); strengthens Chapter 9 (the floor of fifty is a precision rule; gating's cost counted in line 5; "what this changed" outlives consent) and retires "consent at scene level" `[Counterbalance → Ch 9]`; strengthens version 1 on secondary suppression, fixed categories, the walk and community-held, changes threshold three and "with the reason", and retires "consent to be found" and "only consented scenes" as worded. Decision Book: agrees on suppression, DSH-15/16, provenance, BRF-04; gaps on query-time protection, the walk, the held status, lawful basis, the DPIA's consultation step, the OSA, withdrawal tiers, harm to places — a candidate chapter, *Safeguards*. Design system: text-coded status chips are the right furniture; gaps logged (held-status component; three nothings; record beside a panel; withdrawal notice; stigma test in colour).

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Dispatched one after the other this time (the cold reader first; a method slip — see notes). The **verifier** made about two hundred fetches and reported five wrong, about a dozen truncated or reshaped, five misattributed, a handful of markings, and a long list of "(read once)" marks it could lift. Corrected: OSA s.10(3A)'s 48-hour limit (29 June 2026) added; Chainey & Tompson's review re-dated and no longer called the maps' own evaluation; "weakens" taken out of quotation marks ("deteriorates" is the book's word); Ofcom's children's-access sentence quoted from the right page; Strava's post title; "Where appropriate," restored to Article 35(9) in the 60-second version and truth 7; "If possible," restored to the ONS similar-tables sentence and the omitted legal-obligations bullet added; the DPIA list's "when combined with any other criterion" carried into the 60-second version; S.I. 2018/952's "bodies governed by public law" given its three alternative tests; Amnesty's 32% base stated; Article 7(3) "four sentences"; Article 21 "(ea)" unbracketed; point (11) of Article 4(1); capitalisation of two mid-sentence quotations; the GDS sentence's Northern Ireland tail; Grimmelmann's adverbs as sub-headings; the ICO review example as yearly information requests with three reasons; "usually numbers less than three" placed in its list; Census page "last revised"; the "only fully post-Act guidance" claim withdrawn; the rounding sentences attributed to the Handbook; the 29.2% to the nowcasting paper; "sovereign entities" to Kukutai & Taylor in the 60-second version and Belief seven; WCAG 2.2 AA to the GDS guidance; Local Contexts as "a related initiative"; Hawes's title marked unconfirmed; TopDown's sentence to the PDF; Jernigan & Mistree lifted from †; UK Biobank's date; and 130 ledger entries marked "verified".

The **cold reader** (no project context) said what the chapter decided correctly, named the hard line (every floor paired with a form that keeps the small present) and the open choice (the floor's value), and found the headline evidence. Its strongest objection changed the chapter: a held status marked on a single room, with its reason in public, is itself a disclosure — it tells an onlooker which rooms have something to protect, publishes the inference the holding meant to prevent, and can be undone by subtracting the dashboard from the listings that already name the rooms; the precedents coarsen units and declare the status at the level of a list, never a marker; and "the people in it chose" was a consent by another name, performed by a self-constituted forum reached through a door. Draft 2 moves the held status to the category, coarsens the units, tests the held layer by the walk against public lists, restates the forum's agreement as a condition the house sets on itself, and rewrites the sixth truth, the record's line 9 and the rule. Its second objection — that truth 4's consent rule was the chapter's legal reading presented as the regulator's — produced a paragraph that states both readings and a rule re-tagged as a stance. It also found a rule written backwards (corrected), "withheld" doing duty for all three nothings ("not shown" is now the umbrella), eighteen sentences that read as advice (reworded as reporting or house rules, and the DPIA rule re-tagged Nightlife Culture), the cross-references to "consultation in section 12" (now 11), the 60-second version's "unanimous", "a statutory list", "the law does not address at all" and "repealed" (each corrected), four "---" rules without a blank line (a rendering bug; fixed), and ten things missing — a nameable business, the official reader as adversary, information law and partners, dissent within a scene, benefit, broad consent, release cadence, the repository, security and a minimum version — which are now paragraphs in §18 and flags. What it asked for and did not get: a shorter preamble and 60-second version (left; the question stands with Carly); fewer Fixed rules (left; the tiering question is for the Decision Book's candidate chapter).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×14. `[Prototype flag]` ×5 (the walk on the prototype; the floor per view by knowability; overlapping-view limits; the three nothings on one face; differencing across releases). `[Validate with users]` ×5. `[Revisit at end]` ×2 (seven records; the room under both floors). `[Revisit → Ch 11]` ×3, `[→ Ch 12]`. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`, `[→ Ch 4]`, `[→ Ch 6]`, `[→ Ch 9]`. `[Rich reference]` ×10.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **ICO, *How do we ensure anonymisation is effective?*** (published 28 March 2025; under review). The regulator's whole method in one page: the spectrum, the motivated intruder, the release model, the educated-guess line, the NHS k = 5, the review example. An hour.
2. **ONS, *Policy on protecting confidentiality in tables of birth and death statistics*.** §4.2.2 on how a floor is actually set, §4.4 on online tools (the UK's most direct official words on a dashboard's problem), Annex B.2 on differencing.
3. **Elliot, Mackey & O'Hara, *The Anonymisation Decision-Making Framework*, 2nd ed.** (UKAN 2020; CC-BY). "Not a property of the data"; the data environment; the ten components, three of which are about people and one about time.
4. **UK GDPR Articles 4(2)–(7), 8A and 84A–84D as amended**, at legislation.gov.uk. The post-2025 architecture, read in the text rather than in anyone's summary.
5. **Dickert & Sugarman 2005** (open, four pages). Consultation is not consent; when community consent is valid; the four goals of consultation.
6. **Rambaldi, Chambers, McCall & Fox 2006** (IIED, open). The "Who? and Whose?" questions for anyone putting a community on a map.
7. **UK Statistics Authority, *Ethical considerations in the use of geospatial data*** (2021). Redlining, the ecological fallacy, blight, disclosure by location, and colour, in one short official document.
8. **Grimmelmann 2015, *The Virtues of Moderation*** (open). Four verbs, four adverb pairs; every contribution-gating choice in one grammar.
9. **Ofcom, *Illegal content duties under the Online Safety Act*** (February 2026) with the user-to-user Codes. The tiers and the measures for every service, in the regulator's words.
10. **Ada Lovelace Institute, *Participatory data stewardship*** (2021). Five levels with mechanisms, and the redefinition of legitimacy as "the broad base of public support".

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Dispatch cold reader and verifier in one message.** This sprint sent the cold reader first and the verifier after; the reports did not conflict, but the verifier's run could have overlapped the cold read. The Entry 9 note stands and was not followed; follow it.
- **Apply the chapter's own rules to its own resolution.** The held layer's status was proposed as a marker on a unit, which the chapter's disclosure sections forbid for any other marker. Before the symmetry section, run each proposed house form through the chapter's own tests.
- **A legal reading is marked as the chapter's.** The consent rule was the chapter's inference from a research-specific regulator sentence; it became a Fixed rule attributed to the regulator. Where a rule rests on an inference from law or guidance, say "the chapter's reading", give the alternative, and tag it a stance.
- **Check statutory pages for amendments newer than the agents' read.** The 48-hour limit was inserted on 29 June 2026 and the agent read the page in October without reporting it; the verifier found it. Read every F-note on every statutory page cited, not only the banner.
- **A section heading on a page is not the article's title.** Hawes's HDSR title was taken from a section heading. Confirm titles at the publisher's record.
- **A companion paper is a different paper.** The 29.2% sat in a nowcasting paper, not the gentrification paper; name the paper the number is in.
- **"Where appropriate" and "If possible" are part of the sentence.** Two qualifiers were cut from statutory and official sentences in the summary and the truths; the body had them. Quote the qualifier wherever the sentence is quoted.
- **One umbrella word for the kinds, and a different word for each kind.** "Withheld" was both a kind of nothing and the word for all three. Choose the umbrella ("not shown") before drafting.
- **Count the spot-checks before writing the number.** The preamble's first count was wrong (fifteen; fifty-eight) and was corrected after the checks were tallied.
- **Mark the kinds the legend lacks before using them.** "Standard (method)", "(international)", "(regulator, code)" and "(law, by analogy)" appeared in the table before the preamble defined them.

---

## Entry 11 · Chapter 11 — The first screen and the path through it

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 10. First chapter of Part 3, The reading experience.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What does a dashboard say before anyone touches it, and to whom; how are an authored account and a reader's own exploration placed in one display so that neither is a toll on the other; what must an interaction buy to justify existing, and what does each cost; how does a state a reader reaches become something they can cite a year later; and how does a display read from the data layer and respond to it — the records beneath the numbers, the three nothings, a felt distribution, a map that does not stigmatise — so that the face carries what the chapters behind it require without becoming a methodology page?

**Sub-questions.**
1. The first screen as the product. What the empirical dashboard literature says a dashboard is and what its first screen does (the surveys of built dashboards; the design-pattern vocabularies; the studies of how dashboard users actually work); the public design systems' guidance on page structure and starting points (GOV.UK, ONS, NHS); version 1's audit of ten live instruments and its six landing patterns, re-tested against evidence rather than against the audit; "no configuration before meaning"; mobile first because there is no hover; figures before script (Chapter 8); what Chapter 3 already settled (reach, defaults as advice, overview-first audited, the glance) and is cited and not re-argued.
2. Story and exploration. The narrative-visualisation taxonomy and its evidence status (genres; author- and reader-driven; the martini glass; sequence; rhetoric); the storytelling null at source; what linking text to its view does to recall; prediction before reveal; scrollytelling's evaluations; the explorable-explanations principle that interactivity stays optional; data-driven written narrative at scale — official statistics' automated local stories and newsroom automated reporting, read as method; precedents for stating a display's rhetorical positions ("how we did this", "about the data", methodology notes beside the chart).
3. What an interaction must earn. The interaction taxonomies and what they claim; the measured reach of controls; the cost frameworks (interaction cost; working memory); progressive disclosure and its evidence; tooltips and hover against the touch, keyboard and print facts and the accessibility criteria for content on hover; the mobile evidence; version 1's purchase table and the "name the purchase" rule, tested.
4. Citable states. Stable addresses (the W3C's "Cool URIs don't change"; Memento); the measured decay of links and the drift of content behind them (the legal and scholarly reference-rot studies); data-citation principles and persistent identifiers; how public dashboards version and archive their states (versioned charts; archived releases; "withdrawn" and "history" conventions); "share this view" patterns; what a shared or cropped state must carry (the house's crop test); whether anything is known about how charts and dashboard states are cited in policy documents.
5. How a display reads from the data layer and responds to it. The separation of data, derivation and presentation (the grammar of graphics; declarative specifications; the house's data contract); state as a function of data plus the reader's choices; responsive visualisation — what changes between a desk and a phone, with evidence; degraded states (Chapter 8); the display of missing and withheld data and what it does to readers' estimates and trust; how much of each record — provenance set, recipe card, claim record, comparison record, service record, measure record, safeguards record — sits on the face, and the trust-in-visualisation evidence on showing sources and provenance; a felt distribution with its bands and sample on a face (Chapter 9); the three nothings told apart without placing the held units (Chapter 10); maps that do not stigmatise — class breaks, normalisation, area dominance, cartograms and dot density, with the effectiveness evidence; the funnel plot as a first-screen object (Chapter 7); colour never alone (Chapter 3).
6. The failures. "What am I looking at" and "people dont use it" as first-screen and path failures: public-dashboard retirements and their stated reasons; what is known about dashboard abandonment and decay of use; the newsroom's telemetry on completion.
7. The symmetry test. Version 1's positions: write the sentence first; the visitor's question not the institution's description; the six patterns and message-then-input with an exemplar chosen as an endorsement; freshness and provenance as first-screen furniture; the purchase table; nothing crucial behind click or hover; every reachable state has a stable address; shares obey the crop rules; prediction before reveal; one staged transition per reshape; the poster beside an open door; the "how this argues" page; per-place written stories from templates in the house register; reader contributions as a story layer (now subject to Chapters 9 and 10); scrollytelling as editorial output; the human dot plot (weakened in Chapter 9); the Style Guide's dashboard section (DSH-01–21) and the Decision Book's Chapter 7 (name the purchase) and Chapter 15.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Sarikaya et al. 2019 (what we talk about when we talk about dashboards); Bach et al. 2022 (dashboard design patterns); Tory et al. 2021 (dashboard users' practices); Dimara et al. 2021 (unmet visualisation needs in decision-making); Few 2006/2013; Wexler, Shaffer & Cotgreave 2017; GOV.UK Design System and content guidance; ONS design system and style guide; NHS design system; the GOV.UK Performance Platform's retirement notice; Segel & Heer 2010; Hullman & Diakopoulos 2011; Hullman et al. 2013 (sequence); Boy, Détienne & Fekete 2015 (CHI); McKenna et al. 2017 (already in Chapter 3); Zhi, Ottley & Metoyer 2019; Kim, Reinecke & Hullman 2017; Kong, Liu & Agrawala 2018/2019 (titles); Stolper et al. 2016; Riche et al. 2018; Seyser & Zeiller 2018; Victor 2011; Hohman et al. 2020 (Distill); ONS on its automated local-area stories and data-driven content; the Associated Press / RADAR automated reporting, read as method; Heer & Shneiderman 2012; Yi et al. 2007; Dimara & Perin 2020; Lam 2008 (interaction costs); Conlen, Kale & Heer 2019 (in Chapter 3); Nielsen on progressive disclosure, and Springer & Whittaker 2019; WCAG 2.2 success criteria 1.4.13 and 2.5.x; Brehmer et al. 2019 (mobile); Hoffswell, Li & Liu 2020; Kim et al. 2021 (responsive visualisation insights); Berners-Lee 1998 "Cool URIs don't change"; RFC 7089 (Memento); Zittrain, Albert & Lessig 2014; Klein et al. 2014; Jones et al. 2016; FORCE11 2014 data-citation principles; DataCite; Our World in Data's chart versioning and citation guidance; the UK coronavirus dashboard's archive and download practice; ONS release versioning and corrections policy; GOV.UK's "withdrawn" and page-history conventions; Wilkinson 2005 (grammar of graphics); Satyanarayan et al. 2017 (Vega-Lite); Song & Szafir 2019 (missing data); Sarma et al. 2023 (imputation displays); Xiong et al. 2019 (trust in map-based visualisations); Elhamdadi et al. 2022 (measuring trust); Mayr et al. 2019; Kay et al. 2016 (quantile dotplots) and Hullman et al. 2015 (hypothetical outcome plots) as already in Chapter 3; Brewer on class breaks and ColorBrewer; Monmonier *How to Lie with Maps*; Nusrat & Kobourov 2016 and Nusrat, Alam & Kobourov 2018 (cartograms); UKSA geospatial ethics (Chapter 10); the Analysis Function's chart and map guidance; version 1's Chapters 1, 3, 9 and 10 (archived); the Decision Book Chapters 7 and 15; Style Guide v1.5 §DSH.

**What I expect to find.** That the dashboard literature has described many dashboards and measured few, and that the design-pattern vocabularies are menus not findings, as the narrative taxonomy was; that the strongest evidence on the first screen is Chapter 3's and this chapter adds the structure (what goes where, in what order, for whom) rather than new perception facts; that story-as-gate suppresses exploration and linking text to its view helps recall, so the house's "poster beside an open door" has a mechanism; that automated written narrative at place scale is an established official practice with a method the house can borrow; that interaction has a cost framework and a measured reach, and that the purchase table survives as a procedure; that citability has a strong, open evidence base in reference rot and a thin one in dashboard practice, so the rule will rest on the former; that missing-data display measurably changes what readers conclude, which gives the three nothings an evidence base beyond Chapter 4's taxonomy; that showing sources and provenance on the face raises measured trust under some conditions and not others; that cartographic effectiveness evidence will complicate the stigma rules (cartograms are read less accurately; dot density is read more honestly); and that "people dont use it" has public precedents whose stated reasons are about maintenance and audience rather than design.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel: (A) dashboards and first screens — the empirical dashboard literature, the design systems, the public-dashboard retirements, the mobile and responsive evidence; (B) story and exploration — the narrative-visualisation literature at source, linking and sequence, prediction, scrollytelling's evaluations, automated written narrative in official statistics and newsrooms as method, precedents for stating a display's rhetoric; (C) interaction and its cost — taxonomies, cost frameworks, progressive disclosure, hover and touch standards, the reach measurements; (D) citable states and the data-to-display architecture — link rot and content drift, persistent identifiers and data citation, versioned and archived public dashboards, the grammar-of-graphics separation, missing-data and provenance display studies, cartographic effectiveness and stigma.

### Threads pulled

- **The field describes dashboards and does not evaluate them.** Sarikaya et al.'s 83 ("rarely given dashboards their due consideration"; "We did not directly consult dashboard users or designers"); Bach et al.'s 144 and 42 patterns ("purely descriptive"; "beyond the scope of this work to determine what makes for a 'good' dashboard"); the Tableau Public census of 25,620 (75% interactive; median eight blocks; a fifth text; "far simpler than prior research accounts for"); Sultanum & Setlur's 190 (92% titled; experts split on stating takeaways). Two UK official bodies frame the dashboard as a form to be justified, in nearly the same words ("put the burden of interpreting and understanding data onto the user"); the ONS drops Few's single screen. The chapter's evidence therefore comes from Chapter 3's perception findings, single-mechanism experiments and design-system practice, each marked.
- **Where reading starts, and on what.** Sultanum & Setlur 2026 (18 authors, 16 users; titles first in 65.6% of flows; "cleaner and more coherent" author flows; self-report not eye tracking). ONS: 28% read; "Over half" on mobile; "80% … do not scroll past the first quarter" — none sourced or dated; the Analysis Function's "Two-thirds" links back to the ONS's "Over half". Chart titles "15 words or fewer" (the ten-word rule is gone); headings under 60 characters; GOV.UK titles 65. StatCounter 53.56% phones; Ofcom 77% of time online on smartphones.
- **The six landing patterns have no head-to-head test.** Single-pattern evidence: search beside content adopted by 66.7–80.6% (Feng et al., n = 830; "deeply personal"); a guess before the reveal helps when followed by an explanation on moderately familiar data (Kim, Reinecke & Hullman: −4.08 for predict-and-explain; −2.30 predict alone; 1.3–1.8 on unfamiliar data with explanation; a bare guess nothing on unfamiliar; nothing on familiar — the verifier corrected the first draft's attribution of the 4.08 to unfamiliar data); configuration taken up by one or two in a hundred (BLI: six million visitors; 60,000 or 130,000 indexes — the two OECD figures could not be reconciled); the ONS's area tool still input-first; UKHSA redesigned its landing twice with change notes. The rule "no configuration before meaning" stands as a reading with the ONS's chart standard beneath it.
- **The authored account and the open door.** Segel & Heer (58 cases; the martini glass "most common" and able to open on "an interesting default view"; "we are not experts in the study of narrative"). Hullman & Diakopoulos (four editorial layers; "provenance rhetoric"; "Default views provide an initial point of interpretation" — the "defaults are procedural rhetoric" sentence version 1 attributed to them is not there). The storytelling null: full text unreachable by every route; version 1's figures withdrawn; the abstract's "does not seem to increase user-engagement in exploration" carried as † and the stronger "costs" claim re-based on the required-interaction evidence. Zhi et al. (180; slideshow better for comprehension; linking raised engagement; recall an interaction). Kong 2018 and 2019 (a contradicting title remembered over the chart — 133 of 398 title, 68 chart, about half neither; credibility fell with misalignment; 72–87% still called it neutral). Kim, Setlur & Agrawala 2021 (a caption pointing at a weak feature loses to the chart). Stokes et al. (302; "not penalized"; L2/L4 steer takeaways; trend in the title). Ajani (24; focus helps memory; "pushy"). McKenna re-read: no scroller/stepper difference; comprehension flat — Chapter 3's summary corrected. Schneiders (381; scrollytelling recall .65 v text .61; text most efficient per second). Hohman et al.: evidence thin because "large publishers are unwilling to share internal metrics". Victor: "The reader is not forced to interact in order to learn."
- **Generated sentences at scale.** The ONS's 331 Census pages ("not a labour-saving tool"; topics ranked per place; "all data are checked before publishing"; labelled on the page; robo-utils templates; no LLM). The AP method and its stock-split error; "Algorithms cannot ask questions". Graefe & Bohlken: credibility d = 0.0, quality d = 0.5, readability d = 2.8 against automated text; ratings move with what readers are told.
- **What an interaction must earn.** Heer & Shneiderman never say "descriptive not prescriptive" — "Validating and evolving this taxonomy is a community project"; Yi et al. (311 techniques; "not collectively exhaustive"); Lam's seven costs from 32 studies (abstract). Mosca et al. (472 and 1,980 analysed; static ≥ interactive; 43–44% used the filter; 70/40 confounded by self-selection and said so). Zikmund-Fisher (3,371; completion 79.3→65.5; correct choice 73.8→61.6 in the strong-dominance condition). Stoiber (596 across three studies; no correctness gain except one unfamiliar form, on which abstract and body disagree). D-Tour (six users, no baseline). Nielsen's two conditions, in his words. Springer & Whittaker (detail raised expectation, not trust after use). GOV.UK's details/accordion/tabs evidence on hidden content; the NHS expander that tested well when readers were overwhelmed. WCAG 1.4.13's reasons are magnification, time and keyboard — not touch; 2.5.7 supplies the touchscreen sentence; 2.5.8's dense-visualisation exception asks for "equivalent functionality through alternative means" — the table twin. No UK design system has a tooltip component; the ONS's chart components make tooltips keyboard-reachable and put downloads below the chart. The phone removes content and interactivity (Hoffswell; Kim, Moritz & Hullman: density-message trade-off); linear and small multiples faster on phones (Brehmer).
- **Citable states.** Cool URIs; reference rot (49.9% of Supreme Court links; one in five articles; 76.35% drift); Pew 2024 (38% of 2013 pages gone; 21% of US government pages with a broken link); UKGWA: "Interactive content usually cannot be archived". FORCE11's seventh principle; DataCite versioning; the RDA's R1–R14 ("a dynamic, query centric view"); the DCC's three approaches. OWID's three layers — selection in the URL, dated immutable archive, generated citation naming the archive and retrieval date — and its gap (the archive URL drops the selection), which the chapter's rule closes by putting address and snapshot date in one citation. ONS versions and corrections policy; GOV.UK's withdrawn banner and page history (the "history mode" sentence is about a previous-government banner, not replacement — the verifier caught the first draft's misreading); UKHSA area-in-URL; sense.us's state-as-URL and its many-URLs-one-view snag; Shiny's 2,000 characters.
- **The data-to-display separation.** Wickham ("independent from the other components"); Vega-Lite (unit := data, transforms, mark, encodings; selections parameterise encodings); D3's data join. The synthesis — a view is a data version plus a specification plus a selection — is the chapter's.
- **The face.** Burns et al. (metadata → "more thorough", not more accurate; "trust" not in the paper); Peck (17 of 42 moved by sources; 25 not); Xiong (clarity, disclosure and thoroughness predicted choice; trust and accuracy did not — corrected wording); Mayr's factors; Kennedy et al. (source lines as persuasion: "no need for the user to check"); Lin & Thornton (beauty → trust via inferred competence; preprint); Dörk's disclosure principle. Missing data: Sarma (dropping the gap most biased; every uncertainty display lowered confidence); Andreasson (explained emptiness beat blurring; raised risk-taking); Song & Szafir abstract only. Maps: ONS rates not counts, Jenks default; Brewer & Pickle (quantiles; matched legends +28%); Schiewe (260; small areas missed by 30–40%; 92.6/85.4 found max/min from lightness; 93.1% chose the darker of two unlegended maps; about half still read by lightness with a legend — the first draft had a quotation that was not on the page, corrected); cartograms (contiguous best among them; a choropleth with circles beat them in the one head-to-head, reported through the survey).
- **The failures.** The Performance Platform (802 services, 118 with costs; "lack of clarity about the purpose"; "all things to all people"; closed March 2021 for departmental publishing); ITDashboard, JHU, COVID Tracking: supply-side reasons; the UK COVID dashboard's reason not found; the Analysis Function's retirement stage. No study of public non-use exists; the dashboard's own analytics (Chapter 10: how, not who) are the only evidence the studio will have.
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No head-to-head of landing patterns; no empty-state study; no tooltip discovery study; no reach figure newer than 2019; no dot-density or hex evaluation; no "red = bad" map study; no evaluation of dashboards as such; no study of returning readers; no measured cost of the regime.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §15. Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch11-agent-returns.md`. The orchestrator spot-checked sixteen sources the same day (ONS dashboards; Analysis Function building and managing; ONS interactive charts; Sarikaya; Bach; Segel & Heer; Mosca — twice; Stokes; OWID 2025 post; RDA; Jones et al.; Schiewe; Sultanum & Setlur 2026; the ONS 331 blog; GOV.UK details; WCAG 2.5.8) — about ninety strings with controls; all held. The verifier re-traced about 600 strings and figures across about 125 pages.

### What I learned

That the field has described many dashboards and measured almost none, so a first screen is designed on perception evidence, single-mechanism experiments and design-system practice, each marked for what it is. That the first screen is a sentence and the title is where reading starts; that a title that contradicts its chart is remembered over it and a caption that points at a weak feature loses to the chart, so the sentence says what the view shows. That text on the face is wanted and steers; that interaction, added without a task, buys nothing and can cost, and that the people who use a control are not the people who do not. That hover's standard is about magnification, time and keyboard, and the touch case is practitioners' and a sister criterion's; that dense marks may be small if the table is their equivalent. That a link is not a citation: links rot, content drifts, the national archive usually cannot hold an interactive page, and the working model is address plus dated snapshot plus generated citation, with the selection carried in the citation. That a view is a data version plus a specification plus a selection, and so is reproducible. That a source line is read as a reason not to check, so it must open the record. That dropping a gap is the most biased way to show it and showing it lowers confidence, which is the gap working. That a map is the hardest first screen: rates, held breaks, matched legends, and a third of readers who will not see a small area. And that the retired public dashboards died upstream, of stale data and unclear purpose, not of measured non-use.

### What I left out, and why

- Boy et al. 2015 (full text); Hullman et al. 2013; Zhi et al. (full text); Song & Szafir (full text); Lam; Dimara & Perin (body); Hullman, Adar & Shah; Blascheck 2019; Boy 2016; Seyser & Zeiller; Stolper; Thudt; Kennedy's published version; Wilkinson 2005; Kaspar et al.; Sun & Li; Ivanković; Kitchin; UKDS citation guidance; the ONS Census maps' rationale; the OECD working paper's body; Bartram et al.'s findings: unreached or abstract only; nothing quoted beyond what is marked.
- Version 1's two numbers for the storytelling null: withdrawn, not carried.
- The W3C media-queries hover feature: the reader invented four quotations; nothing quoted.
- Topic findings of every study (voting, drugs, treatments, mortality, census areas, crime): read for method only.
- Any decision about the Vibe Map's pattern, sentence, controls, map form or palette: recorded as choices; the filled example is labelled invented and its sentence kept within the house's limit.
- The 60-second version is long again (~1,500 words); raised a seventh time.

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: a head-to-head of landing patterns `[Prototype flag]`; tooltip use on touch `[Prototype flag]`; the three nothings on one face `[Prototype flag]`; a citation that survives a year `[Prototype flag]`; matched legends across filter states `[Prototype flag]`; published reach figures `[Prototype flag]`; the sentence on a phone, generated sentences, the small-area form, the "how this argues" page `[Validate with users]`; one sentence or three; the returning reader; the regime's cost; dot-density and hex maps; "red = bad" measured `[Further research]`; the path (Hullman 2013) `[Further research]`; seven records and a face sheet; the 60-second version `[Revisit at end]`.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's sentence-first with the reading-order and title-recall evidence and counterbalances its first-screen furniture (a signal that must open the record) `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`; strengthens Chapter 2's personal-relevance finding (search) and gives its official reader a citable state; strengthens Chapter 3 throughout and corrects its McKenna summary `[Counterbalance → Ch 3]`; strengthens Chapter 4's marked-never-blank with the missing-data experiments; Chapter 5's default-weighting finding with the BLI uptake; Chapter 6's templated-claim rule with the generated-text method; Chapter 7's funnel and declared peers, and its no-ranking with matched legends; Chapter 8 (figures before script and the table twin as a design system's and a standard's rules; the degraded state as a state; retirement as a stage) and counterbalances its map pricing `[Counterbalance → Ch 8]`; Chapter 9's spread with the n in words; Chapter 10's three nothings with display and measured confidence effects, its analytics rule as the only reach evidence, and counterbalances "states its kind" with the confidence cost `[Counterbalance → Ch 10]`. Version 1: "stable address" becomes three layers; the null's numbers and the taxonomy attribution withdrawn; the purchase list re-priced; the poster beside the open door holds on three read mechanisms. Decision Book: a candidate chapter, *The surface*; design system: snapshot banner, generated citation, generated-text label, small-area inset, "how this argues" link, status chips that open records, degraded-state stamp; DSH-21 conflict carried.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Sent one after the other again (the method note from Entry 10 was not followed; the two Agent calls must go in one message to run together). The **verifier** re-traced about 600 strings and figures across about 125 pages and reported seven wrong, five truncated, three misattributed and a long list of marks to lift. Corrected: the prediction gain re-attributed (4.08 is predict-and-explain on moderately familiar data; 1.3–1.8 on unfamiliar with explanation; a bare guess nothing on unfamiliar; nothing on familiar) in the 60-second version, §3, §6, §10 and the rule; Xiong's two quotations restored to the page's words ("clarity, amount of disclosure. and thoroughness"; "Trust and perceived accuracy") and the † dropped; Schiewe's 93.1 per cent given the page's sentence (the darker of two unlegended maps) and the with-legend figure (about half) added, in §2, §8, truth 7 and the rule; the GOV.UK "history mode" sentence dropped from §7 and the rule, which now rests on the ONS corrections policy; Stoiber's unfamiliar-form exception carried into the 60-second version, beliefs four and eight, truth 4 and §9; Dimara's counts (177 complete, 164 classified; ten interviewed, nine retained); the 92 per cent title share returned to the 190-dashboard study; "Two-thirds" attributed to the Analysis Function; the Boy et al. sentence marked as its abstract (†), with the gallery website named; the 28 per cent's lack of source stated and GOV.UK's "20 to 28%" with its dead link; "2012" removed; WCAG 2.5.8's spacing exception added; Kong 2019's "68 chart, about half neither" added and "titles decide recall" withdrawn; Zikmund-Fisher's strong-dominance condition restored and the seventh/sixth sentence recast in points; 1.4.13's full dismissible exception; Berners-Lee's doubled "the" kept with a note; "five words" for the FORCE11 phrase; Parhi's sample described as the page has it; "four pages" dropped for the RDA; Bach's 23 lifted from †; 98 "(read once)" marks lifted on confirmation and 92 ledger entries marked verified.

The **cold reader** (no project context) said what the chapter decided correctly, named a hard line and an open choice, and found the headline evidence. Its strongest objection was accepted whole: the chapter's pass test asked a reader to say the sentence back, and the chapter's own title evidence says readers do that whether or not the chart supports it; the test now asks for the figure the sentence rests on and a next step, the two-readers test now defines its readers by what is covered (charts covered; written account removed), and the filled example scores the new test. It also found: the title-versus-chart finding stated two ways (now stated as two findings about two conditions); onboarding's exception dropped in §9; "a fifth" for four in five; the BLI used as a configure-first case when its default state could not be established (now evidence on uptake only); version 1's audit sentence contradicting its own patterns (now "weight or configure" against "a place or a selection"); prediction against "nothing asked before given" (a skippable guess after the sentence is not a toll, now in the rule); the two missing-data studies stated as one finding (now two comparisons, with the risk-taking result carried into the rule and nothing imputed into a withheld cell); the null's numbers printed in §4 after being withdrawn in §10 (now withdrawn in both); the taxonomy used as evidence after being called a menu (now "a description of what is common"); "on request" used for one step and for a separate page (now defined, with scrolling placed on the face); the filled example's sentence over the house limit and its district line before the input (rewritten); the citation's contents stated three ways (now one, carrying address, snapshot date, data version and retrieval date, which also closes the OWID gap); Mosca's N and "five" types (2,452 analysed; four types); "legend or no legend" (now nine in ten without, half with); the small-areas rule's kind (now Evidence + Reading, a stance); "the path" promised and not treated (a subsection added); "version 1", the poster rule, the crop test, the walk, the rungs and tiers, lit and held, the floor, the two columns, the three user groups, DSH and COL codes and PECR glossed in Before you start; headings that did not match their sections renamed; the studio's own justification added to the symmetry test; and three missing questions (one sentence or three; the returning reader; the regime's cost) added as flags. What it asked for and did not get: a shorter preamble and 60-second version (left; raised a seventh time); fewer Fixed rules (left for the Decision Book's candidate chapter and Chapter 16); the beliefs moved after the evidence (left; the template's order).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×10. `[Prototype flag]` ×6 (landing patterns head-to-head; tooltip use by device; three nothings on one face; a citation that survives a year; matched legends across filter states; published reach). `[Validate with users]` ×5. `[Revisit → Ch 12]`, `[→ Ch 13]`, `[→ Ch 14]`, `[→ Ch 16]` ×2. `[Revisit at end]` ×2. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`, `[→ Ch 3]`, `[→ Ch 8]`, `[→ Ch 10]`. `[Rich reference]` ×10.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **Analysis Function, *Data visualisation: building and managing dashboards* and *testing dashboards* (6 February 2026)**, read with the ONS service manual's *Dashboards* and *Interactive charts and animations*. The UK's official position on when a dashboard is justified, how it is tested and when it is retired — and the official reader's expectations in their own words.
2. **Sultanum & Setlur, *Not Always Top-Left*** (VIS 2026 preprint). The one study of where dashboard readers start: the title, two-thirds of the time.
3. **Stokes et al., *Striking a Balance*** (2023). How much text on a chart, where, and what each kind does to what readers take away; the authors' own guidelines.
4. **Mosca, Ottley & Chang, *Does Interaction Improve Bayesian Reasoning with Visualization?*** (2021). Static against four interaction types; the use rates; and the sentence to remember when the prototype's analytics arrive: people who interact do better, and the authors say why that proves nothing.
5. **W3C, *Understanding* SC 1.4.13 and SC 2.5.8.** The hover conditions and their stated reasons; the dense-data-visualisation exception and the equivalent route it asks for.
6. **Rauber et al., RDA *Data Citation of Evolving Data*** (2015). Fourteen recommendations that are, together, the specification of a citable dashboard state.
7. **Gerber & Giattino, Our World in Data, *New feature: embed archived versions of our interactive charts*** (2025), read beside a live chart page and its archive twin. The three-layer model in production, with its one gap.
8. **Jones et al., *Scholarly Context Adrift*** (2016). Three out of four web references lead to changed content; the dashboard's problem measured.
9. **Wainwright, ONS, *Semi-automation and Census 2021*** (2023), with the robo-utils repository. The method for a sentence per place, its cost and its label.
10. **Schiewe 2019** (open). Area-size bias and dark-is-more with 260 readers, and the author's refusal to endorse a remedy.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Two Agent calls in one message, or they run in series.** Entry 10 said dispatch together; Entry 11 again sent them one after the other because each Agent call was its own message. The cold reader and the verifier must be launched in the same message to overlap.
- **A summary sentence is a quotation too.** The 4.08 was right in the body paragraph and wrong in three summaries of it; the 93.1 per cent had a quotation that existed nowhere. Check the 60-second version and the truths against the body paragraph, not against memory of it.
- **A test is tested against the chapter's own evidence.** The pass test measured recitation after the chapter had shown that recitation happens regardless. Run each proposed test through the chapter's own findings before the break-test section.
- **Two studies that point opposite ways are two comparisons.** Sarma (against a dropped gap) and Andreasson (against a blurred one) were stated as one finding. Name the baseline of each.
- **Define the distances before using them.** "One step away" and "on request" drifted; the glossary now fixes three distances and places scrolling.
- **Headings are checked against sections last.** Four headings promised something the section did not contain (a test, a table, a first screen, an official reader).
- **The null that cannot be read is not a load-bearing wall.** The house shape now rests on what was read; the unreadable paper is a flag.
- **A design-system phrase is read in its own paragraph.** "History mode" was a banner about previous governments, not a rule about replacement.
- **Count N as analysed or recruited, and say which.** Mosca's 2,452 is analysed; 2,679 recruited.
