## Entry 12 · Chapter 12 — Recognising your place

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 11. Second chapter of Part 3, The reading experience.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What makes a person, or a scene, recognise its place in a display — and what must a display do so that what is counted and what is missed sit together without the counted winning; so that what a place *is* is never ranked while what it *has* is compared fairly, with the gate on the face; so that the unit shown is the one the scene lives at, and the display says which unit that is and what it hides; so that finding a place that suits you is matching and not ranking, with the why shown; and so that whatever is cut out of a view and carried away still says what it is? The brief's fourth failure — "for nightlife, it fails if they do not recognise their place within the front-end interface" — answered as method: what recognition is, in how many senses, how each is tested, and what the display owes to each.

**Sub-questions.**
1. What "recognising your place" means, and how each sense is tested. Three senses at least: the reader finds the *geography* they hold (cognitive maps and their method — Lynch's sketch-map interviews and Milgram's; residents' own neighbourhood boundaries against administrative units; vernacular and fuzzy place; Chapter 3's "a schema the reader already has"; Chapter 9's Community Life Survey definitions of "neighbourhood"); the reader finds *themselves or their group* in the data (personal relevance and attention to data — Peck et al.; Kennedy & Hill on the feeling of numbers; the Seeing Data project; what is known about whether people engage more with data about their own place); and the *described agree the account is them* (member checking when the members are a scene, not a sample — Chapter 9 settled its standing as two kinds of validity evidence plus legitimacy; this chapter asks for the procedure's form: community review in participatory GIS and counter-mapping, synthesised member checking, dissemination practice in community-based research). A fourth sense enters as frame, not finding: recognition as a political category (Taylor; Honneth; Fraser's recognition beside redistribution), named because Chapter 2 said this chapter has a political reason and because the two columns appear to map onto it. Reported as a frame with its critics; no authority drawn from it.
2. The two columns — counted beside missed. The framing evidence version 1 rested on (the Othering & Belonging synthesis via the Decision Book, house) and its empirical status: whether "never repeat the opposing frame" survives the debunking and familiarity-backfire literature as it now stands; asset-based and deficit-based accounts of place (Kretzmann & McKnight; the deficit-discourse literature; the Glasgow Centre for Population Health's asset guidance; Chapter 1's community-safety dashboard that "refocused the conversation"); whether "robustness reads as reality" is measured — precision as a cue to credibility and confidence (Jerez-Fernandez, Angulo & Oppenheimer; Zhang & Schwarz; the precision-effect literature); rendering absence at layout scale (CALVI's 0.39 verified at source; Chapter 11's missing-data findings cited not re-run); commensurability — the dual-axis and dual-scale evidence and the UK guidance against it; equal visual dignity as a rule with or without evidence; the bridging sentence as Chapter 11's sentence applied to two columns.
3. Identity never ranked; conditions compared with a gate. Levels of measurement (Stevens 1946) and the critique that the typology misleads (Velleman & Wilkinson 1993) — because "identity is nominal" is the chapter's load-bearing claim and it must be stated at its true strength; the display forms for a nominal profile and their evidence (radar and star plots — Diehl et al.; Albo et al.; Feldman; glyphs — Borgo et al.'s survey; Chernoff faces and their critique; small multiples; parallel coordinates); classification as identity in official practice — the 2021 Output Area Classification's supergroups and pen portraits, the naming of clusters and the stigma arguments over earlier labels; Understanding Scottish Places' typology and "towns like yours"; OECD regional well-being; comparability as a quality dimension (ESS quality framework; the Code of Practice 3.0's coherence and comparability; the house's Comparative Methodology gate as a house standard); whether any public instrument separates identity from conditions as a layout boundary (version 1 found none); the advocacy sentence (intensity with support) as a form.
4. The scene scale. The modifiable areal unit problem's two effects (Openshaw — Chapter 1; Fotheringham & Wong 1991's demonstration that results change with zoning); the ecological fallacy (Chapter 4); the UK statistical geographies and best-fit (OA, LSOA, MSOA, ward, built-up area 2022, and what each is for); residents' perceived neighbourhoods against units (Coulton et al.; Campbell et al.; Lee & Campbell); vernacular geography from user content (Montello et al. "Where's downtown?"; Hollenstein & Purves); "scene" as a unit of analysis in the sociology of culture (Straw; Bennett & Peterson's local, translocal and virtual; Silver & Clark's scenescapes method — read as a method for constructing a unit from amenities, never for what it says about any place); the scale as a landing choice (Chapter 11) and as a declared comparison unit (Chapter 7); the claim "a borough with a scene beats a borough with a superclub" restated as a MAUP consequence, testable, not as a finding.
5. Discovery as matching, not ranking. Recommender explanations (Herlocker, Konstan & Riedl 2000 — the 21 interfaces, the histogram, the 86 per cent, each verified; Tintarev & Masthoff's seven aims and the persuasion/effectiveness distinction; Nunes & Jannach's systematic review; Kunkel et al.); exposure fairness and popularity bias in ranking and recommendation (Singh & Joachims; Abdollahpouri et al.; Ekstrand et al.'s monograph) — because "an aggregation of the biggest" has a name in that literature; the filter-bubble null (Reuters Institute review; Bruns; Zuiderveen Borgesius) and serendipity's state (Kotkov et al.; later reviews); matching tools as precedents — Teleport (defunct), Discover Uni, Explore Local Statistics' similar areas, Understanding Scottish Places' similar towns, the Better Life Index (Chapters 5 and 11); the money view — 360Giving/GrantNav's "find gaps", the Arts Council's Priority Places and Levelling Up for Culture Places method as an official "absence made visible" and its critique; curated-pool-then-match as a technology pattern (read as technology only); opt-in directories as a rule for a category, now under Chapter 10 (which retired "consent to be found": discoverability is a declared status for a category, consulted, governed and agreed, never a marker on a unit); Every Noise at Once's end as Chapter 8's platform rule, cited.
6. The crop test. The Decision Book's breakout-statistic rule (house); what is known about charts in circulation — Lee et al.'s *Viral Visualizations*, Lisnic et al.'s *Misleading beyond visual tricks*, Lo et al. on misinformative visualisations; the Analysis Function's and ONS's guidance on charts for social media and on charts making sense alone (title, source, alt text in the image); Our World in Data's download and embed carrying source and licence; press use of rankings (university rankings; *Doing Business*; "most deprived" headlines) and the official wording guidance that exists for the Indices of Deprivation; what a cut-out of a view must carry, as a procedure (Chapter 11's citation; Chapter 4's provenance set).
7. Harm through representation, at place scale. Territorial stigma and how it is measured (Wacquant — Chapter 10; Kearns et al.; Permentier et al.'s reputation surveys; Tunstall et al.'s correspondence test on address and employment for the Joseph Rowntree Foundation); the label debates in official classification ("left behind", "hard-pressed", "deprived"); strengths-based reporting guidance; what, if anything, is measured about residents seeing their place labelled.
8. The symmetry test. Version 1's positions: the value column speaks first; the harm column never omitted; equal visual dignity; provenance chip on every figure; harm column not the reference category; no moral gradient across columns; gaps full-size and labelled; no shared axis; the bridging sentence; order chosen and stated; identity never ranked; conditions gated; scale where the scene lives; default self-over-time plus chosen peers; incomparability rendered; no view survives cropping as a ranking; discovery orderings personal, decomposed, on request; the why as concrete cues; effectiveness not persuasiveness; the scene defines the pool; named guides; adjacency against narrowing; the money view as absence made visible, consented scenes only; revenue returns; opt-in granular revocable discoverability (retired by Chapter 10 — re-stated under the held status); informal scenes default to aggregate character; the five-second tests (kid, scene, funder); the represented-not-used test; the Form 696 test (Carly's frame). The house's: "lead with abundance"; "all vibes are valid"; "not about smoothing out the scene"; the deck's slides 7 and 8; Style Guide §12's comparability rule; Comparative Methodology v1.0's eight-criterion gate; Decision Book Chapter 16 (framing) and Chapter 7 (the neutral-to-positive ramp); the community register's governing question (redistribute opportunity rather than display what exists); the three user groups for testing.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Lynch 1960 (*The Image of the City*, the method appendix); Milgram & Jodelet 1976; Coulton, Korbin, Chan & Su 2001; Coulton, Jennings & Chan 2013; Campbell, Henly, Elliott & Irwin 2009; Lee & Campbell 1997; Montello, Goodchild, Gottsegen & Fohl 2003; Hollenstein & Purves 2010; Peck, Ayuso & El-Etr 2019 (*Data is Personal*); Kennedy & Hill 2018; Kennedy, Hill, Aiello & Allen 2016; Burns et al. 2020; Birt et al. 2016; Candela 2019; Motulsky 2021; Lincoln & Guba 1985 (Chapter 2's owed page 314); Sieber 2006; Elwood 2006; Peluso 1995 (counter-mapping); Taylor 1992; Honneth 1995; Fraser 1995/2000 and Fraser & Honneth 2003; Lewandowsky et al., *The Debunking Handbook 2020*; Swire-Thompson, DeGutis & Lazer 2020 (backfire effects); Ecker et al. 2022; Kretzmann & McKnight 1993; Fogarty et al. 2018; Glasgow Centre for Population Health 2011/2012; Schwabish & Feng 2021 (*Do No Harm Guide*); Jerez-Fernandez, Angulo & Oppenheimer 2014; Zhang & Schwarz 2012; Ge, Cui & Kay 2023 (CALVI, at source); Isenberg, Bezerianos, Dragicevic & Fekete 2011 (dual-scale charts); Few 2008; the Analysis Function's chart guidance on secondary axes; Stevens 1946; Velleman & Wilkinson 1993; Diehl, Beck & Burch 2010; Albo et al. 2016; Feldman 2013; Borgo et al. 2013; Chernoff 1973 and Morris, Ebert & Rheingans 2000; ONS 2021 Output Area Classification (method and pen portraits) and Gale et al. 2016 on the 2011 OAC; Understanding Scottish Places (method); OECD *How's Life in Your Region*; the ESS Quality Assurance Framework and the Code of Practice for Statistics 3.0; Openshaw 1984; Fotheringham & Wong 1991; ONS statistical geographies and best-fit guidance; Straw 1991; Bennett & Peterson 2004; Silver & Clark 2016; Herlocker, Konstan & Riedl 2000; Tintarev & Masthoff 2007, 2012; Nunes & Jannach 2017; Kunkel et al. 2019; Singh & Joachims 2018; Abdollahpouri et al. 2019; Ekstrand, Das, Burke & Diaz 2022; Arguedas et al. 2022 (Reuters Institute); Bruns 2019; Zuiderveen Borgesius et al. 2016; Kotkov, Wang & Veijalainen 2016; Teleport (archived); Discover Uni (Office for Students); Explore Local Statistics; 360Giving; Arts Council England's Priority Places / Levelling Up for Culture Places methodology; Lee et al. 2021 (*Viral Visualizations*); Lisnic et al. 2023; Lo et al. 2022; the Analysis Function's data-visualisation guidance (charts; social media); Our World in Data's download and embed; the English Indices of Deprivation 2019 guidance on interpretation and wording (and 2025 if published); Hazelkorn on rankings and media; Wacquant 2007; Kearns, Kearns & Lawson 2013; Permentier, van Ham & Bolt 2008; Tunstall et al. 2014 (JRF); Local Trust / OCSI on "left behind" neighbourhoods; version 1's Chapters 2, 5 and 7 (archived); the Decision Book Chapters 6, 7 and 16; Style Guide v1.5 §12 and the dashboard section; Comparative Methodology v1.0.

**What I expect to find.** That "recognising your place" is three measurable things and one political one, and that the display owes something different to each: a geography the reader already holds (and residents' neighbourhoods will not match administrative units, so the unit is a declared choice with a known cost); a personal relevance that drives attention measurably but is not the same as accuracy; and a procedure (member checking) whose form for a scene is under-described in the literature and must be assembled from community review practice. That version 1's framing rule ("never repeat the opposing frame") rests on a familiarity-backfire claim the debunking literature has since weakened, so the value-first rule will survive as a house stance with a different justification (order as endorsement, from Chapter 11; the deficit-refocusing case from Chapter 1) rather than as a finding. That "robustness reads as reality" has a measured cousin — precision read as confidence and credibility — so the equal-dignity rule gains an evidence base. That the dual-axis evidence is thin but one-directional and the UK guidance is flat against it, so "no shared scale" stands as a standard and a reading. That Stevens' typology is contested and the honest form of "identity is nominal" is "identity is treated as nominal by a stance, because ranking it is a category error for the use" — a stance, not a measurement fact. That radar charts read badly and small multiples read well, so the identity portrait has a form the evidence prefers. That official classification already names small areas without ranking them, and has had its own label scandals, which gives the chapter both a precedent and a caution. That MAUP makes "the scene scale" a declared choice with a demonstrable reordering, not a discovery. That the recommender literature supports explanation and warns about persuasion, that exposure fairness names the biggest-wins problem exactly, and that the filter-bubble claim remains unsupported, so adjacency is designed on first principles. That the crop evidence shows most misleading charts in circulation are misleading by reasoning and missing context rather than by visual trick, which turns the crop test from a layout rule into a provenance rule. And that territorial stigma is measured by reputation and by correspondence tests, with results less dramatic than the theory, so the representation-harm rule is stated with its measured size.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel, in one message: (A) recognition — cognitive maps and their method; residents' neighbourhoods against units; vernacular place; personal relevance and attention to data about one's own place; member checking as community review (participatory GIS, counter-mapping, synthesised member checking); recognition as a political frame; territorial stigma, its measurement, and the official label debates; (B) the two columns — framing and the debunking literature's current position on backfire; asset and deficit accounts; precision as credibility; dual-axis and dual-scale evidence and UK guidance; CALVI at source; (C) identity, conditions and scale — Stevens and his critics; nominal display forms and their evidence; classification as identity in official statistics (OAC 2021; USP; OECD regions); comparability as a quality dimension; MAUP and zoning; UK geographies and best-fit; "scene" as a unit in the sociology of culture, read for method; (D) discovery and the crop — recommender explanations and persuasion; exposure fairness and popularity bias; the filter-bubble null and serendipity; matching tools as precedents; grant-gap and priority-place methods; opt-in directories as category rules; charts in circulation and the social-media chart guidance; press use of rankings and the IMD wording guidance.

### Threads pulled

- **Four senses, five failures.** "They do not recognise their place" split into geography, self, account and standing; and into five testable failures (wrong unit; not in it; account wrong; the counted won; you lost). The house's own slide — "Does it paint a recognisable picture? … Record who is missing and what contributors change" — is sense three as a procedure.
- **The unit.** No UK small-area unit was designed around recognition (OAs for size and homogeneity; the deprivation indices call LSOAs "neighbourhoods" by stipulation; wards "from fewer than 100 residents to more than 30,000"). The largest reachable measurement of residents' own neighbourhoods: 7,498 households, median 0.35 square miles against a 2.23 target area (a programme's, not a census unit; residential, not a scene's). Vague places have a method (Montello: 33 of 36 drew the 50 per cent region larger than the 100 per cent; Boston's 25/50/75 contours). MSOA Names (7,264, named by public correction) and the ONS's draw-your-own-unit tool. MAUP demonstrated on the national census (Flowerdew: "about 60 per cent of cases"; "Most of the time, not very"); the Iowa range verified as +0.979 to −0.811, not ±0.99. "Scene" as a unit: Straw's definition (a space of practices); the Chicago method builds scenes from listings and ranks on them.
- **Personal relevance.** Peck's "how easy or difficult it was to find their home state" — 42 interviews, self-reported usefulness; the places found were states, counties and local authorities, units with names. No controlled study of own-locality attention found. The ONS's 331 stories: "every individual area's page had been read".
- **The framing rule lost its reason.** Debunking Handbook 2020: "exhaustive experimental attempts to induce a backfire effect through familiarity alone have come up empty"; Swire-Thompson et al.: "not a robust empirical phenomenon"; Chong & Druckman: "qualities of frames" over "frequency of dissemination". Lakoff's own page cites no study; the OBI attribution unconfirmed. Value-first kept as a stance on endorsement (Chapter 11); the missed column's standing on Holder & Xiong (hiding variability raises stereotyping), Quinn (gap reporting raises exaggerated stereotypes, fades) and FrameWorks (leading with prevalence data did nothing). Chapter 1's Atlanta case corrected: the original says the data "are largely negative" and "We do not yet know how effective our methods have been".
- **Intervals cost little trust.** van der Bles (n = 5,780; numeric range d = 0.17, not significant; verbal hedge d = 0.75); Gustafson & Rice (48 experiments; quantified uncertainty "only positive or null effects"); producers' fear of looking "questionable" a 17 per cent minority. Granularity read as confidence (Zhang & Schwarz †).
- **Absence, scale and dignity.** CALVI's 0.39 confirmed, and narrowed: its "Missing Data" is a mark drawn over a gap, not an absent column. The Analysis Function's symbols as the vocabulary for holes. Dual axes "do not recommend"; juxtaposition "almost never outperformed" for local comparison (L'Yi; Ondov) — the cost accepted. Equal dignity untested (Wood: sketchy rendering cost accuracy, raised engagement). The Community Needs Index publishes assets as need.
- **Identity as a stance.** Velleman & Wilkinson: scale type "depends upon the questions we intend to ask of the data" — so "identity is nominal" is the house's question, declared. Radar "least effective and least liked" (†); filled radar's area reverses with axis order (Feldman †); linear read best (Waldner, one series). Gale et al.'s naming rule for the 2011 OAC — "words and phrases that might be construed as pejorative were not used"; "consequences of factors that have happened to areas rather than the consequences of human agency" — and its output ("Hard-Pressed Living"; the 2021 groups' "Challenged …"). USP's "no good or bad town type" is not on its pages; OECD regional well-being has "no single ranking" and ranks within topics; CHE's three groups.
- **The gate as a quality dimension.** ESS Code Principle 14; Regulation 223/2009 Art 12(1)(f); Code of Practice 3.0 practices 6.6, 6.7, 7.4, 9.3 ("comparable across and within geographies at regional, national and local levels, where possible"); the ELS's coherence check and its one-median-absolute-deviation wording rule — the official no-league-table precedent.
- **Discovery.** Herlocker's "86%" is 86 per cent of 97 exit-survey respondents in non-control groups about the interface they had just used, and decisions showed "no statistically significant difference"; Bilgic & Mooney: neighbour-style explanations inflate by 0.74, content cues enable accurate assessment; Tintarev & Masthoff 2012 †: personalising the explanation hurt effectiveness. Exposure (Singh & Joachims: 0.03 → 30 per cent less exposure); popularity bias; Salganik (14,341): sorting by popularity "increased both inequality and unpredictability of success". Filter bubble unsupported (Reuters; Zuiderveen Borgesius; Bruns); novelty lowered satisfaction (Ekstrand 2014). Discover Uni never says it is not a league table; the ELS's 20 nearest; Numbeo as counter-case. ACE's priority-places method: a weighted need score standardised within region, staff-scored opportunity, "Need and opportunity scores were not designed to be added together", balancing; no "cold spots". Opt-in directories: the owner applies; no rationale published. Discoverability restated under Chapter 10's held status.
- **The crop, measured.** Lisnic 2023 (9,958 posts, all stances): "only 12%" design violations; "the majority (55.3%)" screenshots; sceptics' charts "more than 40%" dashboard screenshots; reasoning and context errors beside dual axes at 5.4 per cent. Lee 2021: "data literacy in spades". Lo 2022: no cropping or missing-source issue in 74. The Analysis Function keeps title and source outside the image and says the image "can be taken out of context". OWID's "uncaptioned" preset. Lisnic 2025 guardrails: "Juxtaposed approaches are croppable"; 38 per cent did not notice. The official wording for ranks (IoD2025; SIMD; ONS well-being QMI).
- **Harm at place scale.** Wacquant's "blemish of place"; Tunstall's correspondence test: 2.6 points, p = 0.117, "small and it was not statistically significant". Labels: "left behind" kept "in the absence of better". Member checking's procedures (Data Walks; CBPR principle 7; PGIS ethics; Candela's harm) with no evaluation behind them.
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No controlled own-locality study; no UK drawn-boundary study at scale; no test of noticing an absent column; no equal-dignity test; no "better v different" test of profile forms; no lay test of dominance displays; no evaluation of community review; no asset-v-deficit experiment on the same area data; no experiment on two-measure dual axes; Lynch, Stevens, Lincoln & Guba p. 314, Coulton 2001's body, Birt 2016, Kennedy & Hill 2018, Permentier 2008, Kearns 2013 unreached.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §16 (79 rows). Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch12-agent-returns.md`. Per the efficiency change agreed with Carly this sprint, the orchestrator spot-checked only the load-bearing items (Peck's home-state sentence; Flowerdew's three sentences, with the remainder counts found not to reconcile) and left the sweep to the verifier, which re-traced about 700 checks across about 480 items and about 128 pages.

### What I learned

That recognition is four things and a display owes each something different. That the unit is never neutral and never recognised, so it is declared, with the administrative unit beside it, and a vague place is a core and bands. That readers look for a named place and that is the whole of the measured evidence. That version 1's best-known rule rested on a mechanism the field has since failed to find, and that the house's value-first survives as what it always was, a stance, now with its grounds separated — order on endorsement, presence on the deficit and salience evidence. That the missed column's intervals are not a liability. That "identity is nominal" can only ever be a stance, and the measurement literature says why. That the country already names places without blame by a published rule, and that the rule cannot make a condition's name neutral. That the comparability gate is a quality dimension with official cousins and an official working precedent. That explanations persuade before they help, and that content cues are the ones that help. That a sorted list is a treatment. That charts travel as screenshots and that official guidance keeps the furniture off the image on purpose. That the one experiment on a place's reputation penalty found little. And that the procedure for showing a community its own page has no evaluation behind it and one researcher's own account of harm, which is the thing to design against.

### What I left out, and why

- Every topic finding (crime, policing, housing estates, Indigenous health, pandemic opinion, film ratings, songs): read for method only.
- Version 1's three London boroughs in the advocacy sentence: replaced with placeholders; the illustration's policing caveat marked invented.
- The 74 per cent media-coverage figure (Stoneham et al. via Fogarty): a finding about a named community, not a display finding; dropped.
- The "−0.99 to +0.99" MAUP range; Flowerdew's remainder counts; the 2021 OAC supergroup names; USP's "no good or bad town type"; the Warwick Commission figure: unverified, not printed.
- Honneth at source; the Chicago method's limits (Scenescapes); Bennett & Peterson's definitions: unreached.
- Any decision about the Vibe Map's unit, columns, labels, discovery or frame: recorded as choices; the filled example labelled invented, with its identity fields pruned to two after the cold reader found counts among them.
- The 60-second version is about 1,300 words; raised an eighth time.

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: own-locality attention; equal dignity; "different" against "better"; the adjacency row; the crop measured `[Prototype flag]`; the unit's name; the review in the room; the type label; the holes `[Validate with users]`; eleven `[Further research]` items, mostly founding texts unreached; `[Revisit → Ch 13]` text inside images against the criteria; `[Revisit → Ch 14]` and `[→ Ch 16]`; nine records and the 60-second version `[Revisit at end]`.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's recognition failure and corrects its Atlanta case `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`; strengthens Chapter 2's political reason and personal relevance; Chapter 3's schema with the spatial half; Chapter 4's aggregation and nothings; Chapter 5's weights-as-judgements, and counterbalances its small-multiples default with the axis-order finding `[Counterbalance → Ch 5]`; Chapter 6's hollow success; Chapter 7 throughout (the gate as quality dimension; the ELS precedent; the official wording for ranks; exposure as display-side reactivity); Chapter 8 by citation; Chapter 9's member checking with a procedure and a record, counterbalancing its discordant cell with the credibility cost of shown disagreement `[Counterbalance → Ch 9]`; Chapter 10's held status, harm-through-representation and forum; Chapter 11's endorsement, generated sentence and citation, counterbalancing its interaction rule with text inside the image `[Counterbalance → Ch 11]`. Decision Book: ARG-12 in direct conflict; DAT-18, DAT-21, TBL-17, LAY-08 and the geography rules strengthened; candidate chapter *The place-page*. Design system: unit declaration; core-and-bands; hole symbols; fixed-order profile; in-frame provenance; review-and-errors footer; the recognition record — logged, not designed.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Sent together in one message this time, and they ran concurrently (the method note from Entries 10 and 11 was followed). The **verifier** re-traced about 700 checks and found three substantive errors, all corrected: Lisnic's corpus is pandemic visualisations of every stance, not "pandemic scepticism", and the two-fifths figure is of sceptics' charts only (60-second version and section 8 rewritten; the "online" that was not in the abstract removed); Kotkov's "up to 8.5%" is an extrapolation to a ratings database — the measured figure is 302 of 2,146 (14 per cent); the Community Life Survey's 15–20 minutes defines a "wider local area", not a second neighbourhood. Flowerdew's remainder counts do not reconcile with his total — the orchestrator's own fetch confirmed 68 and 60, and the chapter now prints those and says the remainder is not reconciled; the three OA/ward/district counts and one sentence of the verdict carry "(read once)". Also fixed: Gale et al.'s stereotype sentence is Gale reporting Vickers; Taylor's sentence marked secondary; Bostonography's "300 submissions" and "for the neighborhood" denominator; Tunstall's "2.6 percentage points" unquoted and "low-skilled" softened; the Do No Harm ellipsis removed; Chong & Druckman's "But"; "rich-get-richer"; Gustafson & Rice, Albo, Feldman and Tintarev 2012 daggered in the summaries; Lee's "post screenshots of graphs" replaced with the sentence that is on the page; the Ask for Angela quotation that could not be found removed; Lynch's "three American cities" removed as from memory; Aadland's initials; Code 3.0's modified date; 29 "(read once)" marks lifted on confirmation.

The **cold reader** (no project context) said what the chapter decided, named a hard line and an open choice, and found the headline evidence. Its strongest objection was accepted in three parts: the crop evidence is about reasoning and context and provenance cures only the context half — the chapter now says so and claims no more; the guardrail study is about superimposed comparison data, not provenance text, and the chapter now takes only its tamper-evidence lesson; and a cropped counted panel with perfect provenance is "the counted won" in a screenshot — each column's frame now carries the bridging sentence, in the truth, the rule, the illustration and the test. The in-frame rule's tier was split (the departure Fixed as a stance; the accessible route Fixed as a procedure; the form open), and section 13's contradictory "may decide" removed. Its runners-up were accepted: the advocacy sentence's "intensity" is counted activity, neither identity nor condition, and the filled example's identity fields were pruned; the OAC naming rule is a rule for naming without blame, not the identity/conditions line; value-first's grounds were separated (order on endorsement alone; presence on the deficit and salience evidence, with FrameWorks stated as a null); the recognition evidence is at named units, which reconciles section 4 with section 3 and gives the rule its form ("found by a name"); the residential bias of every drawn-boundary study is now stated with a flag; "loses its best-known rule" became "loses the reason for". Consistency fixes: four senses and five failures stated as such everywhere; one list of what a cut-out carries, used in the 60-second version, truth 7, the rule, the illustration, the checklist and the test; the recognition record's lines matched; "the one …" claims softened to "reached"; the "slowest layout" compression replaced with the review's words; the dual-axis rule tiered as a stance on a "do not recommend"; the profile-form rule marked † with the flower-chart result stated and the form held open; incomparables re-tiered; Teleport dropped from a Fixed rule's sources; "unit" disambiguated ("a named room or business"); the Kinds legend extended; the glossary extended with the eight gate criteria, spread against ordered list, the poster rule, the three-phones test, the walk, the forum, address, endorsement, intensity, ELS, OA/LSOA/MSOA and MAUP; the tests given pass criteria, numbers and a statement of what they do not measure; the review record kept by role and count, never by name; fourteen unused sources removed; place names and an unsourced policing quotation removed from the illustration. What it asked for and did not get: a figure (none in any chapter; `[Revisit at end]` with the reader); a shorter 60-second version and fewer Fixed rules (left; raised an eighth time); section 2 and section 11 cut (left; the template's sections).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×10. `[Prototype flag]` ×5. `[Validate with users]` ×5. `[Revisit → Ch 13]`, `[→ Ch 14]`, `[→ Ch 16]`. `[Revisit at end]` ×2. `[Counterbalance → Ch 1]`, `[→ Ch 5]`, `[→ Ch 9]`, `[→ Ch 11]`. `[Rich reference]` ×4.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **Gale, Singleton, Bates & Longley 2016, *Creating the 2011 Area Classification for Output Areas*, section 7** (open). How an official place typology was named without blame, in the authors' own rule, with the stereotype and ecological-fallacy cautions beside it — and the names it still produced.
2. **ONS, *Explore Local Statistics* QMI** (25 April 2025). The official no-league-table precedent in one document: the coherence check upstream, the beeswarm, the twenty nearest areas, and the rule that turns a comparison into "higher", "lower" or "similar".
3. **Swire-Thompson, DeGutis & Lazer 2020, *Searching for the Backfire Effect*** (open via Europe PMC). The paper that retires version 1's framing reason, with a practitioner section on what to do instead.
4. **Lisnic et al. 2023, *Misleading Beyond Visual Tricks*, read with Lisnic et al. 2025, *Visualization Guardrails*.** What travels (screenshots, mostly of dashboards), what goes wrong in it (context and reasoning), and the one tested design against the crop, with the share of readers who never saw it.
5. **Coulton, Chan & Mikelbank 2010, *Finding Place in Making Connections Communities*** (Urban Institute). 7,498 people drawing where they live, the exact prompt, the consensus rule — the method for asking a scene the same question.
6. **Tunstall et al. 2014, *Does poor neighbourhood reputation create a neighbourhood effect on employment?*** (open). The one field test of a place-reputation penalty in Britain, and its small, uncertain answer.
7. **Arts Council England, *Methodology for identifying priority places*** (2021). A fully published official method for naming where provision is low — weights, standardisation within region, and the judgement steps it does not hide.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Two Agent calls in one message works.** The cold reader and the verifier overlapped this time; keep doing it.
- **A corpus's description is a quotation.** "Pandemic scepticism" was the chapter's compression of a corpus of every stance; the verifier caught it in the 60-second version and the body. Describe a corpus in the paper's words.
- **An extrapolated figure is not a measured one.** Kotkov's 8.5 per cent was arithmetic on a database; the measured count was 302 of 2,146. Print the count.
- **When the paper's own arithmetic does not reconcile, print what was read and say so.** Flowerdew's remainder.
- **Separate the grounds for an order from the grounds for a presence.** Value-first had three grounds; only one was about order.
- **A rule that touches two chapters' tests is tiered twice.** The in-frame rule is a stance on provenance and a procedure on accessibility.
- **Every "the one study" is "the one study reached", until search is back.** The agents had no web search; the chapter now says "reached".
- **Efficiency change held.** Three orchestrator spot-checks instead of sixteen; the ledger section compiled by a smaller model from the chapter's own Sources section and checked by the orchestrator; the verifier still swept everything. Next chapter starts in a fresh session.

## Entry 13 · Chapter 13 — Accessible as the good version

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 12, in the same session at Carly's instruction ("please progress with Chapter 13"); the efficiency rules from Entry 12 apply. Third chapter of Part 3, The reading experience.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** What does it take for the accessible version of a public dashboard to be the primary version and not a parallel one — which of the W3C's documents are hard lines, which are interpretable guidance, and what each says about an interactive display of data; what the United Kingdom's law requires of a private, non-commercial studio and of the public bodies that would embed or cite its work, in the statute's and the regulator's words and dates; what is measured about how blind, low-vision, cognitively disabled and otherwise disabled readers read charts and maps, and which non-visual routes — a navigable structure, a written account, a table, an audio rendering — have evidence behind them; what official statistics bodies do about their own dashboards' accessibility and what they admit they cannot do; and whether the claim that the good version and the accessible version converge is measured anywhere, or is a stance.

**Sub-questions.**
1. The W3C documents, classified and read at source in full for an interactive data display. WCAG 2.2's conformance model (levels; "conforming alternate version" and the W3C's own caution about it, which is the "parallel accessible version" trap in the standard's words; the "essential" exception); the criteria that bite on a dashboard beyond Chapter 3's and Chapter 11's lists — 1.1.1, 1.3.1, 1.3.2, 1.4.1, 1.4.3, 1.4.4, 1.4.10, 1.4.11, 1.4.12, 1.4.13, 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.2.2 (moving content), 2.3.1 and 2.3.3 (motion), 2.4.3, 2.4.7, 2.4.11, 2.5.1, 2.5.7, 2.5.8, 3.1.5 (AAA), 3.2.x, 4.1.2, 4.1.3 — in their normative words with their Understanding pages' reasons; WAI-ARIA 1.2 and the Graphics module (no chart role); the APG; the WAI tutorial on complex images; COGA's body text for the numeric patterns Chapter 3 flagged; WCAG 3's status and date; the CSS reduced-motion feature; EN 301 549's web clauses as the harmonised standard; the data-visualisation community group's end.
2. The United Kingdom's legal frame, reported with wording and dates, never advised. The Public Sector Bodies (Websites and Mobile Applications) (No. 2) Accessibility Regulations 2018 as amended — who is bound, the exemptions (and in particular the third-party-content exemption, which decides whether version 1's "distribution argument" holds), the accessibility statement, the standard the monitoring body applies and since when (WCAG 2.2 AA); the monitoring body's published results; the Equality Act 2010 — sections 6, 20, 21, 29 and Schedule 2's anticipatory duty, the explanatory notes, the EHRC's services code and its web guidance, and whether any reported case turns on a website; the European Accessibility Act's application from 28 June 2025 and whether a free public information service is in scope; BS 8878/ISO 30071-1 as process standards. Carly's domain enters nowhere; this is the law of websites.
3. How disabled readers read data, measured. The accessible-visualisation literature at source: the four-level model of what descriptions should contain and which levels blind readers want (Lundgard & Satyanarayan 2022); structure/navigation/description and its 13-participant study (Zong et al. 2022) and its tooling (Olli); the design-space survey (Kim et al. 2021) and the call to action (Marriott et al. 2021); VoxLens; audio data narratives (Siu et al.); Chartability's 50 heuristics and its evaluation (Elavsky et al. 2022) and Data Navigator; Chart Reader; screen-reader users' experiences of pandemic dashboards (Fan et al.); alt-text practice studies (Chintalapati et al.; Mack et al.); platform audio graphs (Apple) and library sonification (Highcharts); the sonification literature's comprehension evidence (the Sonification Handbook; Walker & Nees; Flowers); accessible maps (Froehlich et al.'s grand challenges; tactile and screen-reader maps); low vision and magnification; colour vision deficiency prevalence; dyslexia and typography (Rello & Baeza-Yates; the BDA guide); motion and vestibular disorders; the WebAIM screen reader survey (which readers, which browsers, how much mobile) and the WebAIM Million; the UK disability prevalence from the Family Resources Survey; and the convergence question — whether meeting the guidelines measurably helps non-disabled readers (Schmutz, Sonderegger & Sauer; caption use by hearing viewers; Ofcom).
4. What official bodies do and admit. The ONS's and Analysis Function's accessibility guidance for publications, charts, tables and spreadsheets (alt text against chart text; "Dashboards rarely meet full accessibility requirements", flagged by Chapter 11); the accessibility statements of official dashboards — the UKHSA dashboard, the ONS Census maps, Explore Local Statistics, Fingertips, the Scottish public health dashboards — read for the non-compliances they declare; the GOV.UK Design System's accessibility testing of components and its position on text-only versions; the NHS design system; the BBC's accessibility guidelines and its R&D on audio charts; newsroom practice (the FT's and others' accessible-chart posts); Our World in Data's statement; the monitoring body's audit method. Chapter 12's departure — provenance text inside a chart image — tested against 1.4.5 (images of text), 1.1.1 and the Analysis Function's reason.
5. The failures. "It fails if people dont use it" and "if people dont trust it", read for the disabled reader: what is known about abandonment of inaccessible public services; the "text-only version" history and why it died; compliance theatre (overlay widgets) and the measured record against them.
6. The symmetry test. Version 1's positions: the floor inherited whole (keyboard including chart elements; visible focus; 200 per cent and 400 per cent; semantic structure; alt text generated from the current state stating the finding; user override of colour, contrast and font; observed testing); tooling chosen against the floor in week one; charts as navigable structure, not alt-sentence pictures; the accessible tree as a build step; state announcements reuse the honest-surface wording; audio designed once as first-class display serving access and product; data sonification a labelled probe; the generated written story as the guaranteed non-visual route; the community's phone-read test as the reading-level standard (Vibe Setting Framework — confidential; cited, never reproduced); Chartability per release; the distribution argument recorded once; accessibility outranks brand in the hierarchy; no parallel accessible version; the blind-fan test. The house's: Style Guide §13 (the floor); the Decision Book's Chapter 15 and the LNG rules; §02's hierarchy; §04's plain-language register.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** W3C: WCAG 2.2 (Recommendation 12 December 2024) and its Understanding and Techniques documents; "Understanding Conformance" (alternate versions); WAI-ARIA 1.2; WAI-ARIA Graphics Module; ARIA Authoring Practices Guide; WAI Tutorials, Complex Images; COGA, *Making Content Usable* (2021, and any 2024/25 update); WCAG 3.0 Working Draft (latest date); CSS Media Queries Level 5 (prefers-reduced-motion); the Visualization Accessibility Community Group page; ETSI EN 301 549 v3.2.1 and any v4. Law: S.I. 2018/952 as amended by S.I. 2022/; GOV.UK "Understanding accessibility requirements for public sector bodies" and "Accessibility monitoring" reports (GDS/CDDO); the Equality Act 2010 ss. 6, 20, 21, 29, Sch 2 para 2, and its Explanatory Notes; the EHRC Services, Public Functions and Associations Code of Practice (2011; any revision) and its website guidance; Directive (EU) 2019/882; BS 8878:2010; ISO 30071-1:2019. Evidence: Lundgard & Satyanarayan 2022 (TVCG); Zong, Lee, Lundgard, Jang, Hajas & Satyanarayan 2022 (EuroVis); Blanco et al. 2022 (Olli); Kim, Joyner, Riegelhuth & Kim 2021 (CGF); Marriott et al. 2021 (CG&A); Sharif, Wang, Muongchan, Reinecke & Wobbrock 2021/2022 (VoxLens, CHI); Siu, Fan, Kim, Rao, Vazquez, O'Modhrain & Follmer 2022 (CHI); Elavsky, Bennett & Moritz 2022 (CGF); Elavsky, Nadolskis & Moritz 2023 (Data Navigator); Thompson, Martinez, Sarikaya, Cutrell & Lee 2023 (Chart Reader, CHI); Fan, Siu, Rao, Kim, Vazquez, Greco, O'Modhrain & Follmer 2023 (TACCESS); Chintalapati, Bragg & Wang 2022 (ASSETS); Mack et al. 2021 (ASSETS); Jung et al. 2022 (CHI, "Communicating visualizations without visuals"); Apple Audio Graphs (WWDC 2021 documentation); Highcharts sonification documentation; Hermann, Hunt & Neuhoff 2011 (*The Sonification Handbook*); Walker & Nees 2011; Flowers 2005; Froehlich et al. 2019; Gorlewicz et al. 2020; Rello & Baeza-Yates 2013, 2016, 2017; British Dyslexia Association style guide; the NHS/Ofcom material on vestibular disorders and motion; WebAIM Screen Reader User Survey #10 (2024) and the WebAIM Million (2025); the Family Resources Survey 2023/24 disability figures; Schmutz, Sonderegger & Sauer 2016 and 2017 (Human Factors); Ofcom's subtitles research; the BBC GEL accessibility guidelines and BBC R&D sonification work. Official practice: ONS service manual accessibility pages; Analysis Function "Making analytical publications accessible", "Data visualisation: charts" (alt text), "Releasing statistics in spreadsheets", "Making spreadsheets accessible"; the UKHSA data dashboard accessibility statement; the ONS Census maps and ELS accessibility statements; OHID Fingertips' statement; the GOV.UK Design System accessibility strategy and component accessibility notes; the NHS digital service manual accessibility pages; the FT's accessible charts work; Our World in Data's accessibility statement; the monitoring body's audit method page. House: Style Guide v1.5 §13 and the LNG rules; the Decision Book Chapter 15; version 1's Chapter 11 (archived); Chapter 3 §12; Chapter 11 §6; Chapter 12 §8.

**What I expect to find.** That the W3C's hard line is narrower than practice assumes and that its own conformance model treats a separate accessible version as a last resort — so "no parallel version" is the standard's position before it is the house's; that no WCAG criterion names a chart and the chart rules are readings of 1.1.1, 1.3.1, 1.4.1, 1.4.11 and 4.1.2, with the WAI tutorial as the W3C's own reading; that COGA's numeric patterns, once read, are specific enough to become house rules though not conformance ones; that the Regulations exempt third-party content a public body neither funds nor controls, which weakens version 1's "distribution argument" as law and leaves it as reputation — to be reported exactly; that the Equality Act's anticipatory duty is the binding frame for the studio and that WCAG 2.2 AA is the measure of reasonable by practice rather than by statute; that the European Accessibility Act will not reach a free public information service; that the accessible-visualisation evidence is small-n, screen-reader-centred and consistent: blind readers want the statistics and the trends (the four-level model's levels 2 and 3) more than the visual description, navigable structure beats a flat table, and sonification comprehension is real but slow and untrained; that maps remain the hardest case with the least evidence; that meeting the guidelines measurably helps non-disabled readers in the one controlled study that asked, which is the convergence claim's only measurement; that official dashboards declare their own non-compliances in their statements, which is the most useful list of what a dashboard cannot yet do; that overlay widgets have a measured record against them; and that the ONS's "Dashboards rarely meet full accessibility requirements" sits in guidance that treats a dashboard as a form to justify, which Chapter 11 found from the other side.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel, in one message, with web search rationed (the shared budget is 200 calls a turn; each agent is told to use no more than thirty, and to fetch at source): (A) the W3C documents in full for a data display, with conformance and alternate versions, ARIA, the tutorial, COGA's body, WCAG 3's status, EN 301 549; (B) the United Kingdom's legal frame — the Regulations as amended with every exemption's wording, the monitoring body's reports and method, the Equality Act's sections and notes, the EHRC code and guidance, any reported case, the European Act's scope, the process standards; (C) the evidence — the accessible-visualisation literature at source with n and tasks, sonification and audio graphs, maps, low vision, colour vision, dyslexia, motion, the usage surveys, prevalence, and the convergence studies; (D) official and newsroom practice — the ONS and Analysis Function guidance in full, official dashboards' accessibility statements and their declared non-compliances, design systems' testing and positions, the BBC's guidelines and audio work, newsroom accessible-chart practice, overlay widgets' record, and Chapter 12's text-in-image departure against 1.4.5 and the official reason.

### Threads pulled

- **The standard already says it.** WCAG 2.2's conformance text calls an alternate version "a fallback option" and "directly accessible" the "preferred method"; a page conforms whole, each screen-size variation a page; the Working Group would deprecate print-first "primary" versions but "does not feel they can be forbidden". "Last resort" is not the W3C's phrase. The house's rule is stricter than the standard's preference and is marked so.
- **No rule names a chart.** No WCAG criterion does in its normative text; ARIA has no chart role; the Graphics Module (a Recommendation since 2 October 2018) says chart annotation is "Future work"; the chart-roles wiki was last edited 11 September 2015; both community groups closed in 2023; no WAI document is dedicated to charts. What exists is readings the W3C has written: 1.1.1's four-sentence chart example; G92's equivalence test and two worked descriptions; the complex-images tutorial (revised 8 April 2026; three approaches, no longdesc); 1.4.11's lines and slices; 1.4.12's "canvas implementations of text are considered to be images of text"; 2.2.2's auto-updating clause; 2.3.3's vestibular reasons; 4.1.3's "18 results returned" and "chatty". COGA's body text, read: numbers reinforced with words; the 1,500 most common words; a lower-secondary summary; and a user need that asks *for* graphs.
- **The law, reported.** The Regulations' numbering corrected (reg 2 is a revocation); reg 4(2)(e)'s third-party exemption in its exact words and its directive origin; reg 9's dynamic reference to WCAG with no version number; the 2022 and 2025 amendments; the monitoring report (1,203 sites; "nearly all"; ten clean; top failures 2.4.7 and 1.4.3; automated checkers 30–40 per cent; EHRC letters 93, compliance 66; no fines). The Equality Act's sections and Schedule 2's "disabled persons generally"; "anticipatory" is the explanatory notes' word. The 2011 Services Code revoked and the 2026 Code in force on 5 August 2026 — found during the sprint; its Chapter 7 unread at source. No EHRC text read names WCAG. No reported website judgment found. The European Accessibility Act's scope, its microenterprise exemption and the government's "will not directly apply". The distribution argument re-based as funding and reputation; nothing advised.
- **What is measured.** Sharif 2021's 61.48 per cent and 210.96 per cent; Lundgard's four levels and the blind/sighted divergence (the 63 per cent quotation corrected to the paper's words by the verifier); Jung's two to eight sentences; Chintalapati's 50 and 31 per cent; Mack's anchoring finding; Zong's table easier than the multi-view prototype (version 1's "reduced cognitive load" corrected); Fan's audit (7 of 13 tables; 1 of 15 maps; tactile the most-named route) and its two sighted auditors; Biggs's text map against the table (0.44 v 0.23; lower workload on the table; the developer as author); Walker & Nees's twenty minutes; Sharif 2025's 63 per cent never met sonification; Chundury's equal accuracy; Chartability 50/14 and evaluated on confidence; WebAIM survey and Million (worse on failures, errors and contrast; better on alt text — the verifier caught "every count"); FRS 16.8 million; sight loss 2 million; the charity prevalence figures marked; the reading-age folklore (GOV.UK's gone; NHS's unsourced; the 80 per cent a 376-response legal survey); Rello's fonts and 18 point against the BDA's 12–14; Kuster's and Wery's nulls for dyslexia fonts; Schmutz's two experiments in abstract and the 2019 cost; Ofcom's "had used"; Stagetext's 80 per cent of 18–24s.
- **What official bodies admit.** "Dashboards rarely meet full accessibility requirements" is the Analysis Function's (Chapter 11 had it as the ONS's); its Q&A's "yet to find a fully accessible dashboard"; the ONS and the Analysis Function disagree on where a description lives; every official statement read "partially compliant" (UKHSA unreviewed since December 2023; PHS's criterion-by-criterion list; Fingertips still on 2.1); platforms' claims; the Design System's "does not immediately make that service accessible"; overlays (WebAIM 67/72 per cent; FTC $1 million, 2025); text-only versions' staleness unmeasured.
- **Chapter 12's departure tested.** 1.4.5 is met "where images of text are used in addition to text … and where both are presented to the user"; graphs are named as pictures with "significant other visual content"; PHS's one declared failure was a title in the image only; the rule restated with its condition (SVG text on the page, HTML beside, baked into the export; never the image alone; the identifying items, not the long description).
- **The cold reader's strongest objection and its answer.** The level-4 ban collided with the W3C's "implications" and COGA's "guided interpretation"; resolved by placing interpretation in the authored account, on the page for every reader, and keeping the chart's description to the chart — type, figures, trend — which is what both the W3C's equivalence test and the blind readers' ranking ask.
- **Negative findings, stated as such.** No controlled comparison of description, table and structure for one chart; no convergence measurement for charts; no UK official prevalence for colour vision deficiency, dyslexia or vestibular disorders; no sourced reading age; no UK regulator statement on overlays; no reported website judgment; EHRC Code Chapter 7, the reduced-motion definition, the aria-live table and EN v4.1.1 clause 9 unreached.

### Sources read

See SOURCES §17 (90 rows). Four agent returns kept whole in `02-research-notes/ch13-agent-returns.md`. Under the efficiency rules, the orchestrator spot-checked three pages (Understanding Conformance; Understanding 1.4.5; the Analysis Function dashboards page — all held) and the verifier re-traced about 430 items across about 171 pages.

### What I learned

That the standard's own conformance model is the argument against a parallel accessible version, and that the house's rule goes one step past it. That a chart has no rule of its own and so is held to readings the W3C has written down, which are specific enough to build to. That what blind readers want from a description is the chart's statistics and trend and not the author's view, and that this is a reason to put interpretation in the authored account for everyone rather than a reason to strip it from the page. That the table is the type most often rated accessible and loses on geography. That audio has a smaller evidence base than its vendors say and is an extra. That the Regulations do not bind the studio and exempt what a public body embeds unfunded, that the Act's duty is anticipatory, that no regulator's text read names WCAG, and that none of that is advice. That every official dashboard admits partial compliance and falls back on the data in another form, and that their declared failures are the test plan. That convergence is measured for pages and not for charts, so the title claim is a stance with a gap. And that the trade's reading-age figure has no source.

### What I left out, and why

- Legal application to the Vibe Map or the studio: reported texts only; the adviser's questions listed in section 12.
- The EHRC 2026 Code's Chapter 7 beyond a secondary site; the 2011 Code's text (revoked).
- The BBC's guidelines and R&D, the FT's and the Guardian's practice (domains blocked); Flowers 2005; Schmutz 2018 at source.
- Topic findings of every study (pandemic opinion, film ratings, legal writing, census questions): read for method only.
- Any decision about the Vibe Map's routes, type, statement wording or test numbers: recorded as choices; the filled example labelled invented.
- The deaf reader of a dashboard about sound (transcript or caption for any audio content; 1.4.2): not treated; flagged to Chapter 14.
- The 60-second version is about 1,400 words; raised a ninth time.

### Open questions

Carried in the chapter's flags: convergence for charts; a "where" route for the map; the frame's items at phone size; status-message wording `[Prototype flag]`; the blind-fan question; recorded sound as content; the phone-read test against COGA's level; finding the description in body text `[Validate with users]`; eight `[Further research]` items led by the Code's Chapter 7 and the three unreached specification texts; `[Revisit → Ch 14]` the creative treatment under the floor and the deaf reader; `[Revisit → Ch 16]` the statement and the walks as release gates; `[Revisit at end]` ten records and the 60-second version.

### Looking back

Recorded in full in the chapter. In brief: strengthens Chapter 1's non-use failure with the measured gap; Chapter 2's official reader with the statement and the exemption; Chapter 3's classification, completed, and corrects its "four approaches" `[Counterbalance → Ch 3]`; Chapter 4's table rules; Chapter 7's and Chapter 9's words-for-numbers as COGA's patterns; Chapter 8's table twin and tooling rule with 1.4.10's limit and 1.4.12's canvas sentence; Chapter 10's reported-not-advised; Chapter 11's sentence as the long description and its status rule as 4.1.3, and corrects its attribution of "rarely meet" `[Counterbalance → Ch 11]`; Chapter 12's frame rule, tested and conditioned. Version 1: the floor mapped and extended; "reduced cognitive load" corrected; the distribution argument re-based; the blind-fan test rewritten; the convergence claim half-confirmed. Decision Book: no conflict; the accessibility rules gain six criteria; a candidate section, *The statement*. Design system: a status region, a reduced-motion variant, a table-twin pattern, a long-description block, a pause control, unobscured focus, a statement template, the test platforms — logged, not designed; one tension on body size.

### Cold read and verification — what they found, and what changed in draft 2

Sent together in one message; they ran concurrently. The **verifier** re-traced about 430 items and found thirteen wrong, all corrected: Lundgard's 63 per cent sentence restored to the paper's words ("should not contain an author's subjective interpretations, contextual information, or editorializing about the visualized data"); the WebAIM Million "worse on every count" (alt text improved); two Analysis Function wordings ("within an image"; "an image of a chart") and a mis-joined "decorative" clause; the 2026 Code's para 1.6 quoted whole; truth 5's "2.2 since October 2024" re-worded to the government's sentence; Sharif's "entirely indetectable" restored and re-sourced to the interviews; "One sentence is what most alt text is" withdrawn against the chapter's own source (3.9 sentences each); "half the surveyed users" for tactile (50 of 127) corrected; "screen-reader auditors rated" corrected to seven of thirteen tables, with the auditors named as two sighted and one blind; the Chartability confidence quotation replaced with the abstract's; Kuster's samples split by experiment. Reshaped items fixed: Fan's 94 per cent given its pandemic context; Marriott's "largely heuristic" scoped to cognitive disabilities; the automated-checker claim narrowed to keyboard, focus and reflow; "maps the worst" to "the lowest of the main types"; the regulator-and-WCAG negative narrowed to what was read; "no WAI document on charts" to "none dedicated to"; G92's test placed in its description; the Design System's screen-reader list re-framed as its own statement; the monitoring method sentences re-sourced to the guidance page; the alternate version's three reach conditions; Biggs's numeric questions as "less differentiation"; the burden table by type of test; Rello 2016's sentence replaced with the one on the page; the airline case "reported secondhand as settled"; "No UK court" to "none found"; Trudeau daggered; González-Sordé 2024; 26 "(read once)" marks lifted and the remaining two lifted after the orchestrator's own re-fetch.

The **cold reader** (no project context) said what the chapter decided, named a hard line and an open choice, and found the headline evidence. Its strongest objection was accepted and changed the chapter's spine: the ban on level-4 content in a chart's description collided with the W3C's "implications comparable to those available from the chart", with COGA's "guided interpretation", and with the chapter's own claim that the written account "carries meaning". Draft 2 separates the two things: the description that stands in for the chart carries the chart (type, figures, trend), and interpretation lives in the authored account on the page, read by everyone — which is what the W3C's equivalence test asks, what blind readers ranked, and the convergence point the chapter was looking for. Also accepted: section 12's open choices that the rules had already closed (the statement, the description's location, the AAA criteria, the type) are now rules, and section 12 keeps only what is open; the tests gained pass criteria, a scanner, the phone layout, a "where" question for maps and a time ratio; the status region is not in the tab order and does not read the sentence; the frame's repetition is of the identifying text, not the long description, which answers the "never both" collision; the figcaption-against-caption tension is logged; "a quarter of the population cannot read" withdrawn; the standard's permission for a tailored version stated beside the house's stricter rule; "the prototype closes the gap" softened to "begins to measure it"; the Mack finding placed as a caution about the reading step rather than a bar on templates; legal-advice-shaped sentences re-worded as reports of texts ("the text that bears on a private provider"; "on the government's reading"); councils and statements named; kinds and sub-kinds added to the legend; the deaf reader flagged to Chapter 14. What it asked for and did not get: a worked description beside its chart (none in any chapter; `[Revisit at end]` with the figure question); a ranking of the Fixed rules by priority (left for Chapter 16's register); effect sizes for the convergence experiments (not in the abstracts; flagged); a shorter 60-second version and fewer summaries (left; raised a ninth time).

### Flags raised this sprint

`[Further research]` ×8. `[Prototype flag]` ×4. `[Validate with users]` ×4. `[Revisit → Ch 14]`, `[→ Ch 16]`. `[Revisit at end]` ×2. `[Counterbalance → Ch 3]`, `[→ Ch 11]`. `[Rich reference]` ×4.

### Rich references for Carly

1. **W3C, Understanding Conformance, "Why permit alternate versions?"** — the standard's own case against a parallel accessible version, in the Working Group's voice, with the hard-copy "primary" passage.
2. **W3C, Understanding SC 1.4.5 Images of Text** — the paragraph that decides the chart-frame question: met where the image's text is also presented as text.
3. **GDS, *Accessibility monitoring of public sector websites and mobile apps from 2022 to 2024*** (17 December 2024) — what fails in practice, in what proportions, and what scanners miss.
4. **Lundgard & Satyanarayan 2022, the accessible HTML version** — the four levels, and the one measured divergence between what blind and sighted readers want from a description.
5. **Fan et al. 2023** — the audit of 87 live visualisations by type, the survey of 127 screen-reader users, and the preference for tactile.
6. **Public Health Scotland's accessibility statement** (18 May 2026) — the most specific official list of what R and Tableau dashboards fail, criterion by criterion; a test plan by negation.
7. **Analysis Function, *Making analytical publications accessible*** (2020) with its *dashboards* and *dashboard software* pages (February 2026) — the official rules for a chart's description, text in images, data downloads, and the tools' limits.

### Method notes for the next sprint

- **Two Agent calls in one message works**, twice running now.
- **A summary's quotation is checked against the paper, not the return.** The 63 per cent sentence was "STRING-VERIFIED" in the return and wrong; the verifier's non-leading read caught it. Ask the verifier for non-leading reads on the load-bearing quotations.
- **"Every" and "worst" are claims.** "Worse on every count" and "maps the worst" each failed on one item.
- **Separate what a thing carries from where interpretation goes.** The level-4 rule was right about the description and wrong about the page.
- **An open choice is not open if a rule closed it.** Check section 12 against the rules table before the cold read.
- **A test needs a pass.** Each walk now states one.
- **Legal sentences are re-read for the verb.** "Reaches", "binds", "is exempt" applied to the studio are advice-shaped; "the text that bears" and "on the government's reading" are reports.
- **Efficiency held.** Three spot-checks; the ledger by a smaller model; agents' search rationed to thirty each; the verifier used two of ten searches. Chapter 14 next, in this session if Carly says so.
