## Entry 14 · Chapter 14 — Where evidence meets expression

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 13, in the same session at Carly's instruction. First chapter of Part 4, The boundary and the practice.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** Where is the line between the evidence layer and the expressive one — what must stay true under any creative treatment of a public dashboard, how is that tested (the removal test), what does the embellishment and affect evidence license and on what conditions, what do the conventions of "objective-looking" display themselves do, and what does the strongest work in the field do when read for what each piece breaks and why it still holds? The brief's sixth question answered as a boundary and not as a brief: "the boundary rules: what must stay true under any creative treatment … Not the creative decisions — the line they may not cross."

**Sub-questions.**
1. What the field says expression is for, and how it is judged. The pragmatic–artistic continuum and the case for visualisation criticism (Kosara 2007); "artistic data visualization" (Viégas & Wattenberg 2007); the information-aesthetics model (Lau & Vande Moere 2007); design, art and science as the triangle a visualisation sits in (Vande Moere & Purchase 2011); casual information visualisation (Pousman, Stasko & Mateas 2007); critical visualisation (Dörk et al. 2013); data feminism's chapter on emotion and embodiment (D'Ignazio & Klein 2020); the ethics of visualisation research (Correll 2019) and "black hat" visualisation (Correll & Heer 2017); and the formal frame the house's tier test needs — Mackinlay's expressiveness and effectiveness criteria (1986) and Munzner's nested model (2009), in which "nothing load-bearing in decoration" has a name.
2. What the conventions of objectivity do. Kennedy, Hill, Aiello & Allen (2016) on the four conventions — two-dimensional viewpoints, clean layouts, geometric shapes and lines, the inclusion of data sources — that "do the work" of making a visualisation look objective; Lee et al.'s sceptics using "orthodox" aesthetics (Chapter 12); Hullman & Diakopoulos's visualisation rhetoric (Chapter 11); the Style Guide's own line, "Strong design must never make weak evidence look stronger than it is", read against the finding that plainness is also a rhetoric; the beauty-to-trust path (Lin & Thornton, Chapter 11).
3. What the affect and persuasion evidence licenses. Beyond Chapter 3's embellishment line (Bateman; Borkin; Haroz; Skau; Borgo; Inbar; Ajani — cited, not re-run) and Chapter 2's persuasion line (Pandey; Liem; Allcott): the anthropographics null — Boy et al. 2017 ("Showing people behind data") and Morais et al. 2021 — whether humanising a chart raises empathy or giving; affective visualisation design (Lan et al. 2023; Lan et al. 2021 on infographics and on animated charts); affective learning objectives (Lee-Robbins & Adar 2022); affective colour (Bartram, Patra & Stone 2017; Anderson & Robinson 2021); proximity techniques (Campbell & Offenhuber 2019); redundant encoding (Nothelfer, Gleicher & Franconeri 2017) as the measured ground for the precise-twin rule; dark backgrounds and legibility (display-polarity studies); physicalisation's measured efficiency (Jansen et al. 2013); glanceable displays; what is measured about reading at a distance and in motion (sign legibility), which is the only literature on the poster's conditions.
4. The removal test, formalised. The Decision Book's "name the purchase, ship the precise version alongside" and its tier test (house); Mackinlay's criterion applied as a procedure: strip the expressive layer — mono, silent, still, undressed — and the encoding must still express all and only the data; what the official bodies say about decoration and infographics (the Analysis Function; GOV.UK; the ONS); the W3C's "essential" exception (Chapter 13) as the standard's own version of the line.
5. The gallery, read for breaks. A set of studied exemplars audited live: The Pudding's exemplar-first pieces; Our World in Data; the ONS's local statistics service and 331 stories; the BBC's postcode interactives; Gapminder's ignorance test and Dollar Street; Fragapane's *Noise Pollution* and Viégas & Wattenberg's *Wind Map* as museum-collected data works (the collection records verified); *Dear Data* (Lupi & Posavec) and Lupi's data-humanism manifesto as practice; Mona Chalabi's hand-drawn work and its 2023 Pulitzer as the public record of an expressive register in journalism; *The Fallen of World War II*; Raworth's doughnut as a display; the Information is Beautiful Awards' criteria as the field's stated standard; and the incumbents version 1 read (the Thriving Places Index; a gated-results index; the London observatory) only as rules' case law, never re-researched for content. For each: what it breaks of this book's rules, what substitutes, why it holds or fails, and whether it was ever evaluated (the "evaluated exception" — the one interactive with a published evaluation).
6. The deaf reader of a dashboard about sound. WCAG 1.2.1 (audio-only: a text alternative), 1.2.2 (captions), 1.2.3/1.2.5 (audio description) and 1.4.2 (audio control), in their normative words; what a page owes when sound is content.
7. The symmetry test. Version 1's positions: the art lives in the decorative tier and the register, load-bearing meaning never only there; every expressive form names its purchase and ships the precise version alongside; no beauty exemption from the audit; the prototype genre is the culture's visual tradition, not the dashboard's ("the claim decides the genre"); titles and text carry the message so that decoration can be free; the poster discipline on headlines; salient distinctiveness over normalised ornament; strong evidence never made to look weaker by timid design; the expressive-moves palette, each priced; the committee export "at lower volume"; the two-audience test; the removal test (mono, silent, still, undressed); the gallery's strong-work pattern (words and structure carry the message; craft spent on one authored thing; interaction optional) and failure pattern; the evaluated exception as the probes' standard; definition-as-interaction and the misconception quiz as probe candidates. The house's: the Skiddle-call instruction (house, quoted in version 1), the five-word mantra, Style Guide §06 and the tiers, the Decision Book's Chapters 7, 10 and 18.

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Kosara 2007 (IV); Viégas & Wattenberg 2007 (IV); Lau & Vande Moere 2007; Vande Moere & Purchase 2011 (*Information Visualization* 10(4)); Pousman, Stasko & Mateas 2007 (TVCG); Dörk, Feng, Collins & Carpendale 2013 (CHI); D'Ignazio & Klein 2020 (*Data Feminism*, open access, chapter 3); Correll 2019 (CHI); Correll & Heer 2017 (VIS workshop); Mackinlay 1986 (ACM TOG); Munzner 2009 (TVCG); Kennedy, Hill, Aiello & Allen 2016 (*Information, Communication & Society*); Boy, Pandey, Emerson, Satterthwaite, Nov & Bertini 2017 (CHI); Morais, Jansen, Andrade & Dragicevic 2020 (TVCG) and 2021 (CHI); Lan, Shi, Wu, Jiao & Cao 2021 (TVCG, "Smile or scowl"); Lan, Shi, Zhang & Cao 2021 (TVCG, Kineticharts); Lan, Wu & Cao 2023 (TVCG, "Affective visualization design"); Lee-Robbins & Adar 2022 (TVCG); Bartram, Patra & Stone 2017 (CHI); Anderson & Robinson 2021 (TVCG); Campbell & Offenhuber 2019 (*Information Design Journal*); Nothelfer, Gleicher & Franconeri 2017 (TVCG); Jansen, Dragicevic & Fekete 2013 (CHI); Piepenbrock, Mayr, Mund & Buchner 2013 (*Ergonomics*) and Dobres, Chahine & Reimer 2017 (*Applied Ergonomics*) on display polarity; Blascheck et al. 2019 (TVCG, glanceable); Garvey, Pietrucha & Meeker 1997 and the UK Transport Research Laboratory on sign legibility; the Decision Book Chapters 7, 10, 18; Style Guide v1.5 §06 and the tier definitions; the Analysis Function's and GOV.UK's guidance on infographics and images; the ONS service manual on images; WCAG 2.2 1.2.1, 1.2.2, 1.2.3, 1.2.5, 1.4.2; MoMA's collection records for *Wind Map* and *Noise Pollution*; the Pulitzer Prize's 2023 Illustrated Reporting and Commentary citation; the Information is Beautiful Awards' criteria; Lupi's "Data Humanism" (2017); *Dear Data* (2016); the live pages of each gallery entry; Boyd, Dörk et al.'s evaluated flowchart if it exists as described (to be verified or withdrawn).

**What I expect to find.** That the field has a thirty-year literature on the expressive end of visualisation that is mostly conceptual and almost unmeasured, and that its best contribution to this book is a vocabulary — expressiveness against effectiveness; the pragmatic–artistic continuum; casual visualisation — in which the house's tiers already sit. That the conventions of objectivity are themselves a rhetoric, so the Style Guide's "never make weak evidence look stronger" cuts both ways and the chapter will need to say so without licensing the opposite. That the affect evidence is small and mixed: humanising charts did not move empathy or giving in the two large studies, animation and colour can carry a mood without changing the reading, and the measured gains of expression are attention and memory, not accuracy or persuasion — which is exactly what a boundary rule needs, because it tells the house what expression buys and what it cannot be allowed to touch. That redundancy is measured to help, which grounds the precise twin. That dark grounds cost legibility by a measurable amount, which the house's night register must price. That the removal test is Mackinlay's criterion as a procedure and the official bodies' guidance on infographics is the same test by another name. That the gallery's strong work shares the pattern version 1 found, that almost none of it was evaluated, that the two museum-collected data works are real and the "evaluated exception" may not survive verification, and that the incumbents' failures are this book's rules' case law. And that the deaf reader of a dashboard about sound is owed a transcript or caption by Level A criteria the floor already includes.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel, in one message, with search rationed to thirty calls each: (A) the theory and criticism — Kosara, Viégas & Wattenberg, Lau & Vande Moere, Vande Moere & Purchase, Pousman, Dörk, D'Ignazio & Klein, Correll, Mackinlay, Munzner, Kennedy et al.'s conventions, Lupi's manifesto as practice; (B) the affect and perception evidence — anthropographics, affective design, affective colour, proximity, redundancy, polarity, physicalisation, glanceability, sign legibility; (C) the gallery audited live — each entry's current state, what it shows first, its provenance furniture, its interaction, its evaluation if any, the museum records, the Pulitzer citation, the awards criteria, the evaluated-exception claim verified or withdrawn; (D) the official and newsroom line on decoration — the Analysis Function, GOV.UK and ONS on infographics and images, newsroom style guides' rules on decoration and text, the WCAG audio criteria, and the design-system positions the house's conflict log will meet.

### Researched (6 October 2026)

Four agents returned in one pass; returns assembled at `02-research-notes/ch14-agent-returns.md` (about 27,500 words). Agent A (theory and criticism, 24 searches) corrected the brief four times: Kennedy et al. 2016 is thirteen designer interviews plus a semiotic reading of ten pieces, with the focus groups in a sister paper (nine groups, 46 people); Viégas & Wattenberg 2007 contains no sublime (it is Kosara's); Kosara's phrase is "opposite ends of the sublimity scale", never "continuum"; the Fragapane/MoMA claim is true, three records. Unreached: Lau & Vande Moere and Vande Moere & Purchase in full, Kostelnick 2008, Munzner's textbook, Tufte's books. Agent B (affect and perception, 30 searches) corrected four citations (Nothelfer is JEP:HPP 43(9), not TVCG; Anderson & Robinson TVCG 28(8) 2022; Garvey 1997 is a Clearview study with no case finding; Lazard & Atkinson measured elaboration, not credibility) and reached the anthropographics analysis pages, the Kineticharts and Smile-or-Scowl PDFs, Bartram, Piepenbrock, Nothelfer, Ottley, Blascheck, Garvey 2016, the USSC standard, Bullough, the Traffic Signs Manual and the Xie meta-analysis; HAL, ACM, PMC and ScienceDirect blocked. Agent C (the gallery, 11 searches) audited every reachable entry live by text (not pixels); the BBC, NYT, Guardian, Reuters, FT and WaPo could not be read; the MoMA records, the Pulitzer citation, the IIB criteria, the Fanning & Raworth caption and the Pomerance, Boyd & Dörk paper were verified at source; the evaluated-exception claim held with three qualifications. Agent D (official and newsroom line, 14 searches) read the Analysis Function's five pages, GOV.UK content and Design System, NHS, ONS (nine pages), the five WCAG audio-related Understanding pages and 1.1.1, Material, Carbon, OSR's Code 3.0 and three casework items, the GLA guide, the EU guide, FT, Datawrapper, the Economist, Urban Institute, ProPublica and Sunlight; found the DWP two-houses case and the W3C's "specific sensory experience" category.

**Orchestrator spot-checks (four, per the efficiency rules).** MoMA record 163892 (artist line, title, date, medium, "Gift of the designers", 1751.2012; "Purchase" absent) — held. Anthropographics Experiment 2 analysis page ("Effect 1 = 3.4, 95% CI [0.021, 6.8]"; "Number of valid submissions: 786") — held; the standardised-effect string the agent marked verified was not found by this fetch, so the chapter marks it read once. Fanning & Raworth Figure 1 caption, both sentences ("quadratic scaling of wedge area"; "bar-chart representation that avoids this quadratic scaling issue") — held; the false control "proportional to the area" correctly absent. Understanding 1.1.1 (pure-decoration definition; the rearranged-or-substituted note; "a performance of a flute solo") — held.

### Written (6 October 2026)

Draft 1 written in three parts (~30,400 words), then style-checked (one "not X; it is Y" reworded; the banned words absent outside quotations). Cold reader and verifier ran together in one message.

**The verifier** (fidelity against the returns, then twelve fetches at source) found no source quotation differing in wording from the returns across about 850 quoted strings. It found: about twenty single-pass, abstract or secondary items repeated without their mark (mostly in the 60-second version and section 2 — all marked); the 54 per cent in the flowchart study is sessions, not time (corrected); "25 studies" should be "25 empirical articles" (corrected); the humanised-marks rule called a hypothetical-allocation effect an "empathy effect" (corrected); the raw Morais interval does not include zero though the standardised one does (corrected); Kineticharts asks three comprehension questions, not four, and reports engagement — including focused attention — significantly higher than baseline (corrected; the engagement result is now the chapter's one measured attention gain); the captions "non-speech information" sentence is Understanding intent text, not the criterion (marked); 1.2.3's title (corrected); two Chalabi quotations attributed to the wrong interview, and two interviews joined with a causal "because" (separated); "harmful junk" is the EU guide quoting Kosara (attributed); the Urban Institute is not a newsroom (corrected); the DWP houses "drawn to fit their captions" was stated as fact where the guidance offers it as a possible innocent explanation (hedged); "attention, mood and memory" claimed a memory gain no study in the chapter measures (re-based to engagement, mood and recognition, with memorability as Chapter 3's line); the strong-work pattern over-generalised "leads with a sentence" to entries that lead with a mode choice (qualified); "never action" overstated against a small non-zero allocation effect (qualified); "six glances to one" came from one eighteen-person study in which the donut matched the bar (qualified); the five-page Analysis Function negative was the infographics page's (corrected); the "orchestrator-confirmed" wording in Sources renamed "re-checked at source for this chapter"; Space Junk, 110269 and the press release marked read once; three uncited Sources entries removed. At source, eight of ten targets confirmed (Kosara "stand on its own"; Munzner's abstraction sentence; Kennedy's "no need for the user to check"; Kineticharts; Mackinlay's bar sentence; the AF two-houses section; the IIB criteria; Correll's glyph sentence), with seventeen false controls rejected; the flowchart paper's body and the Springer meta-analysis were unreachable.

**The cold reader** (a senior reader meeting the framework for the first time) made the chapter's spine explicit: the removal test presupposed a sort it claimed to perform, and a lost fact was described three ways. Accepted and rewritten: section 6 now states the sort (the designer declares each element's layer; the test checks every element declared expressive), and distinguishes a lost fact (a wrong sort — the element returns to the evidence layer, is judged as an encoding and must meet the floor) from an added fact (a breach); the fifth check is a separate line of the log; the log is eleven lines, with status and owner, a glance-cost estimate for forms, and a reader comparison whose sample is the user groups' (two readers is the shape of the test, not its size); shipping is gated by the removals and the fifth check, the comparison may follow in a probe. Also accepted: "register" had two senses, so the record is renamed the *boundary log* and "register" defined; a glossary of earlier-chapter dependencies added to the working words (the ten records by name; tier test; floor; cut-out rule; identity line; gate; base; probes; two-audience test); Munzner's line placed inside the encoding level in the 60-second version to match the body; redundancy and the twin separated; truth 6 reworded so the Doughnut's caption-and-bar is the rule done rather than its breach; "Fixed = expressiveness" qualified (floor and disclosure rules are Fixed too); the "measured to buy" list harmonised; the two polarity abstracts shown to disagree on lighting; the capitals finding shown as a disagreement between literatures; the audit order given in three steps everywhere; sound-as-content distinguished from a sensory experience; genre moved from Free to Priced; the "not the studio's" paragraph re-titled "not open" and the Adaptable choice among effective encodings returned to the studio; research-process jargon ("control string returned NO") removed from the body; the Sunlight Foundation named; the flowchart named; the sign literature's presence explained; several "not X; it is Y" constructions and three self-congratulatory sentences reworded; two checklist items added. Not accepted: cutting the symmetry test (the template requires it); cutting the theory that yields no rule (Dörk, Correll's obligations, Kosara's rules of criticism are the grounds for sections 7 and 11's stance on criticism); and the 60-second version at 200 words — it stands at the length of the earlier chapters' and is logged again under the question Carly has not yet answered.

Draft 2: about 32,400 words; 122 "read once" marks; the dagger on every abstract-only figure. Ledger §18 (82 rows) compiled by a smaller model from the chapter's Sources section and checked at the Morais, Fanning & Raworth, MoMA and Pomerance rows.

### What was found that was not asked for

The field's main prize does not score truth: eight criteria, none on accuracy, and the description text judged alongside the work. Fanning & Raworth print their own perceptual defect in the caption and supply a bar chart — the fifth check done by the authors in a *Nature* paper. Chalabi's "pixel-for-pixel" statement: the drawn layer is registered to a computed chart. Stated preference and logged behaviour diverge in the flowchart study (40 per cent say narration; 54 per cent of sessions mostly explored; 7 per cent balanced). JoVI publishes its review rounds and an Open Practices Review, which records that the evaluation materials could not be opened. A MoMA label states that information design "goes beyond mere description to become an expressive interpretation of reality". The W3C has a third category — the specific sensory experience — that the house's tiers lack. The ONS licenses, and reasons about, emotion in its brand pattern in the same manual that forbids chart decoration. The DWP two-houses infographic is the government's own teaching case of a decorative container read as an encoding. Capitals out-read lower case in all 31 fonts at threshold legibility; the mixed-case advantage is for recognising a known word. "Seven words" has no source. The disfluency effect behind "visual difficulties" is null on recall across 3,135 participants. Text and chart together did not raise accuracy and were not integrated. For charts, dark against light polarity helps different individuals in roughly equal proportion. Three museums hold *Flight Patterns* under three dates and two flight counts. Both incumbents have drifted further since version 1 read them. Unread: arXiv 2607.01593 (rate-limited) and Gomasta et al. on music in data videos (abstract only).

### Looking back (6 October 2026)

Recorded in the chapter's own section. In brief: strengthens Chapters 0, 1, 2, 4, 6, 7, 8, 10 and 13; counterbalances 3 (memorable, and image-rated displays convey less), 11 (a plain-to-trust path beside beauty-to-trust; genre re-tiered) and 12 (the orthodox look is available to anyone). Version 1's Chapters 12 and 13: most positions stand; genre and the headline count re-tiered Adaptable; the MoMA proof re-stated as custody; the removal test formalised with a sort before it and a fifth check after; the committee export gains precedents; the evaluated exception specified and qualified. Carly can decide later whether the eleven records are one document.

### Rich references

Fanning & Raworth 2025 (*Nature* 646), the Figure 1 caption — the authors' own fifth check. Pomerance, Boyd & Dörk 2026 (*JoVI* 1(1)) with its published reviews — the evaluated exception, specified. Kennedy, Hill, Aiello & Allen 2016, accepted manuscript — the conventions of objectivity in the designers' own words, with a method section that bounds the claim. Morais et al. 2021's open analysis site — every estimate with intervals. Arunkumar et al. 2023 — the one reader study separating aesthetic pleasure from taking the message. The Analysis Function's §10 "Presentation really matters" — the DWP houses told by government against itself.

### Delivered (6 October 2026)

`04-chapters/ch14-where-evidence-meets-expression.md` (draft 2); `02-research-notes/ch14-agent-returns.md`; `01-sources/SOURCES.md` §18; this entry; `00-plan/STATE.md`. Carly reviews and commits.

## Entry 15 · Chapter 15 — Learning from the best

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 14, in the same session at Carly's instruction ("please proceed with chapter 15 … please dont change the method at the final furlong"). Second chapter of Part 4. The full method: framed here first; common brief; four research agents in one message with search rationed; returns assembled; orchestrator spot-checks of the load-bearing items; draft in three parts; style check; cold reader and verifier together; revision by exact replacement; ledger by a smaller model, checked; log; STATE; delivery to the repo folder; Carly commits.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** Sector by sector — public health dashboards, finance and risk, economic development and place, the culture and charity sectors' impact practice, and the great public data projects — what did the best public data displays get right about trust, provenance, change over time and reach; how is each of those four properties measured or evidenced where it is; what did each sector get wrong or leave unsolved; and what, as method and never as content, does a public dashboard about a place's culture borrow from each? The brief's words: "what they got right about trust, provenance, change over time and reach, and what nightlife borrows" (Q1, Q2). The brief also records that this chapter "could fold into Chapters 2 and 5" and is kept because "take inspiration from the best in the world" was named; the chapter must therefore earn its separation by finding things Chapters 2 and 5 did not, and must say so in its symmetry test if it does not.

**Sub-questions.**
1. *The four properties, made measurable.* Trust: the Public Confidence in Official Statistics survey series and what it measures; trust in a display as distinct from trust in its producer (Chapter 2's line, cited); what the dashboards that report usage report. Provenance: metric documentation, change logs, vintages (real-time data archives such as ALFRED), revision policies and revision triangles, "last updated / next expected update", data-as-code releases. Change over time: how the exemplars show it — fan charts and their evaluated comprehension, indicator "direction of travel" rules (Scotland's National Performance Framework arrows; the Health Index), significance banding against a comparator with confidence intervals (Fingertips), revisions shown as revisions. Reach: who reads these things, measured — published usage figures, the audiences named in user research, the evaluations of COVID dashboards' actionability.
2. *Public health.* The UKHSA (formerly PHE) COVID-19 dashboard: its public metric documentation, change log, API, user research and published usage; OHID's Fingertips: the benchmarking method (confidence intervals; "significantly better/worse/similar"; the RAG and quintile schemes), the indicator metadata template, the "compared with England" rule; Public Health Scotland's dashboards; the Johns Hopkins CSSE dashboard (its *Lancet Infectious Diseases* paper; its retirement in March 2023 and why); Our World in Data's COVID datasets (the *Scientific Data* papers on testing and vaccinations); the evaluations of COVID dashboards (Ivanković et al. 2021 JMIR on "actionable" dashboards; Barbazza et al.; the dashboard-fatigue literature); the WHO dashboard. Read for method; no finding about any place.
3. *Finance and risk.* Central-bank communication to the public and its measured effects (Haldane & McMahon 2018; Bholat, Broughton, Ter Meer & Walczak 2019 on simplified and visual summaries, which was an experiment; the Bank of England's layered Monetary Policy Report); the fan chart (the Bank's since 1996; the OBR's; evaluations of fan-chart comprehension); the ESRB and ECB risk dashboards (what a "risk dashboard" is for a regulator; its indicator set and colour rules); FRED and ALFRED (vintages as provenance; "real-time data" literature — Croushore & Stark); the IMF's Special Data Dissemination Standard (release calendars; advance release); the ONS revisions policy and revision triangles; the UK Statistics Authority's pre-release access rules; what finance got right about change over time (vintages, revisions, uncertainty) and what it gets wrong (the look of precision; the heatmap that ranks).
4. *Economic development and place.* The ONS's Explore Local Statistics (Chapter 11 and 12 have it; here its user research and evaluation, its "similar areas" method, its subnational strategy); Nomis (longevity since the 1980s; provenance; its users); the English Indices of Deprivation (the technical report's rules on what a rank may and may not say; the guidance against "X is twice as deprived as Y"; the decile rule); the ONS Health Index (method; change over time as the design goal; its 2024 status); Scotland's National Performance Framework (the "performance improving/maintaining/worsening" method and its thresholds); the Welsh national indicators and well-being of future generations reporting; the OECD Regional Well-Being tool (eleven topics, no composite rank — verify); the Centre for Cities' Cities Outlook and data tool; the Thriving Places Index (Chapter 5 and 14 have its state; here only whether its method PDF is readable and what it says about change over time); the What Works Centre for Local Economic Growth's evidence standards (the Maryland scale) as the sector's own hierarchy; the Levelling Up missions' metrics annex (what the government chose to track and at what geography).
5. *Culture and charity impact practice.* Arts Council England's quality metrics line — the 2016 national test, Culture Counts, the Impact & Insight Toolkit, its 2019–2023 evaluations, and Illuminate as its successor (what changed and why, in ACE's words); the Audience Agency's liquidation (10 September 2026) and what has become of Audience Spectrum and Audience Finder since; the DCMS Participation Survey and the Community and Engagement Survey that may succeed it (status); the Culture and Heritage Capital programme's valuation guidance (DCMS; what it asks for and what it warns against); the charity sector's impact codes — Inspiring Impact's Code of Good Impact Practice, NPC's four pillars, the Charities SORP's "achievements and performance", Social Value UK's principles, the Impact Management Project's five dimensions; what the sector's own reviews say about impact reporting's quality (NPC's state-of-the-sector reports; the Charity Commission's research on public trust in charities — the annual trust tracker — as a trust measure for a sector that reports impact). Read for method; the nightlife sector itself is never researched.
6. *The great public data projects.* Our World in Data (its stated principles, funding, update cadence, usage figures as published, the "next expected update" convention, its data pipeline documentation); the ONS census programme (release practice; the Census 2021 "how life has changed" template is Chapter 14's; here the release calendar, the quality and methodology information convention, and the Public Confidence in Official Statistics series — NatCen for the UK Statistics Authority: what proportion trust ONS statistics, trust the ONS, think the government presents them honestly, by wave); Eurostat and the European Statistics Code of Practice (Chapter 4 may have it — cite); the UK Data Service and the Human Mortality Database (custodianship of long series; what a long-run project does to stay comparable); Wikipedia's "Wikidata" as a provenance model only if a source speaks to it; the Johns Hopkins retirement and OWID's COVID sunset as cases of a project ending well or badly; the Gapminder and Dollar Street records (Chapter 14 has them; cite). What these projects report about their own reach.
7. *The symmetry test.* Version 1's Chapter 12 gallery positions (already tested in Chapter 14; here only the sector claims: public health as the exemplar of provenance furniture; the ONS as the exemplar of comparison without rank; the incumbents as failure case law); Chapter 2's trust line and Chapter 5's composite audit, which this chapter must add to rather than repeat; the brief's "could fold" note; the house's own borrowings already made (the recipe card from the ONS's QMI; the face sheet; the comparison record from the ELS; the service record from the Observatory's drift).

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** UKHSA COVID-19 dashboard "About the data" and metrics documentation, its change log and its GOV.UK blog posts on user research and usage; Dong, Du & Gardner 2020 (*Lancet Infectious Diseases*) and the JHU CRC retirement notice; Hasell et al. 2020 and Mathieu et al. 2021 (*Scientific Data*; *Nature Human Behaviour*); Ivanković et al. 2021 (*JMIR*); Barbazza et al. 2021; OHID Fingertips technical guidance (benchmarking; confidence intervals; indicator metadata); Public Health Scotland's dashboard guidance; Haldane & McMahon 2018 (*AER P&P*); Bholat et al. 2019 (Bank of England Staff Working Paper 750); the Bank's Monetary Policy Report "in brief"; OBR fan-chart methodology; evaluations of fan-chart comprehension (e.g., the Bank's own; academic); ESRB risk dashboard methodology; ECB; FRED/ALFRED documentation and Croushore & Stark 2001 (*Journal of Econometrics*); IMF SDDS; ONS revisions policy; the Statistics Authority's pre-release access statement; ONS ELS user research posts; Nomis "about"; MHCLG English Indices of Deprivation 2019 technical report and FAQ (and 2025 if published); ONS Health Index methodology; Scotland's NPF performance methodology; Welsh Government national indicators; OECD Regional Well-Being methodology; Centre for Cities; What Works Growth evidence standards; the Levelling Up White Paper technical annex; ACE quality metrics / Impact & Insight Toolkit evaluations (Knell & Whitaker 2016; the 2019–2023 evaluation reports; ACE's Illuminate pages); the Audience Agency liquidation notices and successor arrangements; DCMS Participation Survey and Community and Engagement Survey pages; DCMS Culture and Heritage Capital guidance; Inspiring Impact Code; NPC; Charities SORP; Social Value UK Principles; Impact Management Project; Charity Commission trust research (2024/2025/2026 waves); Our World in Data's about, principles, funding and data documentation; NatCen/UKSA Public Confidence in Official Statistics (2021, 2023, 2025 if published); European Statistics Code of Practice; UK Data Service; Human Mortality Database methods protocol.

**What I expect to find.** That the four properties are measured very unevenly: trust is measured for producers (PCOS; the charity trust tracker) and almost never for a display; provenance is where the best projects are strongest and most alike (documentation, change logs, vintages, next-update dates) and where the house's records already follow them; change over time is where finance is far ahead (vintages, revisions, fan charts) and where public health's banding against a comparator with intervals is the most borrowable mechanism; reach is reported as pageviews when it is reported at all and almost never as decisions changed. That public health got provenance furniture and metric documentation right under pressure and got change over time wrong in places (cumulative counts; the look of precision); that finance got uncertainty right and the look of precision wrong; that economic-development displays are the nearest kin to the Vibe Map and their own technical reports warn against the uses they are put to (ranking; "twice as deprived"); that the culture sector's impact practice is a cautionary tale about a profile instrument scaled into a compliance exercise, and that its audience yardstick is now orphaned; that the great public data projects share a short list of habits — a release calendar, a methods document, a change log, a stated next update, open data, a named custodian — that the house can adopt whole; and that the chapter earns its separation from Chapters 2 and 5 only if it delivers mechanisms with their evidence, sector by sector, rather than a reading list.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel, in one message, search rationed to thirty calls each: (A) public health and the COVID-era dashboards — documentation, benchmarking method, retirement, evaluations; (B) finance, risk and the economics of change over time — central-bank communication experiments, fan charts, risk dashboards, vintages and revisions, release standards; (C) economic development and place — ELS, Nomis, the deprivation indices' rules, the Health Index, the NPF method, OECD regional, Centre for Cities, What Works Growth, Levelling Up metrics; (D) the culture and charity sectors' impact practice and the great public data projects — ACE's metrics line and Illuminate, the Audience Agency's successor arrangements, the Participation Survey's status, Culture and Heritage Capital, the impact codes, charity trust tracking, OWID's and the ONS's own practice, PCOS, the long-run custodians. Every agent: method only; never the nightlife sector; no finding about any place; quotation discipline as Chapter 14's brief.

### Researched (6 October 2026)

Four agents returned in one pass; returns assembled at `02-research-notes/ch15-agent-returns.md` (about 28,800 words). Agent A (public health, 15 searches) could not read the two *Lancet Infectious Diseases* papers on the Johns Hopkins dashboard, the retired UK coronavirus dashboard's own pages, the Fingertips FAQ or Public Health Scotland's guidance; it read the UKHSA successor's compliance page, metric documentation and change log, the PHE/UKHSA blogs, the Fingertips RAG, confidence-interval and trend-marker guidance and API, the JHU repository and retirement notice, the two OWID data papers and sunset notes, the WHO disclaimer, Ivanković, Barbazza (preprint), Ismail, Padilla, Crepaz (blog) and OSR's 2025 dashboards guidance. Agent B (finance, 16 searches) corrected the brief's Bholat citation (SWP 750 is 2018, five authors; the 2019 item is the journal version) and found that the fan chart was not scrapped, that its band convention changed from 10 to 30 per cent steps, that the Bernanke review's "little attention from the public" cites no survey, and that a July 2026 Bank staff paper tested fan charts against five formats; it read ALFRED, Croushore & Stark, the ONS revisions policy and revision sizes, the pre-release Order, the IMF standard, van der Bles, Galvão & Mitchell, OSR's 2022 and 2023 statements, Cox, the ESRB, EBA and Fed displays, Eurostat's flags and the Analysis Function's shorthand guidance. Agent C (place, 17 searches) corrected the brief's "twice as deprived" (the FAQ says "not half as deprived" of ranks; the research report "not simply twice as deprived" of scores), found the Health Index unreleased since June 2023 with no notice, Scotland's per-indicator arrow rules redirected away, the levelling-up reports still laid annually under a statute, the OECD's printed refusal to aggregate and ±5 per cent arrow rule, County Health Rankings' move from ranks to groups, the JRC's rank intervals and the GSS's model sentence. Agent D (culture, charity, public data projects, 23 searches) corrected three premises in the brief (the Toolkit is live and Illuminate replaced Audience Finder, not it; no independent evaluation of the Toolkit exists, only Nordicity's 2016 evaluation of the national test; the Culture and Heritage Capital guidance carries no multiplier warning), found no custodian named for the Audience Agency's data, read the Participation Survey's replacement and its dotted-line rule, the codes, the Charity Commission tracker, OWID's metadata rules and 2022 reach figures, PCOS 2021 and 2023, the Human Mortality Database and the COVID Tracking Project's ending.

**Orchestrator spot-checks (five).** PHE RAG guide: the significance rule and the one-in-twenty sentence — held (false control, a quintile rule, absent). OECD user guide: no composite; ±5 per cent; Manhattan distance — held (ranking control absent). NatCen PCOS 2023: the 87 per cent sentence and the 2,364 sample — held (control absent). IoD 2025 FAQ: "not half as deprived"; "time-series"; "not affluence" — held; "twice as deprived" absent, as the agent said. UKHSA compliance page: "not possible … official statistics"; "4pm every Thursday"; "over 100 individual user research sessions" — held.

### Written (6 October 2026)

Draft 1 in three parts (~28,600 words); style check (six wordings fixed before review). Cold reader and verifier ran together.

**The verifier** matched all 799 quoted strings against the returns and found none differing in wording; it found about twenty single-pass, abstract or secondary items without their mark (added), several over-marked items (two-pass items re-marked), and corrections now made: the blue "reporting delay" band belongs to the measles chart (four weeks) and not the cases metric (five days); the Fingertips amber rule is narrower (amber when a leave-one-out trend runs the opposite way); the 2008 Order's wording is a prohibition ("may not grant"); "interesting rather than useful" applies to triangulated data for one subgroup while 63 per cent found the framework useful; the quality-metrics line and Illuminate are separate; the ELS metadata list has eleven items; the PCOS waves are 2,364 and 3,398; the producer–presenter gap runs 54 to 60 points; "metrics do not map to goals", "comparability by exclusion", "did this inform a decision" and "not a time series" were paraphrases in quotation marks (removed); the HMD principles are on its overview page; "Change is assessed since 2015" is Wales's; Karlan & Wood was abstract only; the OWID next-expected-update derivation is the agent's inference (marked); OSR's guidance lists the plan among the minimum rather than requiring it before launch; the CES's two totals do reconcile (removed); Ter Meer capitalised. At source: Fingertips trend markers, the UKHSA cases page, SWP 750, Ivanković, OSR's guidance (sub-page 2) and the Audience Agency notice all confirmed, with fifteen false controls rejected; OSR's "prominent note" sentence sits on an unfetched sub-page and is marked read once.

**The cold reader** found the stand-alone claim failing in the back third and the tiers overreaching the chapter's own "decides nothing". Accepted: a glossary of every earlier-chapter dependency, the eleven records with a line each, the failure register named with its author, and the abbreviations expanded; "look / habit / bet" given one definition and used in it; the 60-second version re-based (the producer–presenter pair named; the record's nine lines; the truths and the offer distinguished); a criterion for "the best"; the two-dates passage, the compliance-page paradox and the fan chart's backward extension explained; the measles window separated from the cases window; the Scottish evaluation named and its twenty respondents counted; County Health Rankings and the Hodge review named in the body; the kinds added to each truth; colour decisions (amber, blue, "flat amber") returned to Fingertips and the house's rule stated as a neutral default and ramp; the arrow rule given one strength everywhere; vintages and the retirement plan made consistent between truths, rules and section 13; what the display shows in its first years under these rules stated; the reach rule reconciled with the two evaluations that asked; the sources' self-published figures labelled as such; "Every public data project ends" bounded to the five read; the Arndt sequence un-causalised; "the one place in any sector" and "every regulator" withdrawn; several "not X; it is Y" constructions and three self-congratulatory sentences reworded; two checklist items added. Not accepted: cutting section 2, section 11 or Looking back (the template's); folding the conflict log into the body; and the 60-second version at a minute — held at the earlier chapters' length and logged again under the question Carly has not answered.

Draft 2: about 31,000 words; 106 "read once" marks. Ledger §19 (98 rows) compiled by a smaller model and checked at the Nordicity, Audience Agency, PCOS and Fingertips rows.

### What was found that was not asked for

The fan chart survived its own abolition and was then found the best-understood of six formats in the Bank's own test; its band rule changed from 10 to 30 per cent steps under the same picture. Fingertips prints that one red or green in twenty is chance, and will not draw an arrow without five independent annual points, a 99.8 per cent test and a leave-one-out check. The UKHSA dashboard says on a public page what it is not. Johns Hopkins kept two ledgers, one for its own errors and one for its sources' revisions. The deprivation indices never say "twice as deprived". The Health Index, built for change over time, has shown "Next release: To be announced" since June 2023. Scotland's arrow thresholds were per indicator and have been redirected away. County Health Rankings dropped ranks for ten unequal groups in 2024. The OECD's regional tool prints its refusal to aggregate. Trust in the ONS (87 per cent) and agreement that government presents its figures honestly (32) have sat 54 to 60 points apart across four waves. Verbal hedges did little; ranges cost little or no trust. OWID's survey said 17.5 per cent download data; its analytics said 1.6 per cent of sessions do. The Audience Agency's notice names no successor. Illuminate's survey requirement was suspended from 2023 to 2025. The levelling-up reports are still laid annually by a government that has left the programme. The COVID Tracking Project's ending promised gap analyses. The Analysis Function advises against the dagger this book uses.

### Looking back (6 October 2026)

Recorded in the chapter's own section. Strengthens 4, 5, 7, 8, 12, 13 and 14; strengthens and bounds 2; counterbalances 6 (the yardstick orphaned; no multiplier warning) and 11 (fan charts; the GSS's sentence); opens a thread for 9. Version 1's sector claims hold and are specified. The fold question is answered with a count and left to Carly.

### Rich references

Fingertips' technical guidance with the PHE RAG guide — exact printed rules for colour, comparison and trend arrows, with their own warnings. The UKHSA data dashboard's "What's new" and "Compliance" pages — a live change log with issue/resolved pairs and a public statement of what the dashboard is not. OSR's *Regulatory guidance: Dashboards* (August 2025) — the minimum list and the archive-notice rule. The English Indices of Deprivation 2025 research report, chapter 3, with FAQ Q22–Q25 — what a rank, decile and score may be used to say. The OECD Regional Well-Being user guide — the refusal to aggregate, the ±5 per cent arrow, the Manhattan-distance peers. McMahon, Naylor, Rholes & Rickards (Bank of England SWP 1,196, July 2026) and Galvão & Mitchell (ESCoE 2021) — formats tested against trust. Nordicity 2016 and Selwood 2019 — the culture sector's own evidence on its instrument.

### Delivered (6 October 2026)

## Entry 16 · Chapter 16 — Breaking these rules well

*Sprint opened 6 October 2026, after Chapter 15, in the same session at Carly's instruction ("lets go with c16"). The closing chapter of Part 4 and of the book's teaching chapters: the assembly. The full method applies where the chapter researches; most of its work is reading the fifteen chapters before it and the house's own documents, and that reading is done by an agent and checked like any other.*

### Framed (written before research began)

**The chapter's question.** When a rule in this book is to be broken, how is it done well — which rules have a right answer and which a defensible range; what the kinds of deviation are and what separates an earned one from an expedient one; what record is kept (the override log, and now the boundary log and the precedent record beside it — are three logs one); what test is run first (the removal tests of every chapter, assembled); what the book could not find (the gap register, assembled); and what the build inherits as its rule register — every rule in Chapters 1 to 15 with its tier, its kind and its source, checked for the three senses of "Fixed" that Chapter 0 named. The brief's words: "Tiers, deviation, the override log, the assembled removal tests, the gap register, the rule register for the build." Version 1's Chapter 14 transferred the Decision Book's machinery whole and added the assembled tests, a seeded register of "circa 150 Fixed-tier rules", a gap summary and nine original-contribution candidates; this chapter re-derives none of the machinery but tests it, and assembles from version 2's chapters rather than version 1's.

**Sub-questions.**
1. *The tier test, tested.* The Decision Book's Fixed/Adaptable/Decorative and its grounds (the Stanford *Navigating Ambiguity* sentence on uncertainty against ambiguity; *Drawing on Courage*'s "fear enlists its good friends reason and comfort"; *This Is a Prototype*'s "close the gap between I wonder and I know") — verified at source, since the house's documents borrowed them; Chapter 0's three senses of Fixed (a rule to obey; a statement of the record; a sequence); Chapter 14's finding that Fixed is, for a chart's encoding, Mackinlay's expressiveness, and the W3C's fourth category ("specific sensory experience") that the tiers lack; Chapter 15's three-sentence evidence standard.
2. *Deviation regimes elsewhere.* How other rule systems let a rule be broken on the record: "comply or explain" in corporate governance (the UK Corporate Governance Code and the FRC's own guidance on what an explanation must contain; the measured quality of explanations); the accessibility regulations' "disproportionate burden" statements and WCAG's partial-conformance and conforming-alternate-version provisions (Chapter 13 has the texts; here the regime); the Government Service Standard's assessment outcomes and exemptions; the statistics Code's voluntary application and exemptions; a regulator's "waivers and modifications"; the quality-management standard's nonconformity and corrective action. For each: who decides, what the record must say, whether anyone checks it, and what is known about whether explanations are any good.
3. *Records of decisions, elsewhere.* Architecture decision records (Nygard 2011 and what has been written about their use); registered reports and the literature on deviations from preregistration (how often they occur, how they are reported, what a transparent deviation statement contains); the change-log convention; the consolidated documentation forms in machine learning (datasheets for datasets; model cards) as precedents for "are the twelve records one document"; the Decision Book's own override log, gap register and rule register, read as documents.
4. *Overrides in practice, measured.* What happens to a rule that fires too often: clinical decision support's alert override rates and alert fatigue (the review literature); checklists and whether they work (the surgical checklist trials and their critics); evidence on whether written justifications improve decisions or become boilerplate (the comply-or-explain studies again; "tick-box" findings). The question is whether an override log stays a decision record or becomes a reflex.
5. *The book's own machinery, assembled.* From the fifteen version 2 chapters (and Chapter 0): every rule in every rules table, with its tier, its kind and its source, de-duplicated and checked for which sense of Fixed it carries; every "What proves a break works" test; every record beneath the dashboard (twelve); every `[Revisit → Ch 16]` and `[Revisit at end]` flag; every gaps table; every design-system and Decision Book conflict log entry still open (ARG-12; colour.md's contrast line; Decision Book Chapter 7's "alongside"; the status-chip component; the dagger against the Analysis Function's square-bracket standard; the retirement plan as a Fixed rule; the tier vocabulary and the sensory experience). Compiled by an agent reading the chapter files, and checked.
6. *The build's register and the open calls.* What the register is for (the Decision Book's "a rule that can be measured is a rule that is run"); the three build-time checks version 1 specified (crop; recipe; state-honesty) tested against version 2's rules; what remains Carly's (the trim; the fold; the adviser; the unit; the twelve-as-one question); and the symmetry test against version 1's Chapter 14 (its seeded 150; its nine candidates; its deviation taxonomy).

**Expected sources (to be read, not assumed).** Stanford d.school, *Navigating Ambiguity* (Levy & Morrison?) and *Drawing on Courage* (Hong) and *This Is a Prototype* (Utley) — at source for the three borrowed sentences; the UK Corporate Governance Code 2024 and the FRC's "What constitutes an explanation under 'comply or explain'" (2012) and later FRC reviews of explanation quality; academic studies of comply-or-explain explanations (Seidl, Sanderson & Roberts 2013; Arcot et al.); the Public Sector Bodies Accessibility Regulations' disproportionate-burden provisions and GDS's guidance on claiming it (reported, as Chapter 13 did); WCAG 2.2 conformance (partial conformance; alternate versions — Chapter 13 has the text); the Service Standard and its assessment outcomes; the Code of Practice for Statistics' voluntary application; the FCA's waivers and modifications page; ISO 9001 clause 10.2 (nonconformity and corrective action), as quoted at source or in an accessible secondary; Nygard, "Documenting Architecture Decisions" (2011); the ADR GitHub organisation and any survey of ADR use; Claesen et al. 2021 (preregistration deviations in psychology), Willroth & Atherton 2024 (reporting deviations), the Center for Open Science guidance on transparent changes; Keep a Changelog; Gebru et al. 2021 (*Communications of the ACM*) datasheets; Mitchell et al. 2019 model cards; van der Sijs et al. 2006 and Ancker et al. 2017 or a recent review on alert override rates; Haynes et al. 2009 (NEJM) surgical checklist and Urbach et al. 2014 (NEJM Ontario) as its counter; the Decision Book Chapters 7 and 18, gap register, rule register, checklists; Style Guide v1.5 §06; the fifteen chapters.

**What I expect to find.** That the tier test's borrowed sentences are real but their books are not online, so the chapter will quote what it can verify and mark the rest; that comply-or-explain is the best-studied deviation regime and its finding is uncomfortable — explanations are often boilerplate, the quality depends on who reads them, and a regime with no reader is a regime with no explanations; that the preregistration literature finds deviations common and under-reported, which argues for a log that is checked rather than kept; that alert fatigue shows what happens when too many Fixed rules fire — overrides become reflex — which is an argument for a short Fixed register and a long Adaptable one; that the surgical checklist works where it is used and not where it is merely present; that architecture decision records are the nearest cousin of the override log and are kept as one file per decision in the repository; that datasheets and model cards are the precedent for one document with sections rather than twelve documents; and that the assembly will find far fewer than 150 Fixed rules once the three senses of Fixed are separated, and several rules stated twice in different chapters' words, which the register must reconcile.

**Dispatch.** Four research agents in parallel, in one message, search rationed to thirty calls each: (A) deviation regimes elsewhere — comply-or-explain and its measured quality; disproportionate burden; WCAG partial conformance; the Service Standard; the Code's voluntary application; waivers; nonconformity; (B) records of decisions — ADRs; preregistration deviations; changelogs; datasheets and model cards; the one-document question; (C) the book's own machinery assembled from the fifteen chapter files and the house documents — no web; every rule, test, record, flag, gap and open conflict, compiled and counted; (D) overrides in practice — alert fatigue; checklists; boilerplate; and the three borrowed sentences verified at source. Every agent: method only; never the nightlife sector; quotation discipline as before.

### Researched (6 October 2026)

Four agents returned in one pass; returns assembled at `02-research-notes/ch16-agent-returns.md` (about 25,200 words) and the compilation at `02-research-notes/ch16-assembly.md` (about 600 KB, 3,746 lines, eight parts). Agent A (deviation regimes, 18 searches) could not read Doran et al. 2008 at NEJM or PubMed, the ISO browsing platform, the FRC's 2024 annual review, the Columbia report at NASA, Seidl et al.'s published text, or WCAG 2.2's conformance section on the TR page; it read the 2024 Code, the FRC's 2012 paper and 2021 review and 2025 annual review, SI 2018/952, the GDS guidance and 2022–24 monitoring report, WCAG's conformance text in the editors' source, the Service Manual, the OSR TQV guidance and exemptions page and Code 3.0, FSMA ss.138A–B and the FCA's pages and SUP 8.3, the ISO 9001 status pages and the ISO 9000 definitions via a railway agency's terminology collection, the QOF 2024–25 statistics and Doran 2012, Roland 2016 and Kontopantelis 2016, the Treasury's ministerial-directions page, DMRB GG 101 and MISRA Compliance 2020, and the Columbia figure through a secondary excerpt. Three corrections to its brief: the 2024 Code's own sentence has no mitigating-actions element (that is the FRC's 2012 and 2021 papers); OSR's scheme is now "TQV Voluntary Application"; s.138A has two conditions, not four. Agent B (records of decisions, 13 searches) read Nygard, adr.github.io, AWS, Thoughtworks, three ADR studies (two abstracts), Claesen, Willroth & Atherton, Field, Lakens, the COS and OSF pages, Keep a Changelog, SemVer, Gebru, Mitchell, Liang, the GDS Way and the DSIT/GDS framework, the Green Book 2026, the NAO procurement investigation, Eurostat's SIMS manual and metadata page, the ONS QMI page, the Analysis Function's quality page, the UKDS and Cornell pages, Lethbridge, Harrison, Aghajani, the IfG turnover report, the Allan review and Klein; three corrections to its brief: Nygard never says "immutable" and argues against the large document; no experiment on disclosed deviations and trust exists. Agent C (the compilation, no web) copied by script and re-checked against source lines 486 rule rows, 129 record lines, 19 test paragraphs, 66 flag lines, 181 gap rows and 35 conflict paragraphs; the 48 gap and conflict rows of Chapters 14–15 came through the script without the re-check. It reported eight places the files did not match the brief, chief among them that Chapter 0 has no rules table and that the deviation taxonomy is never set out. Agent D (overrides, checklists, written reasons, the three borrowed sentences; 19 searches) could not read PMC, NEJM, the Lerner & Tetlock body or van der Sijs's body; it read Ancker, Poly, Felisberto, PSNet, the Haynes and Urbach abstracts, Leape through a blog, Russ, Bergs and De Jager, the Tetlock 1989 abstract, Hoffmann, Chang, the two NAO reports, the 2013 planning memorandum, Wright, Haggerty, the three educators' guides and publisher pages, RFC 2119 and 8174, the ISO Directives, the WCAG source, the Code 3.0 practices, the AQuA Book, Hale & Borys, Degani & Wiener, Paterno, Shah, Powers and the Australian sunsetting note; three things to know: the "unknown views" condition is the 1989 paper's and not in the 1999 abstract; Leape is verified only on a third-party page; the house's date for *Drawing on Courage* is wrong and the Prototype sentence is a question.

**Orchestrator spot-checks (five).** FRC 2021: "74 cases … only four explanations" — held (control absent). Claesen 2021 abstract: "Two out of 27"; "One study (4%)"; "24 of 27 (89%)" — held. Nygard 2011: "Large documents are never kept up to date."; superseded; no "immutable" — held. Ancker 2017: "dropped by 30% … 10%" — held. Tetlock, Skitka & Boettger 1989: the three predictions — held. One note from the FRC page: its boilerplate sentence continues "and information about governance outcomes", so the chapter prints it whole. Internal check of the rule-row counts and the six `[Revisit → Ch 16]` lines against the assembly — held.

### Written (6 October 2026)

Draft 1 in three parts (~33,300 words); style check (nine wordings fixed before review — "honestly" outside quotations; five "not X; it is Y" constructions). Cold reader and verifier ran together.

**The verifier** re-traced 42 items at source with WebFetch (three searches used) and found nine corrections, now made: two FRC 2021 sentences had been closed before their ends ("…and offered an insight into the companies' approach to good governance"; "…and information about governance outcomes"); the SIMS manual says "creating" where the draft opened a quotation at "create"; "most statements" had been written where the monitoring body says "many"; the 2013 planning memorandum reports the local government planning community's suggestion, not a finding; "55 flag lines" in the sources note was the test count (66 flag lines); the chapter with the fewest gap rows is Chapter 0, not 12 or 13; the tally under the eleven-rule table was 9/1/1 not 8/2/1; and the per-question breakdown of the 44 `[Revisit at end]` uses did not sum and is now given as proportions. It confirmed the Tetlock, Skitka & Boettger authorship through Crossref, noted the OSR exemption sentence is shown as Code text of an unstated edition (now said), and could not reach WCAG 2.2's conformance section on the dated TR page (the editors' source confirmed it; the caveat stands). All other quoted strings and counts checked held, including every count against the compilation.

**The cold reader** found the chapter repeating the fault it diagnoses (its own rules table 85 per cent Fixed; truths resting on daggers without the flag), the inference from alert fatigue to a register asserted rather than argued, and the remedy of two hundred build-failing checks sitting uneasily beside the finding that hard stops are routed around. Accepted: the 60-second version re-pointed to sections 7 and 8 and brought into line on the entry's nine lines, the cost, the record's ten sections (the two logs having gone to the deviation log) and the "short enough" claim as an estimate the build tests; the regimes' four recurring features stated the same way in the 60-second version and section 4; the gate clarified (with an entry present, the check the departure breaks is marked as a logged exception and still runs elsewhere); truth 1's "named approver" placed at the right tier; truths 2, 3 and 7 flagged `[Further research]` for their daggers; Powers 2018 given its dagger; the rules-table row on the three senses reworded so a statement of the record is not called a rule; the nine lines listed identically in section 5, truth 4 and section 11; the test forms counted as six once the scan is admitted; the example entry's first line made fillable; the Fixed/Adaptable estimate made to sum (about two hundred and about two hundred and fifty, before the forty-two groups collapse) and the sentence about the Decision Book's share corrected; "the house" no longer used of government guidance; "the industry entry" attributed; a glossary of earlier-chapter dependencies and the abbreviations added to the working words; "nine in ten firings" bounded to interaction alerts; eleven self-congratulatory or padded clauses cut; DD-002's chapters and kind corrected. Declined, with the reason: the inference from alert fatigue to a register is kept as the chapter's reading, which section 3 names as the third of three possible readings rather than a demonstration; the number of Fixed rows in the chapter's own table stands because most are requirements the register and build can check, and truth 2 remains the test of them; the verdicts in section 10 are the symmetry test's form, used in every chapter; the repetition of the headline figures across the 60-second version, the sections and the truths is the template's, which Carly has not yet asked to trim.

Draft 2: about 33,800 words; 42 "read once" marks; 69 daggers. Ledger §20 (118 rows) compiled by a smaller model and checked at the FRC, GDS, Nygard and Tetlock rows.

### What was found that was not asked for

The Decision Book's four-class deviation taxonomy was never written; it is one checklist line, and the two books that drew the same lines — a highways design manual and a C coding standard — list the reasons a deviation may never be granted, developer convenience first. The 2024 Code's own sentence is weaker than the regulator's guidance around it. ISO 9001:2015 was withdrawn three weeks before the chapter was written. Patients excluded from a quality target by a legitimate override had a higher adjusted risk of death the next year, whichever side excluded them. WCAG's partial-conformance statement is drafted so that its first words are "This page does not conform". Nygard never says "immutable" and argues against the one large document; the government's own ADR framework was published in November 2025; the Green Book was rewritten in February 2026 and now says "must" of recording the reason for each rating. The limits section of a model card is filled in about one time in six. Editors rated a disclosed deviation neutral to slightly positive and a concealed one strongly negative. A reason-giving duty was repealed in England in 2013 because the reasons were being written twice. Requiring a justification changed neither accuracy nor strategy in two experiments and lowered confidence; mandatory reason fields fill with "a space or random characters". The book's own tables are 89 per cent Fixed against the Decision Book's 74, use fifty-three tier wordings and 182 kinds, tier eleven rules differently in different chapters, and name their twelve records seven ways; and the word "dashboard" appears 1,271 times across Chapters 1–15 and survived.

### Looking back (6 October 2026)

Recorded in the chapter's own section, chapter by chapter, Chapters 0 to 15. The register and Chapter 0's revision carry the changes; no earlier chapter is rewritten. Eleven tier conflicts and five pulling rules reconciled as readings; four of five direct conflicts with the house documents resolved as readings and referred, ARG-12 carried as disputed; the twelve records answered as one record of ten sections and the three logs as one log with three types; Chapter 0's eleven drifted conventions and two adoptions listed for transcription.

### Rich references

FRC, "Improving the quality of 'comply or explain' reporting" (2021) — a regulator scoring its own regime's explanations: four of seventy-four. National Highways, DMRB GG 101 — a design manual whose verbs fix in advance which rules can be broken, by whom, with what record. Doran et al. 2012 — a legitimate override measured across 8,229 practices, with reasons, cost and the signs of misuse. Willroth & Atherton 2024 — the deviation table, the timing field, and the only data on how gatekeepers treat disclosed and concealed deviations. Gebru et al. 2021 with Liang et al. 2024 — the one-document design with maintenance built in, and which sections people leave empty. Nygard 2011 with Santana Junior et al. 2026 — the case for many small records, and 5,800 of them fifteen years on. Hale & Borys 2013 — the two models of a rule. Tetlock, Skitka & Boettger 1989 with Lerner & Tetlock 1999 — when having to give reasons helps. Russ et al. 2015 — a checklist's use measured by watching.

### Closed (7 October 2026)

**Chapter 0 revised** as a transcription of Chapter 16 §7, with no new judgement: the template table (the question; "What people believe about …"; the symmetry test; the later section names; the gap columns `Gap | Where it bites | Interim position | Who closes it` with the cost as a word; the conflict log as a status table); the kinds (the sub-kinds of Standard reduced to eight; Evidence of method; adjacent domain; binds/adopted as a register field); the tiers (Fixed in three senses with their tests; Adaptable (house default); the sensory-experience note under Decorative; "as a stance" and "as a procedure" explained as the chapters' wordings and re-based by the register); the marks (the dagger's widest definition; reconstructed; read once; two and three passes; the presence check; the reader's bracketed words; the dagger status in the register); `[Counterbalance → X]` widened; the symmetry test defined; the glossary (the deviation log's nine lines and three types; the one record; the rule register; a canonical truth's two tests; on the face / one step away / on request; the crop and removal tests updated; the five-word rule's tier; identity and conditions settled); its own gap table in the new columns with a fifth row. The previous text is at `archive/ch00-how-to-read-this-book-2026-10-06.md`. Chapter 0: ~6,100 words.

**The reader rebuilt** — `05-book/reader.html` from `05-book/build_reader.py` with Chapters 0–16 (3.7 MB; about 460,000 words; 17 chapters; 2,028 bracketed marks). The script now renders the manuscript's marks as bracketed words — "†" as [abstract or secondary], "(read once)", "(two passes)", "(three passes)" — except in Chapter 0, which explains them, and except where a dagger is itself quoted. Checked in Chromium at 1,280 and 390 pixels: no horizontal scroll, no script errors, search working, sections folding. The chapters' rules tables stand as printed and the Claude note says so.

A closing note beyond Chapter 16's: not asked for. Everything awaiting Carly is indexed in Chapter 16 §12 and listed in `STATE.md`.

## Entry 17 · After the chapters: the register, the library pass, the probes

*Opened 7 October 2026, at Carly's instruction ("can we please do this?") on the three pieces of work Chapter 16 pointed at. Not a chapter: no new research question, and the teaching template does not apply. The method that does apply: framed here before the work starts; the work done by script where it is counting and by agents where it is judgement, with the judgement rules quoted from the chapters and every row traceable to its source line; a verification pass on a sample; delivered as files, not committed.*

### Framed (written before the work began)

**What is to be made.** (1) `06-register/rule-register.csv` and `.md`, generated from `02-research-notes/ch16-assembly.md` Part 1 (486 rows) plus Chapter 16's own 27, by the fields of Chapter 16 §8: one register rule per duplicate group with the group's rows as its sources; the tier re-based into the five wordings by §3's rules (requirement; sequence; Adaptable (house default); Adaptable; Decorative) with the open part beside it; the §7 reconciliations applied to the eleven conflicts and five pulling rules; kind, sub-kind of Standard, binds or adopted; chapters and rows; test form and check; record section; status (current; superseded; disputed; awaiting Carly; full text not read); *cited by* left for the build. `06-register/deviation-log.md`: the nine-line spine stated once, the three types with their extra lines quoted from Chapters 14 and 15, and the first real entry — Chapter 12's in-frame furniture against official practice — with its test result as *pending the first build* rather than the invented figure Chapter 16 used for method. A build script, so the files regenerate from the compilation. (2) `01-sources/LIBRARY-PASS.md`: the 32 unreachable sources in the gap tables and the sources behind the 49 Fixed rows resting on a dagger, de-duplicated; for each, an attempt to find an open copy (repository, preprint, author page, PMC), and where one is found the load-bearing claim re-read against it; where none, the citation a library request needs. No chapter is rewritten; a claim found wrong is logged here and flagged for Carly, in the chapters' own convention. (3) `03-tests/PROBES.md`: every `[Validate with users]` and `[Prototype flag]` line in Chapters 1–16 (about 190 lines), numbered, with the chapter and rule it serves, what it would settle, the form of the test (reader test, count, comparison, walk), who it needs (which of the three user groups, the scenes, the build's own data) and which of the Vibe Map's four tests it serves; grouped and de-duplicated; prioritisation left to Carly.

**What I expect to find.** That the register's Fixed count lands near Chapter 16's estimate of two hundred and that most of the hard calls are in the 212 unmarked Fixed rows of Chapters 1–4 and 7, which say "Fixed" and mean several things; that a third or so of the unreachable sources have an open copy the fetch tool did not find (PMC author manuscripts; institutional repositories; preprints), and that most of the paywalled abstracts hold when read in full, with a few numbers that move; and that the probe candidates collapse to perhaps sixty distinct probes, many of them the same five-second or recognition test asked of a different element.

**Dispatch.** Scripts parse the compilation; three agents classify the rows in three slices with the rules quoted; one agent chooses the canonical wording per duplicate group; the orchestrator spot-checks the eleven conflict rows and twenty others. Two agents run the library pass, search rationed. One agent writes the probe sketches from the extracted lines. A verifier samples the register (thirty rows) and the library pass (ten claims).

### Done (7 October 2026)

**The register.** `06-register/build_register.py` parses the compilation's Part 1 (486 rows, with chapter, row and file line) and Chapter 16's own table (27 rows) into `work/rows.csv`, and the 42 duplicate groups into `work/groups.csv`. Three agents classified the 513 rows in three slices (Chapters 1–5; 6–10; 11–16) by the rules quoted in `work/BRIEF.md` — Chapter 0's revised tier table and Chapter 16 §3, §7 and §8 — returning for each row the re-based tier, the open part, a reason of twelve words or fewer, the test form and check, the record section, the kind fields, the binds/adopted letter and the status (`work/classified-1..3.csv`). A fourth agent chose, for each duplicate group, the member row whose wording is the strictest the others are consistent with, and the group's tier (`work/groups-decided.csv`); it split nine groups and reported thirteen with no single covering wording. The orchestrator then split six more groups by Chapter 16 §7's two- and three-rule decisions (`work/group-splits.csv`: the headline's length and the letter-height requirement out of the title group; text inside the frame out of the provenance group; the interval-and-wording rule out of the ranking group; reach by asking and trust out of the measuring-use group). The verifier sampled thirty rules and every grouped rule and found fourteen faults, all now fixed in the data or the build: the record-carrying group dissolved into one rule per record, with the claim and comparison records as sequences; four rules printing a non-member's wording (the build now always prints the canonical row's own); a Level 1 reader rule and a conformance rule split out of the small-screen group; dagger status computed from every member row (54 rules now carry *full text not read*); two identical Decorative wordings merged; the ranking requirement re-based on 1.11's wording rather than 2.13's, which licensed a league table with intervals; the one-step rule on 11.18's; "resilience named before measured" as a sequence; the book-only rule applied the same way to 13.31, 13.32 and 1.17 (requirements, since the build can check them); the one statement of the record listed apart under its own identifier; open parts without a tier given one; the references to Chapter 16 §8's illustrative identifiers re-pointed; ARG-12's status completed. Result: **377 register rules** from 513 rows (one statement of the record apart): Fixed (requirement) 245; Fixed (sequence) 35; Adaptable (house default) 56; Adaptable 34; Decorative 7 — Fixed 74 per cent. Chapter 16 §3 estimated about two hundred Fixed before the file existed; the estimate is not confirmed, and the register says so in its header. The reason is in the classifiers' reasons: most of the 212 unmarked Fixed rows and the 102 procedures are presences, thresholds, lines of a record or prohibitions the build can scan or recompute, and the re-basing moved the stances (51 of 90 to house default) more than it moved the rest. The register's share is the Decision Book's own. Test forms: record check 137, scan 105, reader test 77, recomputation 19, adversarial walk 15, removal 13, as first built. Every one of the 513 source rows appears in exactly one rule; no two rules share a wording; every wording is a chapter's. The third ranking rule Chapter 16 §7 names (whether position is shown at all) has no source row of its own and is carried as the prohibition's open part.

**The deviation log.** `06-register/deviation-log.md`: the nine-line spine stated once; the expressive type's eleven lines and the borrowed type's nine quoted from Chapters 14 and 15; the first entry, DL-001, Chapter 12's in-frame furniture against official practice, status *proposed*, with the test result *pending the first build* and the checker and owner to be named. Chapter 16 §11's invented result is not in it. The two re-tierings Chapter 14 carried to the register are noted as changes of tier, not deviations.

**The library pass.** Ninety-six distinct sources attempted; 34 read in full or in the part that matters; 21 open but behind a wall the fetch tool cannot pass; 41 with no open copy, each with a citation for a request. Twenty-three readings change something, nine of substance; the one-page summary at the head of `01-sources/LIBRARY-PASS.md` lists ten corrections proposed for Carly, led by the RSS working party of 2005 (it permits league tables with uncertainty shown and "considerable caution", and does not support rule 7.2's prohibition), Kaliisa et al. 2024 (nine of 38 studies, with the larger effects confounded), Sjoberg et al. 2017 (reports marked fixed by another user, 11.6 against 18.2 per cent, "cannot estimate causal effects per se"), Zhi et al. 2019 ("We did not find any evidence for linking improving the recall task performance"), Paterno 2009's hard stop, Tintarev & Masthoff's "may improve", Birt et al.'s "proved unnecessary", the EHRC Code's Chapter 7 read at source, and Stevens 1946 in his own words. Five daggers can be removed without new reading because the ledger already records a full-text read. The verifier re-traced twelve changed readings and found the load-bearing strings present, with five details corrected (paragraph numbers in the EHRC Code; Stevens at p. 679; two Tintarev quotations mid-sentence; Sjoberg's unique-users sentence; the two agents' weights on Birt reconciled). No chapter was changed.

**The probes.** 189 flag lines extracted by script (`work/probe-lines.md`); `03-tests/PROBES.md` makes them 75 probes — 162 lines placed, 27 left out with the reason (the sixteen legend lines; four platform-terms decisions; three summary rows; the standard for running probes; two indexes). Eleven name the scenes; sixteen the three user groups; twenty-eight unnamed readers; nineteen the build alone. The probe most chapters depend on is the recognition check, asked for by six. Cost: 27 small, 36 a session, 12 a build. Prioritisation is Carly's and the file says so.

**Left for Carly.** The ten corrections; whether the five stale daggers come off; the register's identifier scheme (provisional `DD-nnn`, `DS-nnn`); whether `06-register/` lives in this repository or moves to the prototype's (the files regenerate from the compilation wherever they sit); the checker and owner for DL-001; and which probes run. `AGENTS.md`'s "Where things go" table gains a row for `06-register/`.

### Delivered (7 October 2026)

`06-register/` (build script; `rule-register.csv` and `.md`; `deviation-log.md`; `work/` with the inputs, the classification files and the two library reports); `01-sources/LIBRARY-PASS.md`; `03-tests/PROBES.md`; this entry; `00-plan/STATE.md`; `AGENTS.md` (one row). Carly reviews and commits.
